Extends ADR 0008 rather than needing its own: both entries are that ADR's two rules holding under pressure. | D16 predicted email authentication as the obvious next entry and it was; supply chain is the one the OWASP editors themselves added because that is where breaches are coming from, which is the argument for the capability. |
| D18 | **`net.probe` splits on the read/write line: `net.probe` connects, handshakes and listens (Read); `net.send` writes caller-chosen bytes (Write + `NeedsGrant` + `Scope: host`).** Split rather than reclassified, because the capability really is read-only without `--send` — and taken now, before M2, because it removes a published schema property. ADR 0009. | `--send` shipped as Read, which put a remote write primitive — strictly more capable than the `http.post` this same catalogue classifies Write — on every MCP server with no flag, no grant, and a `readOnlyHint` the client was entitled to believe. |
| D19 | **How wide a tile needs to be (`MinWidth`) and whether a host may run one unasked (`NoPreview`) are capability declarations, not host-side maps of capability IDs.** Both state something the host cannot work out and the capability knows for certain, and both are declarative rather than instructions — which is what keeps them on the right side of D1. ADR 0010. | They lived inside the TUI, so a third party's only route to correct behaviour was a patch to `rta` itself: "my tile is unreadable at half width" and "my tile must not run on the refresh tick" were host bugs filed against a host that had never seen the plugin. Tolerable while every plugin is built-in, intolerable one milestone later. |
| D20 | **`Field.Type` is a closed set *and* the zero value is not a member, decided before the freeze rather than after.** Closing the set stops `Type: "integer"` from silently becoming a string flag; requiring it is a deliberate second decision, because `Secret` is what makes a value masked and `Path` is what makes it completable, and neither is inferable from a name. ADR 0011. | Both must be errors while they still can be: after `pkg/plugin` is semver-committed, turning a silent default into an error breaks every plugin that had come to depend on it. Found as a side effect of the first change, then made deliberate — an untyped input is a security question, not only a tidiness one. |

| D21 | **Plugin confinement is macOS-only at M2, and plugin process lifetime is keyed by the confinement spec rather than by the surface.** A sandbox is self-applied on both platforms that have one (`sandbox_init`, `landlock_restrict_self`; there is no `landlock_restrict_pid`), so it is immutable for the life of its process and a process must not outlive the fit of its spec. Linux ships no filesystem confinement: `go install` defaults to `CGO_ENABLED=1`, go-landlock's pre-ABI-8 path returns `ENOTSUP` in cgo binaries, and `BestEffort()` downgrades rulesets rather than syscall errors — fail-closed, that is *no plugin ever spawns*. Supersedes the lifetime half of D3. ADR 0012. | D3 stated lifetime in terms of the surface, which is a proxy for "how many future calls" when what matters is "does the spec still fit". And the denylist inversion the design rested on turned out to be a macOS property, not a design property. |
| D22 | **Declared text is rejected at registration; displayed data is cleaned at render.** Opposite treatments, because a declaration has an author to tell and runtime data does not. Plugin prose is framed inside published tool descriptions with rta's own sentences *after* it, and `ToolAnnotations.Title` is host-derived, since Title sits outside the description where no frame reaches it. `-o json` stays byte-exact and is safe *against a terminal*; the MCP consumer is a model and reads the decoded string, so it gets its own cleaner. ADR 0013. | A server's response body wrote to the reader's system clipboard via OSC 52 through `rta http get` — one URL, no plugin, no grant. And rta's own "You cannot issue one yourself" came *after* plugin prose in the same string, in the same voice. |
| D23 | **Caller-supplied paths are confined to roots at the MCP boundary, hooked on `Field.Type == Path`.** An allowlist rooted at the server's working directory, not a denylist of credential locations: a denylist still misses the `.env` beside somebody's `docker-compose.yml`. The type is the hook because guessing from the value is wrong — base64 contains `/` and a JPEG starts `/9j/`. ADR 0014. | The default MCP surface read arbitrary files: `fs_hash` returned the sha256 and size of any path, `fs_tree` listed any directory, `cert_inspect` was an existence oracle. All `read`, all correct, all doing their job — nothing asked whether *this* caller may point them there. |
| D24 | **The grant file is sealed with a keyed MAC, and `MaxTTL` is enforced where a grant is read rather than where it is issued.** Tamper-evident, not tamper-proof: it stops a writer that cannot read the directory — exactly the shape a sandbox creates — and stops nothing that can. ADR 0015. | `loadAll` was `ReadFile` plus `Unmarshal`, so anything that could write the data directory authored the answer to "what may this agent do"; 82 bytes turned a refused `kv_get` into the secret. And a forged `expires: 2099` was honoured for seventy years by a cap enforced only in the CLI. |
| D25 | **SDK plugins are `rta-plugin-<name>` on `$PATH`, discovered rather than installed.** The exec tier already owns `rta-<name>` (P8's escape hatch), so the longer prefix keeps the two decidable in one direction and stops one binary being reported under two tiers with two different sets of guarantees. `$PATH` rather than a directory rta owns, for the reason krew, `gh`, `kubectl` and `git` all land there: it is what a package manager already installs into and what `which` already answers questions about. First match wins, in `$PATH` order, the way a shell resolves it. A plugin that fails to load is reported and skipped, never fatal. | An install step nobody else's tooling understands is how a plugin ecosystem fails to start. And a tool where any third-party binary on `$PATH` can stop the binary from starting is a tool people stop installing plugins for. |
| D26 | **A plugin's declaration is cached on disk keyed by its content digest, and its process starts on first call rather than at startup.** Measured first: 42 ms per plugin per invocation on an 18 MB binary, of which 8.7 ms is the hash and the rest is fork + exec + `sandbox-exec` + handshake + mTLS + `Describe`. Shell completion runs `rta` on every tab press, so ten plugins would put half a second between the key and the suggestions. Now 8.7 ms — the hash, which has to happen anyway for D27. Keying on content is what makes it safe: a hit means the bytes are identical, so there is no mtime to forge and no invalidation rule to get subtly wrong; every damaged entry is a miss rather than a failure. | Lazy launching cost almost nothing to add because `Client.live` already handled an absent process — it was written for the crashed-plugin case, so "never started" and "died" are one path rather than two that drift. |
| D27 | **`--allow-write` names plugins, and `--allow-destructive` pins an external plugin's capability to its artifact.** `AllowWrite` was one boolean consulted for every Write capability in the registry, including every one arriving from a plugin installed later; the property that matters is not that a namespace list is narrower but that it cannot *silently widen*, since a decision can only name what existed when it was taken. `--allow-destructive` allowlisted a capability *name*, which a malicious update keeping the ID would inherit — the one place in rta where a permission attached to a name rather than an artifact, on the surface with no human. The pin is required for an external plugin and **refused for a built-in**, whose artifact is the rta binary the operator already chose to run. `rta explain` prints the exact flag string. ADR 0015. | The review's last outstanding must-change, and the one the host-computed digest (ADR 0012 §4) made buildable. A control whose correct invocation has to be computed from a hash is a control people turn off, so the string is handed over rather than looked up. |
| D28 | **The plugin declaration cache is sealed, and the MAC covers the digest.** An unauthenticated cache defeats the very property D26 and D27 are built on: rta re-hashes a plugin on every `Open` so an authorisation binds to an artifact, and a cache entry is an assertion about a binary made *without* the binary. Anything that can write the data directory could therefore change what rta believed a plugin declared while its digest stayed identical — reproduced by flipping `hello.greet` from `Read` to `Destructive` with an attacker-chosen summary, which is `--allow-destructive` and a human-issued grant bypassed in one file write, and a prompt-injection channel (ADR 0013) that survives restarts. The digest is inside the MAC because a MAC over the declaration alone lets an authentic entry be moved to another digest's filename: every byte genuine, the signature valid, and rta serving one plugin's capabilities as another's. | Same bound as the grant seal and worth restating: it stops a writer that cannot read, which is exactly what a filesystem sandbox creates, and not an attacker who can read the directory and seal their own. The third instance of a pattern already named twice — `kv/crypt.go`'s recipient check, then `internal/grant` — and the first added after the rule was written down, which is the argument for the seal being a checklist item on anything rta writes and later trusts. |
| D29 | **rta follows the conventions it enforces on plugin authors.** `rta plugins` was a top-level bare plural that listed things, one letter from `rta plugin`, while every other listing in the app is `<namespace> list` and `sdktest` warns an author who invents a verb outside that vocabulary. It is now `rta plugin list`. Separately, a command that only groups others rejects an unknown subcommand: cobra's default is help-and-nil, only the registry-built namespaces overrode it, and `rta mcp serv` exited 0 having done nothing. | Both were invisible for the same reason — the majority behaved correctly and the exceptions were the commands nobody types twice. The group rule is now one function and the test walks the real command tree rather than naming groups, because naming them is what failed. |
| D30 | **A claim in prose that rta also enforces in code gets a test.** The authoring guide's verb list held five words that are not vocabulary and omitted eleven that are; the README undercounted the catalogue by three. `sdktest` exports `Vocabulary()` so the list has one home, and prose is a second home whether or not anybody meant it to be. | The README test is deliberately brittle — adding a capability fails it — because the fix is one word named in the failure, and the alternative is a headline number rta does not stand behind. |
| D31 | **A plugin's origin is recorded on its registration, not in a side map.** `registry.Register` takes a built-in; `RegisterFrom` takes a plugin and the artifact it came from. The gate, the inventory and every renderer read it from the registry, so provenance and registration cannot disagree — which they could, and did: a plugin that stayed registered while dropping out of the plugin host's process cache had no entry in the old `Origins` map, the gate read absence as "built in", and a built-in needs no digest pin on `--allow-destructive`. ADR 0015's artifact binding was defeated with no flaw in the check itself. Absence now means "not registered" and is refused. `pkg/plugin` still has no origin field: a value a plugin could state about its own provenance is a value it could state falsely. | The shape matters as much as the rule. The first attempt added a `BuiltIn` set every caller had to populate — three tests caught it, one by panicking, because a control whose zero value silently removes functionality teaches people to fill in a field rather than to be right. `NewServer` now defaults the lookup to the registry it was already handed, so the zero `Options` is correct and forgetting is impossible. Deleted two side maps, one of which I had added the same day for the TUI — the same mistake one screen over. |
| D32 | **A detail page composes every sibling that helps, bounded.** `NoPreview` means "do not run me unprompted" — the dashboard refreshes on a timer — and not "never run me". A detail page is the opposite case: somebody pressed enter. `fs.tree --detail` now includes `fs.usage`, given this request's own `--depth` so its walk is the same shape as the one that just happened. | The bound is the whole of it: composing an unbounded recursive scan off a keypress would have been the bug the old comment feared. `fs.usage --detail` is deliberately left alone — it already answers its own question completely, and a tree there is padding. "As much as makes sense" cuts both ways. |
| D33 | **A plugin's dashboard tile is `<namespace>.overview`.** The tile used to be whichever previewable capability was declared first, unless the plugin was a built-in named in a map inside the TUI — so a third-party plugin with a debug dump at the top of its list showed the debug dump, and had no way to say otherwise short of patching rta. `overview` costs no new API: it is already the vocabulary word sdktest enforces, with `dashboard`, `stats` and `summary` normalising onto it, and it already means at-a-glance. `rta plugin dev` now prints the tile it resolved, because which capability wins is a consequence of safety class, defaults, `NoPreview` and declaration order at once and is therefore invisible in the author's own source. | The convention does not override the capability: an `overview` that declares `NoPreview` still does not get put on a five-second timer, and neither does a pinned one — the pin chooses between tiles, it does not grant an exemption. `preferredTile` shrank from seven entries to one: five were restating what the rules already resolve to, which made the exception list look like the mechanism. The outcome is pinned instead by a test asserting the whole shipped dashboard, which catches the two things the map never could — a plugin that silently lost its tile, and one that silently gained a different one. `kv` is the survivor: `kv.status` is the glance precisely because the capability that would be called an overview is the one that cannot run unattended, and calling it `kv.overview` would read as a summary *of the secrets*. |
| D34 | **State a user would be upset to lose is written by `internal/atomicfile`, and nothing else.** Six places had grown their own copy of temp-file-plus-`Chmod`-plus-`Rename`, and the seventh — the config file — had grown a plain `os.WriteFile`, which truncates before it writes. That is the file the dashboard rewrites on every `[`, `]` and `H`, so the one written on a keystroke was the one that could be left truncated, and a truncated config is `config.invalid` on every subsequent run. Two of the six copies were the *same function*, `sealKey`, in two packages — and only `pluginhost`'s had been got right. `grant`'s wrote the seal key with `os.WriteFile` while `Load`, called from `Check` on every gated MCP call, reads it **without a lock**: a reader that caught it mid-write saw fewer than 32 bytes and reported that the grant file "was not written by rta", advising the operator to delete every grant they held. The same truncation left by a crash was permanent. | Two operations, because there are two guarantees. `Write` replaces: the path holds the old bytes or the new ones. `Publish` creates once, via `Link` rather than `O_EXCL` — `O_EXCL` creates the file *before* writing it, so the loser of the race opens a real empty file and reads "no key" — and it is what a key file needs, because a second key silently replacing the first invalidates every seal made with the first. It also fixed `kv`'s pre-upgrade backup, where `Stat`-then-`WriteFile` was check-then-act *and* could leave a short file that permanently satisfied the guard, so the real backup was never taken. **No `fsync`, and that is measured rather than assumed.** The first version synced before the rename, for the power-loss window; on macOS `os.File.Sync` is `F_FULLFSYNC` and a write went from ~0.21ms to ~5.3ms — 26x, on the path a dashboard keystroke takes and the path every gated MCP call takes twice. The guarantee actually wanted is against a *process* dying, and rename gives that whole. `bench_test.go` keeps the number reproducible instead of remembered. A parse-based drift test now fails on any new `os.WriteFile` outside a two-entry allowlist — both defects were a discipline held everywhere but one place, with nothing to make the omission visible. |
| D35 | **The grant lock is held by identity, not by name.** Every operation on the sentinel file was by path: `release` removed whatever was there, and a waiter that judged the lock stale removed whatever was there. A name is not the file you looked at, so two waiters that both found a crashed holder's lock both removed it — the second removing the *winner's* fresh lock — and both went on to hold it; and a holder whose lock had been broken as stale removed its successor's on the way out. Either way two processes are inside the read-modify-write the lock exists to serialize, which puts back exactly the lost `grant revoke` that motivated the lock in the first place. The sentinel now carries a token: acquiring is an `atomicfile.Publish` that reports whose token won, releasing is a no-op unless the token is still ours, and breaking a stale lock renames it aside first and confirms by `os.SameFile` that it moved the one it judged — restoring with `Link`, never `Rename`, so putting a live lock back can never overwrite a third process's. | The first regression test for this was **vacuous and the mutation run caught it**: eight goroutines racing a planted stale lock passed against the unconditional remove every time, because the window between one waiter's stat and another's remove is two syscalls and the scheduler never obliged. Replaced with a deterministic test that hands `breakStale` a stale `FileInfo` and a live file. Reusing `Publish` also exposed a hole in it: a lock is created and *released* constantly, so "someone beat me to it" and "and then they let go" is the ordinary case, and returning an error there turned every contended acquire into a failure. The grant suite's existing concurrency test caught that within a minute of the change. |
| D36 | **Every section rta's own catalogue produces carries a stable id.** `view.Section` has had an `ID` and a `Title` since the two jobs were separated — the title is read by a person and should improve, the id is what a script pulling one section out of a page or an agent citing a fact addresses it by — but `Key()` falls back to the title, and 33 of the catalogue's 38 sections were taking that fallback. So rta shipped the contract and then relied on the escape hatch, which made its own headings load-bearing: rewording one silently renamed the handle. `audit` was the sharpest case, because its group constants were *one prose string* used as both the finding-group key and the section heading, so the two could not be changed independently even in principle; `group` is now `{id, title}`. | Optional for plugin authors, mandatory for rta. `pkg/view` keeps ID optional on purpose — a section pays for stability only when it wants it — and `sdktest` now warns rather than fails, in the same register as the verb vocabulary (D8). rta's own pages are the ones agents read, so `internal/app` asserts it: every `Read` capability reachable from the conformance inputs is run, every section must have a kebab-case id, unique within its page. The test **logs what it reached and what it skipped and fails below a coverage floor**, because the first version quietly reached three capabilities and passed — a green test that had stopped covering the thing it was named for. |
| D37 | **The gate that enforces the frozen proto did not run anywhere.** `proto/v1 frozen ✅` is an M2 exit criterion and `make proto-check` is the `buf breaking` run that enforces it, against `main` rather than the working tree so it answers "is this release compatible". `make ci` did not call it — the target listed `proto-lint` only — and the GitHub workflow called neither, because it runs its steps directly and never invokes `make ci`. The `ci` target's own comment, "everything CI runs, so a green laptop means a green pipeline", was false in both directions. Reproduced: renumbering `Field.has_suggest` from 11 to 13, which breaks every plugin compiled against v1, passes gofmt, vet, build and the full `-race -shuffle` suite, and is caught by nothing. `ci` now calls `proto-check`; the workflow runs both, on one OS since they read `.proto` files rather than the machine, with `fetch-depth: 0` because `buf breaking --against main` has no base in a depth-1 checkout. | The same shape as `HardenSelf` earlier in this session: a control documented in two places and implemented in none, and the worst form a security or compatibility claim can take. Found by an adversarial design review that was looking for somewhere to put a new proto field and noticed there was nothing standing between it and a breaking one. |
| D38 | **Restarting a plugin leaked the process it replaced.** `live()` swaps `client`, `stub`, `cmd` and `logger` in place — that is what lets a restarted plugin keep serving handlers registered against the dead one — and dropped the old `*goplugin.Client` without killing it, so nothing closed its gRPC `ClientConn`. Measured against the example plugin: **four goroutines per restart**, none collected — grpcsync callback serializers, an HTTP/2 client transport and a yamux session, all serving a process that no longer exists. Invisible on the CLI, where rta exits; accumulating in `rta mcp serve`, which is long-lived and is the entire reason `live()` exists. `Close`'s bounded teardown is now a shared `teardown(client, cmd, logger)` that `live` runs after releasing `c.mu` — registered as a defer *before* the unlock defer, since defers are LIFO. | The review that surfaced this proposed asserting on a removed socket directory, citing `Kill`'s `os.RemoveAll(hostSocketDir)`. **That test would have passed with or without the fix**: `socketDir` is only allocated when `RunnerFunc` is set, and rta passes `Cmd`, so the `RemoveAll` is a no-op here. The leak is the connection, and the only thing that observes it is a goroutine count — which is what the regression test does, and what the mutation confirms at 3.9 per restart. |
| D39 | **Per-plugin configuration is a declared input, and its section is named after the artifact rather than the namespace.** §4.5 specified six host services as broker callbacks and none were built; an adversarial review concluded three rows do not survive contact with the tree, and that the thing actually missing is not a callback. `Field.Config` names a key an input may be filled from when the caller supplied none — precedence caller, config, `Default`, applied in `plugin.Resolve`, invisible to the handler. The section is `plugins.<ns>` for a built-in and `plugins.<ns>@<digest>` for anything on `$PATH`, reusing `--allow-destructive`'s grammar and all three of `destructiveAllowed`'s branches: **a namespace is what a plugin declares about itself and `$PATH` order decides who declares it first**, so a squatter that already impersonates `pg.query` must not additionally be handed the operator's stated values, unprompted, on a five-second dashboard timer. `Secret` and `Positional` inputs refuse `Config`. ADR 0016; §4.5 amended; ADR 0012's revisit trigger re-taken and re-armed. | **Most of the work was invisible from the protocol.** Three surfaces could never have lost to config: `collectValues` recorded every declared flag because cobra bakes defaults into its flag set, so "the caller supplied none" was inexpressible on the CLI; `candidates` never called `Resolve` and had been getting defaults *by accident* through that same behaviour, so gating it would have silently narrowed `Field.Suggest`'s contract for every plugin; `capForm.values()` wrote every bool unconditionally, so config could never fill a `Bool` in the TUI. `Resolve` took a third parameter rather than growing a `ResolveWith` sibling — nothing is published, and a `Resolve` still callable the old way is a fourth shadowing surface waiting to happen. A mutation run then found the config overlay's `if supplied { continue }` guard was **dead**: the caller's values overwrite it two lines later, so precedence is carried by ordering and the check only looked like the rule. |
| D40 | **A plugin may only declare the namespace it is installed under.** `Found.Name` had said so since it was written — *"a binary named rta-plugin-x declaring namespace kv is a collision to refuse, not a name to trust"* — and nothing implemented it. `RegisterFrom` refuses a namespace that is already *taken*, which protects the built-ins because they register first and protects nothing at all between two plugins on `$PATH`. Reproduced: a file called `rta-plugin-aaa-innocent` declaring `acmedb` registered as `acmedb`, appeared in `rta plugin list` as `acmedb`, and served `rta acmedb …` with nothing connecting the two names — and since discovery is `$PATH` order then alphabetical, it also beat a correctly-named `rta-plugin-acmedb` to the namespace. `LoadInto` now refuses the disagreement, names the file and its digest, and says what to rename. `rta plugin dev` is deliberately exempt: it bypasses `LoadInto`, and an author running their own build is not the threat. | **The fourth control this session that was documented and not implemented** (after `HardenSelf`, `proto-check`, and `make ci`'s claim to mirror the pipeline) — and the fourth instance of one defect: *a name a thing chooses for itself is not an identity*. `pathguard` compared path strings, the grant lock released by path, config sections keyed on a declared namespace, and registration trusted a declared name. The filename is not a better name in the abstract; it is a name the **operator** controls by choosing what to install where, and where the two disagree the operator wins. What it does not close is a file genuinely called `rta-plugin-pg` earlier on `$PATH` — ordinary shadowing, the risk model every command on `$PATH` already has, which `Discover` now records on the winner and `rta doctor` reports rather than dropping silently. |
| D41 | **The path guard judged one string and handed on another, and the string it judged was the wrong one.** `resolve` called `filepath.Abs`/`Clean` before touching the filesystem, and `Clean` cancels `..` against the preceding component lexically — so a directory symlink was gone from the string before `EvalSymlinks` ran. `root/link/id_rsa` was refused; `root/link/../secrets/id_rsa` was **allowed and read the same bytes**, because the kernel resolves the link first and *then* applies `..`. Each real directory level under a root bought one more `..`, so the reach was arbitrary — `/etc/passwd` and rta's own `grants.key` both. Now resolved component by component, so `..` means what it means to the kernel, while a path that does not exist yet still resolves. | **The second half is the one that generalises.** `Check` was a predicate: it computed the true path, decided, and threw it away, and `checkPaths` passed the caller's *original spelling* to the handler. Two readers of one string — `builtin/fs` survived this bug only because its own `resolvePath` happens to `Clean` the same way, `builtin/net` opens the raw value and did not. `Check` now returns what it judged and `checkPaths` substitutes it, so the next `resolve` defect is a handler opening a path that is not there rather than an escape. The existing symlink tests could not have caught any of it: they build paths with `filepath.Join`, which `Clean`s, so the crafted spelling is inexpressible in them. |
| D42 | **Two path-valued inputs were declared `StringSlice`, so the MCP path gate structurally could not see them.** `checkPaths` hooks `Field.Type == Path`; there is no type for a *list* of paths, so `cert.expiry`'s `targets` and `kv.init`'s `recipient` both landed as `StringSlice` and both vanished from the gate. `cert.expiry` shared `loadCerts` with its confined siblings, giving it a file branch its own help never claimed — over MCP with no flag and no grant it answered "exists / is PEM / is unreadable / is a directory" for anywhere on the machine while `cert.inspect` was refused for the same string. `kv.init`'s `recipient` is `os.ReadFile`d by `parseRecipient`, which echoes the file's first meaningful line back in the parse error — 32 characters of any readable file, plus a classifier for every other outcome. `cert.expiry` now dials and never reads; every kv `recipient` is `Local`, including `kv.rekey`'s, which ADR 0014 had recorded as an accepted gap. | **`TestEveryPathInputIsConfined` asks the control its own question.** It iterates `f.Type == Path`, so it can only ever confirm the gate agrees with itself, and an input declared as anything else is invisible to it — which is why both shipped. Its replacement asks from the other side, and **its limits are measured rather than claimed**: reverting the `kv.init` fix leaves the new test green, because that input's help names no file and the read happens two functions away. It catches the `kv.rekey` shape and not the `kv.init` one, and says so in its own comment. The general answer — walk `builtin/` for filesystem calls reachable from a handler and require that capability's String-ish inputs to be `Path` or `Local`, the inversion `atomicfile/drift_test.go` already makes for state writes — needs interprocedural reachability and is recorded as open rather than half-built. `checkPaths` also stopped `continue`-ing past a non-string value: a silent pass in the one function whose failure mode must be a refusal. |
| D43 | **A panel drew its own name in the colour its contents use for their keys.** `theme` defines `Key`, `Header` and `Title` as three names for one appearance — Primary, bold — and `panel()` took a pre-rendered title string, so each of its four call sites styled its own and each independently reached for one of the three. The result is that a dashboard tile announced itself in exactly the orange of the keys inside it, and the eye had nothing to separate the pane from its contents. Added `theme.Label` = `#7794B6`, a slate blue chosen against four measured constraints rather than by eye: hue 212° against Primary's 15°, near enough opposite that a title can never read as content; **calibrated to Primary's exact relative luminance** (0.286 both), which is also what keeps its 3.12 contrast on a light terminal; 30% saturation, so it stays subordinate to the content it names; and 57° from `Good`, the nearest status hue. **The first shortlist was mostly wrong** — sky blue, cyan and mauve all read well in isolation but sat 26–38% brighter than Primary, dropping them to 2.4–2.6 on white, under the 3.0 floor for bold text, so every candidate was recalibrated to Primary's luminance and the choice reduced to hue alone. Four otherwise-good hues were then ruled out on collision rather than taste: sage is 12° from `Good` and muted cyan 34°, dusty rose is 24° from `Bad`, and warm taupe is 4° from `Accent` at 18% saturation, so it reads as *faded orange* — the exact ambiguity being removed. | **The colour is the smaller half.** A convention four places have to remember is one the fifth breaks, so `panel` now takes a `panelHead{Title, Note, Right}` of *plain text* and paints the whole top border itself — there is nowhere left to decide it differently, and the four call sites got shorter. The first regression test **could not fail**: it compared the SGR sequences before the title and before a body key, and the title carries a reset left over from the border segment ahead of it while the key does not, so two identical oranges compared unequal and painting the title with `theme.Key` still passed. Comparing the `38;2;r;g;b` triple instead catches all three mutations — Label reverted to Primary, `panel` painting with `theme.Key`, and Label set to a status colour. |
| D44 | **A plugin could disable `--dry-run` by declaring an input called `dry-run`.** cobra resolves a subcommand's own flag before an inherited one, so a declared input does not *collide* with a root persistent flag — it silently becomes it, and the host's copy is never set. Reproduced: against a plugin declaring that input, `rta acme wipe --yes --dry-run` exits 0, prints success, and **performs the wipe**. The operator asked for a preview and the one flag promising nothing would happen is the flag that stopped working. `reservedInputs` existed to prevent exactly this and named one thing, `detail`, while the CLI had grown five more: `dry-run`, `yes`, `output`, `no-color`, and cobra's own `help`. Only `dry-run` fails open — a shadowed `--yes` prompts, a shadowed `-o` merely formats wrong, a shadowed `--help` makes help unreachable — but all six are now reserved, refused at registration, and each carries *why* in the map. | **The list could not have stayed right, because nothing could see both halves.** `reservedInputs` is in `pkg/plugin`, which knows nothing of cobra or `internal/app`; the flag set is in `internal/app`, which never reads the list. Neither derives from the other, so the map is now the *contract* and `TestTheCLIReservesEveryNameItOwns` walks the built command tree — calling `InitDefaultHelpFlag` first, since cobra adds `--help` at execute time and a tree inspected before that has none — and fails on any owned flag nobody reserved. **It checks the reverse direction too**, so a reservation left behind by a renamed flag is caught as well: a name refused for nothing costs an author a legitimate input. Mutation-confirmed on all four: the original one-entry list, a newly added global flag, a stale reservation, and `Validate` no longer refusing. Single letters are deliberately *not* reserved — `--o` and `-o` coexist because pflag keeps long names and shorthands in separate namespaces, which was verified rather than assumed, since over-reserving refuses real names for a collision that does not occur. |
| D45 | **Every rta invocation handed the caller's standard input to every plugin on `$PATH`.** go-plugin does `cmd.Stdin = os.Stdin` unconditionally (`client.go:659`, no opt-out), so `internal/stdio` exists to repoint that variable before anything is spawned. It was written as a per-surface courtesy — `Claim` for `mcp serve`, `Silence` for the human surfaces — and **`Silence` had zero call sites**, while the single `Claim` sat inside `mcp serve`'s own `RunE`, which cobra runs from `fang.Execute` long after `main` has called `LoadPlugins`. Discovery is eager, because the command tree is built from plugin declarations, so the spawn that copies fd 0 had already happened on **every** command. Reproduced with a binary that does not even complete the handshake: `printf 'SECRET-PASSPHRASE…' \| rta explain sys.status` handed it 36 bytes — an ordinary read-only command, not `mcp serve`. fd 0 is now claimed once by `main` before anything launches, surfaces ask `stdio.Real()`, and `Silence` is deleted. ADR 0012 §5 corrected: it claimed the repoint happened "before the registry can spawn anything" and that "it covers the CLI and TUI cases", and **both were false as written**. | **The fifth control this session documented and not implemented**, after `HardenSelf`, `proto-check`, `make ci`'s claim to mirror the pipeline, and the namespace rule — and the sharpest case for the fix being *ownership* rather than a missing call. A resource with one owner cannot be protected by asking four surfaces to remember something; `Silence` existing at all was the tell. Claiming centrally inverts the failure mode: every reader now has to be wired to `stdio.Real()` or it reads `/dev/null` immediately and loudly, instead of one forgotten call leaking silently. Five readers were rewired (`mcp serve`, `tea.WithInput`, two kv passphrase prompts, the kv `$EDITOR` hand-off) plus `canPrompt`, whose `term.IsTerminal(os.Stdin.Fd())` would otherwise have answered "nobody here" on every CLI run. `TestOnlyStdioNamesOsStdin` walks the tree with an **empty** allowlist. The mutation that mattered was the one the first six tests all missed: deleting the call from `main` fails only the build, and deleting the import too compiles clean and reopens it in full — so a separate AST test asserts `main` calls `Claim` *and* that it precedes `LoadPlugins`, presence and ordering apart, since the original defect was a correct call in the wrong place. |
| D46 | **The same defect one level up: a plugin's namespace could replace one of rta's own commands.** A namespace becomes a top-level command, and nothing reserved the seven names rta already holds — `doctor`, `explain`, `plugin`, `mcp`, `init`, plus cobra's `help` and `completion`. Reproduced for all seven: a plugin named `doctor` registers, and `rta doctor` then prints *the plugin's* usage and exits 0, having run none of the checks. **The command most likely to reveal a hostile plugin is the one a hostile plugin can switch off**, and nothing anywhere reports that it happened. `RegisterFrom` already refuses a namespace another *plugin* holds, which is why `sys` and `kv` were never available — rta's own commands are not plugins and were protected by nothing at all. Refused in `Plugin.Validate` now, with the same shape as D44: reasons in the map, and a drift test walking the built tree in both directions. | **Found by a sweep agent, one hour after I shipped D44 and thought the class was closed.** D44 fixed reserved *inputs* and I wrote a drift test that walks flags — so the fix was correct and the generalisation was not made. The vocabulary rta owns is not "flags": it is *names*, at every level where a declaration meets the command tree. The two tests are now deliberately symmetric, and both call cobra's lazy initialisers first (`InitDefaultHelpFlag`, `InitDefaultHelpCmd`, `InitDefaultCompletionCmd`) — a tree inspected before those runs is missing exactly the names nobody thinks to reserve, because they are not in any rta source file. Mutation-confirmed on all three: nothing reserved, a new rta command added without reserving it, and `Validate` no longer refusing. **A verifier then found a flaw in the flag test itself**, which had walked every command's persistent flags and *added root's local flags* to cover `--help`. That over-collects: `--version` is local to root too, and cobra gives it to root alone, so the test would have demanded `version` be reserved to protect a flag no capability can reach. It now asks an actual capability command for `cmd.Flags()` — precisely the set pflag resolves a declared input against — which cannot make that class of mistake. The same verifier reported `version` as an eighth hijackable namespace; **that part was wrong** and the reverse-direction check is what says so: `rta version` is `Unknown command`, `--version` is a flag, and a plugin named `version` would *create* the command rather than shadow one. |
| D47 | **`--root` did not confine three built-in read capabilities, because a declared `Default` was never shown to the path gate.** `handler()` ran `checkPaths` on what the caller sent and applied declared defaults *afterwards*, so an omitted argument reached the handler unchecked. Reproduced over real MCP stdio with `--root` pointing elsewhere: `fs_tree {"path": "<launch-dir>"}` refused with `core.mcp.path.outside`, and `fs_tree {}` **read that same directory and listed its contents**. The exemption was deliberate and its comment gave a reason — a default is the plugin's own choice, and "`net.hosts.list` defaults to /etc/hosts and must keep working". **That capability declares no default**: `/etc/hosts` is the handler constant `hostsFile`, so the exemption's one named beneficiary never used it, and its only real users were three inputs declaring `Default: "."` — `audit.deps`, `fs.tree`, `fs.usage` — where "." is not a considered choice of a system file but *wherever the server happened to be launched*, which is the exact thing `--root` exists to overrule. `checkPaths` now runs after defaults. Where root and cwd coincide — every run without `--root` — nothing changes, verified. | **The existing test could not have caught it and passed against the bug for as long as it existed.** `TestADeclaredDefaultOutsideTheRootStillWorks` called `checkPaths` directly, but the defect was the *ordering of two calls inside `handler()`*, which no direct call can observe — the same shape as D42's `TestEveryPathInputIsConfined`, a control asked its own question. Its replacement drives `CallTool`, and its other half is kept as a separate test because that half still holds: a caller-supplied path is checked even when it equals the default, since matching a default is not a permission. The `--root` help text was corrected too — it promised "a capability's own default path is not affected", which was the hole stated as a feature, and justified it with the same wrong example. It now says what is actually true and names the real limitation: **the gate governs path *arguments*, never what a handler opens**, so a plugin reading a fixed file needs no `Default`, no `Path` input, and no MCP at all — which is why the reported escalation to `grants.key` was struck rather than fixed here. On macOS that read is refused by `sandbox-exec` (`paths.Data()` denied); on Linux `confine_other.go` is `const confined = false` and nothing stops it. |
| D48 | **The two MCP allowlist flags had drifted into two grammars, and the stricter one silently disabled the control.** `--allow-destructive` *requires* `id@digest` and its refusal hands over the exact string to type (ADR 0015); `--allow-write` compared the whole entry as one string, so it did not parse that grammar at all. An operator who learned the pin from the flag that demands it and applied it to the other got **`--allow-write pg@1a2b3c` → not exposed**, while the weaker `--allow-write pg` worked — the wrong direction, and completely silent, because an entry that authorizes nothing is indistinguishable from one nobody wrote: the capability is simply absent from `tools/list` and the agent reports only that the tool does not exist. Both flags now share `entryMatches`, with the one deliberate difference carried as a parameter rather than as duplicated code: a bare entry is refused for destructive and accepted for write, which is the namespace granularity ADR 0015 chose on purpose. `writeAllowed` also resolves provenance now, so an unwired gate allows no write — it already allowed no destructive. | **The gap was visible in the ADR the whole time.** Its heading says "`--allow-write` and `--allow-destructive` are now artifact-scoped" and its body describes `--allow-write` as scoped to namespaces, with a different justification ("cannot silently widen" to plugins installed later, which is true). A heading that overclaims what the body delivers is the same defect as ADR 0012's stdin paragraph one commit earlier — **the second false ADR claim found this session, both about controls nobody re-read.** The fix is not only the grammar: `Options.Problems` now reports every entry that authorizes nothing, printed at `mcp serve` startup where the operator is, and it caught two cases the finding never mentioned — `--allow-write sys`, where the namespace has no write capabilities at all, and `--allow-destructive kv.list`, where the capability is `read`. A stale pin's message names the installed short digest, because ADR 0015's own argument is that a control requiring homework is one that gets turned off. Five mutations, including one that had to be rewritten to compile before it proved anything. |
| D49 | **Open item 1 answered by testing the property instead of approximating it.** The recorded plan was an interprocedural AST walk of `builtin/` — find filesystem calls reachable from a handler, then demand that capability's String-ish inputs be `Path` or `Local`. That is a proxy for the thing that matters, needs whole-package reachability, and would be defeated by any indirection it failed to follow. The question is really *can a caller tell an existing file from a missing one through this input?* — an oracle is exactly what the gate exists to prevent, and it can be **observed** instead of inferred. `TestNoInputBehavesLikeAFileWithoutBeingDeclaredOne` sets each non-`Path`, non-`Local` String/StringSlice/Text input to one canary path, then runs the capability with the file absent, present and absent again; if the answer moves, the input is a path the gate never sees. Write capabilities run under `dry_run`, which the contract already makes mandatory for them; `Destructive` and the three network-reaching namespaces are excluded and say so. 44 inputs across seven namespaces. | **The first version reported four defects and every one was a false positive**, which is the whole design lesson: it compared two *different* path strings, so `todo.search` — which answers `No tasks match "<value>"` — differed because the string differed, not because anything was read. Holding the value fixed and moving only the file makes the filesystem the sole variable. A stability pass (two runs in the same state) then removes the genuinely non-deterministic inputs, and it correctly found `gen.token.encoding` and `gen.uuid.version`. **It catches neither case that motivated it, and the comment says so with the measurement**, including a correction: reverting `kv.init`'s recipient does not fail it, and my first explanation for that — undrivable — was wrong. It *is* driven and the input *is* probed (43 → 44, named); it bails on a passphrase this environment cannot supply, so the read never happens. A separate sweep of all twelve namespaces found **zero** unprotected inputs today, and dismissed its one candidate with a finding about D41: declaring `kv.edit`'s `key` as `Path` would be *actively harmful*, because `checkPaths` now **substitutes** the resolved value, so the handler would look up an absolute path in the store and every legitimate call would fail. `Path` is not a free safety annotation — it changes what the handler receives. |
| D50 | **An external plugin had no way to obtain a credential, and the ADR said it did.** Found in the first ten minutes of building `pg`, which is exactly what M2 kept it on the list for. ADR 0016 states "a plugin declares a `Secret` input with `Local: true`, `plugin.Resolve` fills it" — `resolve.go` had **no `Local` handling at all**. `Local` meant only *never accepted from a remote caller*; nothing ever supplied one. Config is refused on `Secret` by design, an MCP caller's value is stripped by design, and the plugin's own environment is an allowlist of seven names (`PATH`, `HOME`, `TMPDIR`, `TZ`, `LANG`, `SSL_CERT_*`, `LC_*`) that deliberately excludes `RTA_*` so a plugin cannot exfiltrate an exported cloud session by starting up. Every route was closed. Demonstrated rather than argued, with a probe plugin: `PGPASSWORD` empty, the `Local` input empty. | **A built-in never noticed, which is the P6 violation.** `kv` runs inside rta and reads the environment itself — `RTA_KV_PASSPHRASE`, `RTA_KV_IDENTITY` — so the documented pattern worked for exactly the plugins that did not need it to. That is already `RTA_<NAMESPACE>_<INPUT>`: **the convention existed, implemented once, written down nowhere, and reachable only by a built-in.** `Resolve` now fills any unset `Local` input from it, so the fix generalises kv rather than inventing anything, and the derived names match kv's hand-written ones exactly — which is how I know it is the same convention. The name is derived and never declared, and that is the whole security property: a declared `Env` field would let a hostile plugin name `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`. **The inherited environment is untouched** — verified after the fix, the plugin's `env-names` list is byte-identical and the variable is absent; the host reads it and passes the value over the AutoMTLS socket. `rta explain` prints the variable name, never the value. Third false ADR claim this session, after 0012's stdin paragraph and 0015's "artifact-scoped" heading. |
| D51 | **Walking the plugin lifecycle end to end, as an operator, on a plugin that was not written for the test.** Scaffold → build → install → `list` → `doctor` → `explain` → configure → upgrade → misinstall → delete, with a stand-in for `pg` declaring the two things nothing in the catalogue had ever used: a `Config`-backed field and a `Local` credential. **The chain works, and this is its first end-to-end proof**: the declared default applies, the operator's `plugins.weather@c6416e832936` section beats it, `RTA_WEATHER_API_KEY` fills the credential, and `rta explain` hands over *both* strings ready to paste — the pinned section and the variable name. Rebuilding the binary changed the digest, the config silently stopped applying exactly as ADR 0016 says it should, and `rta doctor` reported the stale pin **and** the installed one. D40's refusal fired on a misnamed copy, and deletion left `doctor` reporting the orphaned section. | **Three operator-facing defects, all in output rather than behaviour, two of them in code written earlier this session.** (1) D40's refusal read "rename it to `rta-plugin-weather`, or install it as `rta-plugin-weather`" — two ways of saying one thing, one of which the operator had just done, because the declared name was interpolated into both halves. There are genuinely two remedies — move the file, or move the declaration — and now it says so; the test asserts the halves *differ*, which is the assertion that would have caught it. (2) `doctor` said `1 capabilities`. (3) `doctor` said "1 from $PATH" and "none found on $PATH" in the same report: both true, one tier apart, and nothing on the page said so, which makes a reader distrust whichever they read second. The row is named for what it scans now. None of the three was findable by reading the code — they are only visible to somebody who runs the thing and reads what comes back. |
| D52 | **`pg` ships, and it was driven against a real PostgreSQL rather than a mock.** `plugins/pg` — its own `go.mod`, consuming the SDK as a stranger would (P6). Five capabilities: `pg.status`, `pg.database.list`, `pg.table.list`, `pg.query`, `pg.activity`. **`pg.query` declares `Safety: Read` and PostgreSQL enforces it**: it runs inside a `READ ONLY` transaction, so the server refuses writes against the *parsed statement*, not against a string. Verified on a live server — `delete from orders` refused, and so was `with gone as (delete from orders returning *) select count(*) from gone`, which is a SELECT that deletes and which no regex-based allowlist catches; 500 rows still there afterwards. Connection inputs are `Config`-backed and the password is `Secret`+`Local`, so `rta explain pg.status` hands over both the pinned config heading and `$RTA_PG_PASSWORD`. Every classic failure is classified with a hint that names the next command: wrong password, refused, unknown host, missing database, insufficient privilege, timeout, no TLS. | **Four things the first real plugin found, which is what it was kept on the list for.** (1) **`plugins/` is invisible to CI.** A separate `go.mod` means the root `go test ./...` never compiles it, so a change to `pkg/plugin` breaking the first external plugin would have gone green — the exact breakage it exists to catch, and D37's shape again. `make plugins` now walks every module under `plugins/`, `make ci` and the workflow call it, and a mutation renaming `plugin.LocalEnvVar` fails it with `undefined`. (2) **`view.Pair` has no `Kind`.** A `Table` column can declare itself a byte count and a key-value pair cannot, so `pg.status` has to format its own size server-side with `pg_size_pretty`. Recorded rather than papered over — an additive contract gap. (3) **`fmt.Sprint` is wrong for driver values**: `select total` printed `{5 3 false finite true}`, pgx's `pgtype.Numeric` struct. Every pgtype scalar implements `driver.Valuer` and knows how to render itself. (4) **A hint named `rta pg database list` before that capability existed.** The capability now exists, and a test asserts no hint names a `rta pg …` command this plugin does not declare — mutation-confirmed by deleting it. |
| D53 | **Distribution designed (ADR 0017), and the design is built on the one thing rta has that plugin managers do not: a declaration available before the plugin runs.** Krew's shape is adopted — a git repository of per-plugin manifests, `index add` for third-party sources so the official one is a default rather than a monopoly, a `store/` + `bin/` split so uninstall and upgrade are operations instead of `rm` and overwrite. **Krew's trust model is rejected.** Its manifest carries a SHA256 checked once at install, and its own documentation says the plugins are unaudited arbitrary programs — which is not a failure of care, because a kubectl plugin is an opaque executable and there is nothing an index could truthfully say about it. rta's manifests carry the namespace, capability IDs and safety classes, so a catalogue is searchable by what a plugin may *do*, and **install verifies the manifest's declaration against what the binary actually declares** — a check no opaque-executable manager can perform. The index states claims; `rta.lock` records only what rta computed from the bytes. | **The roadmap contradicted an ADR and nobody had noticed.** ADR 0002's consequences say "trust is handled via lockfile checksums + cosign signatures" and the M3 row said `rta.lock` + cosign; ADR 0015 says *"three 2026 campaigns shipped malware with valid provenance, so signing would not have answered it either"*. Both cannot stand. 0017 resolves it in 0015's favour: **a signature is recorded, never required**, because a gate an attacker can satisfy is the one people stop looking past — and the event that matters is a capability changing safety class, which the same publisher signing a worse plugin verifies perfectly. The upgrade flow therefore prints a *declaration diff* (`+ pg.table.drop destructive`, `! pg.query read → write`), which is only expressible because the declaration exists. Two findings recorded where somebody will look for them: publishing the Go module is the precondition for every other item, since `plugin new`'s scaffold cannot resolve its own dependency today (D51); and confinement is macOS-only (`confine_other.go` is `const confined = false`), which distribution makes sharper rather than softer — the point of an index is to make installing easy, and on two of three platforms an installed plugin reads whatever the user can. |
| D54 | **Tunnels re-decided (ADR 0018), and the thing that changed is what dials.** ADR 0004's contract survives untouched — the host resolves the tunnel, the plugin sees a plain `host:port`, one orthogonal feature × N plugins — and three things around it did not survive three days. **Profiles are still unbuilt**, so "profiles may declare `tunnel:`" has nowhere to live; a named `targets:` block is a profile in all but name, scoped to one job. **ADR 0016 built the delivery mechanism the day after ADR 0004 was written, without knowing it**: `pg` already declares `host`/`port` as ordinary inputs, and `plugin.Resolve` already layers sources so a handler cannot tell which one supplied a value — so a tunnel is *one more source* (caller → target → config → default) and `plugins/pg` does not change by a line. Filled through a declared `Field.Endpoint` rather than by convention on the names `host` and `port`, because *a name a thing chooses for itself is not an identity* is the lesson this codebase has now learned five times (D40). | **"The host dials via client-go" was never costed, and the number decides it.** Measured for the exact surface a resolver needs (`clientcmd` + `spdy` + `portforward`): **+67 modules, +11 MB** against a 147-module 27 MB binary — roughly +40% on the core for a feature a minority uses. The rest of the argument is authentication: OIDC refresh, `aws eks get-token`, `gke-gcloud-auth-plugin`, corporate proxies. client-go supports all of it and adopting it means adopting its *maintenance*, so every provider's auth story becomes rta's problem the first time one changes. `kubectl` has solved it already, on the user's machine, in the version they trust — and ADR 0002 ships an exec tier precisely so nobody is blocked by rta's own SDK. The contract is unchanged either way, so client-go stays available behind the same `targets:` surface. **Five questions were recorded for the prototype to answer before the ADR was amended**, so that the amendment is a comparison rather than a story — and `internal/tunnel` now answers three. The local port is parsed off kubectl's stdout and the endpoint is **dialled** in the test rather than merely parsed; six failure shapes are classified from kubectl's real message text; teardown is asserted by a `SIGTERM` trap deleting a marker, so a forward outliving its call fails the test. Two need a cluster and stay open: the real setup cost (stubbed, rta's own overhead is **52 ms open / 0.4 ms close**, which excludes the round-trip that matters) and whether `Forwarding from` means traffic actually flows. `kubectl exec … -- psql` is rejected in writing: it returns text, and the point of `pg` is typed views — a tunnel gives the plugin a connection, `exec` gives it a screenshot. **Four defects, three in the prototype and the interesting one in its test.** `-race` caught a stderr race on the first run, because `os/exec` writes `cmd.Stderr` from a goroutine it owns while the classifier reads it. A **two-second stall on every failed open passed every test**: two waiters, one *send* on a buffered channel, and whichever arrived first consumed it — fixed by closing a channel, which everyone observes. The first timing figure was ~1 s and was **mostly the fake kubectl's SIGTERM latency**; split into open and close it is 52 ms and 0.4 ms, because a measurement that cannot be attributed is not a measurement. And the regression test for the stall first asserted on **elapsed time**, passed alone, and failed in the full `-race -shuffle` suite where it was measuring the machine; rewritten to assert the mechanism it was then **vacuous** — `Open` returns nil on failure, so it was asserting against a nil tunnel — and only mutation found that. |
| D55 | **The operator's plugin configuration reached the CLI and no part of the TUI.** `tui.Run` took a `pluginCfg` and `tui.New` had no parameter to put it in, so `Model.pluginCfg` was declared, documented, read in five places, and **never once written**. Every reader saw nil: a form opened on the declared defaults, so somebody who had stated `host`/`port`/`user` in their file was shown `localhost:5432/postgres` and had to retype all of it; a run launched from the shell went wherever the defaults pointed; and a dashboard tile refreshed against them every five seconds, unasked. ADR 0016 exists so an operator states a connection once — it was delivered on one surface of two. Found by driving the real TUI against a real database through a real port-forward and reading the form, which is the only place the value is visible. | **Nothing could have caught it, and that is the transferable part.** `New` was the only way to build a `Model` and it had nowhere to put a configuration, so no test *could* hand one over — all 48 call sites passed a registry and a dashboard, and the parameter that mattered was accepted by a function no test calls. A value that only one uncallable entry point can supply is not covered by tests; it is invisible to them. The fix puts it on the constructor rather than behind a setter, which is ADR 0016's own argument for `plugin.Resolve`'s third parameter reused: nothing is published, so the breaking change was available, and a constructor still callable the old way is the same defect waiting to be reintroduced — every test now types `nil` deliberately. Three tests, one per reader (`startForm`, `startRun`, `Init`), each mutation-checked separately; the run test **survived its own mutation** on the first attempt because it called `runCmd` with `m.configFor(c)` directly and was therefore asserting its own plumbing rather than the shell's. |
| D56 | **A target may also say who you are to the thing at the other end (ADR 0018 §6), and the cluster answered the two questions the stub could not.** A tunnel without credentials is half an answer: the database is in the cluster and so is the Secret holding its password, so an operator spared the port-forward is still copying a credential into shell history or an exported variable — *a security control that requires homework is a security control that gets turned off* (ADR 0015). A target now names the Secret beside the service and maps its keys onto declared inputs. **The mapping is the operator's, never the plugin's** — D50's rule unchanged: a plugin that could name the Secret it wanted could name any Secret in the namespace, so it declares only that it *has* an input. The mapping is an allowlist, the Secret lives in the target's own namespace, and the file holds a reference rather than a value, so ADR 0016's refusal of `Config` on a `Secret` still holds. | **Answered against `kind` plus `docs/examples/kube-lab.yaml`.** Open **54 ms median**, close **1.5 ms** — and attributed, because a measurement that cannot be attributed is not a measurement (D54): `kubectl version --client` alone is ~25 ms and one API round trip is ~55 ms, so an open *is* one kubectl invocation and rta adds nothing measurable. Not the 5 ms of a local socket, and per-call tunnelling pays it every call. `Forwarding from` does mean traffic flows — the test speaks eight bytes of PostgreSQL through the endpoint and reads the one-byte reply — and after `Close` the same endpoint refuses, so teardown is a fact rather than a call that returned. All three Secret failures are classified against **real refusals**, including an RBAC denial from a ServiceAccount that may `create pods/portforward` and may not `get secrets`, verified with `auth can-i` in both directions — which is what makes "that is a different permission" a claim about the cluster and not about a string. The first end-to-end draft imported `pgx` and `go mod tidy` put a **database driver in the host module's require block**, the exact thing `plugins/pg` being a separate module exists to prevent; it goes through `psql` instead, and the only place that showed was `go.mod`. |
| D57 | **Four output defects, each of which told an operator something untrue.** `internal/tunnel`'s `reap` asked the kernel for the process group instead of using the one rta configured — the exact mistake `internal/pluginhost/procattr_unix.go` documents at length, remade in the package written after it. Both consequences are on the *common* path: every failed open closes its tunnel, by which time the watcher goroutine has usually returned from `Wait`, so `Getpgid` is asked about a released pid — `ESRCH`, or a **stranger's group** if the kernel reused it, which rta then SIGTERMs; and for any command rta did not harden the shared group is rta's own, i.e. the shell that launched rta and every job in it. `Discover` walked a `$PATH` directory once per occurrence, and duplicates are ordinary, so `rta doctor` reported a file as a shadow of itself: *"2 further copy on $PATH not used"*, then one path, twice. `view.ColumnKind` promised cells are *"formatted/aligned"* and nothing has ever formatted one, so `pg.table.list` declared `KindBytes` and printed `1392640`. And a capability that failed did not say its configuration had been ignored. | **The last one is the one worth keeping.** Rebuild a plugin, its digest changes, the section stops applying — ADR 0016's intended behaviour — and what the operator sees next is `localhost:5432 rejected the credentials for "postgres"`. The database really did refuse, and every value in that sentence is a declared default that arrived *because* the section was skipped. The reason sat in `rta doctor`, three commands away, with nothing connecting them. `SetPluginConfig`'s argument for keeping these quiet stands — a stale pin is a fact about an installation and printing it before a command that worked is noise — it simply does not cover failures, which is the one moment the fact explains something. Attached there and nowhere else, onto the `Hint` the plugin already set rather than beside it, because `Hint` is the field every output format carries and a note only pretty output shows is a note an agent never sees. Its cross-namespace mutation **survived** at first: a config whose only section applied produces no problems at all, so matching every problem regardless of namespace passed — caught by offering pg's failure a problem belonging to a different plugin. `internal/format` moved to `pkg/format` in the same pass: its own doc says it exists so producers share one vocabulary, and under `internal` every producer outside this repository was held to the pre-formatted-strings contract with no way to import the words for it. |
| D58 | **PostgreSQL TLS through `kubectl port-forward` kills the forward, and `prefer` is the default.** Not on connect — on the *clean disconnect*. PostgreSQL processes `Terminate` and closes, the TLS layer's trailing `close_notify` arrives at a socket that is already gone, the pod-side read returns `connection reset by peer`, and kubectl reports `error: lost connection to pod` and **exits**. The next call gets `connection refused` on a local port, and nothing anywhere connects the two. Reported as "`pg.table.list` broke my port-forward", and reproducible only against a real CloudNativePG cluster: a `kind` PostgreSQL with no TLS survived 85 back-to-back runs, 600 tables, RST closes and twenty abandoned connections without noticing. | **The first three explanations were wrong, and the measurement is what settled it.** The suspects were rta's own overhead, an abrupt close, and an auth failure — and the matrix says otherwise: `sslmode=disable` **alive**, `prefer` **dead**, `require` **dead**, with the call succeeding in all three. A *failed* auth leaves the forward alive; a *successful* connection kills it, which is the opposite of the guess. The control is what proves ownership: **`psql --sslmode=require` kills the same forward identically** and `--sslmode=disable` does not, so it is the transport rather than rta. What is rta's is that `prefer` is `pg`'s declared default, so it happens on the first call to somebody who changed nothing. The fix costs nothing, which is what makes it a decision rather than a trade: a forward is already inside the API server's TLS for the only hop that leaves the machine, so PostgreSQL-level TLS re-encrypts a loopback socket at both ends. Recorded as ADR 0018 §7 — **a resolved target owns that setting**, because the plugin cannot know it is talking through a forward and rta cannot not know. Until that is wired, `pg.conn.refused` at least asks the right question: "is the server up" is the wrong thing to ask about a port on this machine, and it was the only hint on offer. |
| D59 | **`rta init` deleted the `plugins:` block every time it ran.** Its own summary is "Create or **update** the rta config file interactively", and it assembled a fresh `config.Config` from the wizard's two answers and wrote that — so every per-plugin section an operator had written was gone, silently, in a file that is still perfectly valid afterwards. ADR 0016 added the block; nothing brought the wizard along. Found by asking the plain question "how do I update a plugin config?" and reading the one command that claims to. Second defect in the same function: it read through `config.Load` rather than `config.LoadFile`, whose doc states the rule in as many words — *Load would fold this session's RTA_* into the value, and saving that would bake one shell's environment into the file for every future run* — so `RTA_OUTPUT=json rta init` offered json as the current setting and wrote it permanently, from a variable exported for one command. | **`internal/render/tui/arrange.go` had both right from the start**, and says why: *"the dashboard is one part of the file, and moving a tile must not rewrite anything else — including this shell's RTA_* environment, which is why it re-reads the file rather than the resolved config"*. Two writers, one discipline, held in one of them — the recurring shape (D45, D48, D57). **The test that matters is not about Plugins.** A test asserting the block survives would have been written the day the block was lost and not one day earlier; what was needed is a test that fails when the *next* field is added. So `initConfig` is now a fold over the config as it stands on disk, and a reflective drift test partitions every field of `config.Config` into what the wizard decides (`Output`, `Dashboard`) and what it must carry untouched (`Plugins`) — a field in neither fails the build, verified by adding a `Targets` field and watching it fire. The `Load`/`LoadFile` half needed a second test, because a fold tested directly cannot see which reader fed it: an AST walk asserting **no file both calls `config.Write` and calls `config.Load`**, in the package that states the rule. There are two writers today and the obvious third is a plugin-config editor — the one whose mistake would be an operator's credentials rather than an output format. |
| D60 | **`pg` gains a sixth capability, `pg.overview`, and it stays off the automatic dashboard on purpose.** Asked for directly: "if a db is connected it could show some interesting sections with all the database monitoring infos quickly accessible." It composes the other five — status, replication, cache hit ratio, largest tables, current activity — through the **one connection its own call already opened**, calling each section's view function directly rather than through `plugin.Page.AddAs`, which invokes a full `Handler` and would have opened one connection per section for what is supposed to be a single glance. The obvious next step — let it join the automatic dashboard the way `sys.overview`/`kv.status` do — was not taken: every capability in this plugin reaches off the box, and ADR 0018 §7, found two turns earlier in the same session, measured a **successful** connection killing a `kubectl port-forward` on its own clean disconnect. An unattended five-second timer opening and closing a real connection to a database behind a fragile forward is not hypothetical, it is the exact bug this session had just found. `cap()` now forces `NoPreview` on all six capabilities unconditionally rather than trust each declaration to remember it, and says why in the comment. An operator who has looked at their own setup and decided the cost is fine is not locked out: `dashboard.tiles` runs whatever is named there regardless of `NoPreview`, because naming a capability in a config file *is* the asking — `pkg/plugin.Field`'s own doc for the field says so, and it was already true, just never used for this plugin. Verified live: `dashboard.tiles: [{id: pg.overview}]` against the operator's own homelab Keycloak database shows role, size, active queries and cache hit ratio on the landing screen, refreshed on the same timer every other opted-in tile uses. | **Two found by actually running it against a real cluster with real replication**, not by reasoning about the schema. The `-ro` service load-balanced onto a genuine streaming standby and `replicationView`'s standby branch reported `role: standby, replay lag: 24s` — the primary branch lists `pg_stat_replication`, and a fresh cluster with no connected standby renders as a table with headers and no rows, which is the same honest emptiness `pg.table.list` already gives an empty schema. And `pg.activity`'s query was showing itself: every call to `pg_stat_activity` sees its own backend mid-execution — psql does too — but here it is not a person reading a live terminal, it is a fixed capability whose contract is "what is running", and its own introspection query appearing as the newest active row on every single call is not a session anyone asked to see. Fixed with `pid <> pg_backend_pid()`, in `activityView` itself, so `pg.activity` alone gets the same fix as `pg.overview`'s activity section — the shared function is exactly why one query change reaches both. The cache-ratio verdict (`cacheVerdict`) is pulled out to a plain function of a float for the same reason `usageStatus`/`loadVerdict` are in `builtin/sys`: the boundary is the entire content of the message, and a `>` that should have been a `>=` fails precisely at the number somebody is staring at. The mutation worth catching for `NoPreview` was not "flip it on `pg.overview`'s own literal" — `cap()` overwrites that unconditionally, so the test that only checked the literal passed against the mutation. The one that catches it removes the line from `cap()` itself, which is the realistic way this safety property actually breaks: not one capability's declaration drifting, but the shared enforcement being trusted away. |
| D61 | **The palette became operator-overridable through config, and the styles built from it needed a way to rebuild.** `Key`, `Header`, `Title` and eight others were `var`-block literals derived from `Primary` etc. at package init — before `main` runs, before a config file is ever read — so a var written once could never reflect an override applied afterward. Every style is now a zero-valued var populated by one function, `rebuildStyles`, called once at init and again by `Apply`; the single-copy discipline exists because the first design (derive inline at init, derive again inline inside Apply) is the same seven lines twice, and the two are exactly the pair a later edit updates one of. A second, unrelated type surprise: `lipgloss.Color` is a *constructor* (`func(s string) color.Color`), not a defined type over `string` — the existing `var Primary = lipgloss.Color("#D97757")` had never needed to ask a color what hex built it, and `Current()`/a config editor both do. Fixed by naming every hex as its own const (`primaryHex`, …) and having both the color and the live record built from it, so `Apply` and `Current` never call `lipgloss.Color` without also writing down the string that produced it. `config.Config` gains `Theme map[string]string`, untyped for the reason `Plugins` already is: config stays the leaf package it was, and `rta mcp serve` — which colors nothing — loads it just the same. | **Verified against real output, not just the reflected style.** `#00FF00` in `theme.primary` and a genuine `38;2;0;255;0` in `rta sys overview`'s ANSI, through the whole CLI render path. A malformed value (`bad: not-a-hex`) is reported by `rta doctor` and changes nothing else — every other field applies, the field itself keeps its built-in — mirroring `pluginconf.Resolve`'s "one bad entry costs its own line, not the page." `SetThemeProblems` is `SetPluginConfig`'s own shape reused: not fatal, not printed before a command, reported once by doctor. The mutation worth catching in `Apply` was not "a bad value gets applied anyway" — the obvious one — it was **a second call not resetting what the first one set**: override `primary`, then call `Apply` again naming only `bad`, and `primary` has to come back to the built-in rather than keep leaking the first call's value forever. `rta init`'s own drift test (D59) caught `Theme` the moment it was added to `config.Config` — failed to compile past an unclassified field until `Theme` was named as something the wizard must carry through untouched, exactly the guardrail it was built for one D-entry ago. |
| D62 | **The palette is editable from inside the TUI, `t` from the dashboard, matching the discipline `[`/`]`/`H`/the plugins pane already established: state visible on screen is state changeable from the same screen, written straight through to config.** Purpose-built rather than routed through `capForm` — a color is a hex string with one validator, not a `plugin.Field` carrying `Positional`/`Local`/`Config` semantics that mean nothing to it, and reusing that type would have cost more than the form this took standing alone. Seeded from the config file's own `theme:` section, deliberately not from `theme.Current()`: `Current` always answers every field — the built-in when nothing overrides it — so seeding from it would start every box non-empty, and submitting an untouched form would pin all ten built-ins into the file as though the operator had chosen them, silently outliving any future rta that changes one of those shades. An empty box means "no override"; what it currently resolves to is shown beside it, never written into it. On submit: `config.LoadFile → set Theme → Write`, the same load-modify-write arrange.go's dashboard saves already use, then `theme.Apply` again in this same process — an operator editing colors wants to see the result, not restart rta to learn whether they got it right. | **The first layout was wrong, and driving it through a real pty rather than reading the code is what showed it.** A live preview beside the form, `lipgloss.JoinHorizontal`, one swatch line per field, looked correct read in isolation and was unreadable once actually rendered: a `huh` field is two to four lines depending on how its description wraps, ten of them make a form far taller than ten fixed preview lines, and the join padded the shorter side rather than aligning it — the swatch for `faint` landed next to a description three fields away. Replaced with one compact strip above the form instead of a column beside it, which has no height to disagree about. Mutation-tested: seeding from the live palette instead of disk, `overrides()` writing empty fields, `saveTheme` skipping `theme.Apply` or skipping `config.Write` — each one caught. `keys_test.go`'s `TestEscapeGoesBackFromEveryScreenThatHasABack` gained the theme editor rather than a new assertion beside it — the same universal property, one more screen proving it, which is what a test named for a property rather than a screen is supposed to make cheap. |
| D63 | **A plugin's configuration is editable from inside the TUI — `c` on a row in the plugins pane — closing the gap the previous session left open: the pane could show and hide a tile and had no path to the block driving the connection it showed.** Reuses `capForm` rather than a purpose-built type, unlike the theme editor two entries up: these fields genuinely are `plugin.Field` values collected from a plugin's own capabilities — real `Type`/`Options`/`Min`/`Max`/`Help` already declared — which is exactly the shape `capForm` exists for, and a `Secret` input can never appear among them by construction (ADR 0016 already refuses `Config` on one). `internal/pluginconf` gains `RawSection`, the one deliberate exception to the pin: `For` — the path every capability actually runs with — correctly refuses a stale pin, but an operator who opens this screen to *fix* a stale pin needs to see the values it would have applied, not the declared defaults `For` hands back for the same namespace.
