Migrates on save rather than accumulating: the heading is always the one an operator would have to write by hand — bare for a built-in, pinned to the *currently installed* digest otherwise — never the one `RawSection` read the seed from, and any other heading already naming the namespace is deleted in the same write, so confirming a stale section's values does not leave both the old pin and the new one claiming the same plugin. | **Verified against the exact scenario ADR 0016 exists for**: a fabricated stale pin (`pg@000000000000` against an installed `pg@160a6101928a`), opened through the real TUI over a real pty — the form seeded `stale-host.example`/`9999` from the stale section rather than the declared `localhost`/`5432`, confirming every field unchanged wrote a clean `pg@160a6101928a:` heading with the stale values carried across, and the old heading was gone. `rta doctor`'s "this pin does not match the installed" warning before, `1 configured` clean afterward. Two mutations could not even be *written* as a partial deletion — removing the line that reads `RawSection`'s result, or the line that deletes the stale heading, leaves a now-unused variable and fails the build before a test ever runs, which is a stronger guarantee than the test that catches the same defect expressed as a full deletion including its dependents. |
| D64 | **A generated secret now has a byte-exact clipboard path — `c` on a `gen.password`/`gen.token`/`gen.uuid` result — because the only existing one, `y`, was never built for this.** Reported directly: copying a generated password truncated wherever it landed, worse with special characters or a small terminal. Two real mechanisms, not one: `y` copies the *whole view* as `json.MarshalIndent`, which inserts a literal newline between every field, and most paste targets — a password field, a shell prompt, `kubectl create secret --from-literal` — read a newline as Enter, so the paste stops wherever the JSON structure happened to break first; separately, the full-page result table is rendered through `cli.Render` at whatever width the terminal currently has (`MinWidth`, confirmed by grep, is consumed nowhere but the dashboard tile grid), and `lipgloss/table` wraps a cell too wide for its column rather than truncating it — a password has no spaces to wrap on, so a narrow terminal breaks it mid-string, invisibly, with no "…" to say so. Fixed at the source rather than patched at either symptom: `builtin/kv/clipboard.go`'s subprocess mechanism (stdin only, never argv — kv's own hard-won fix for the same class of bug) moves to `internal/clipboard`, and a new `copySpecs` map in `internal/render/tui` names, per capability, the one column or `KeyValue` pair meant to be copied whole — gen has no store to re-read the way `kv.copy` does (a generated value exists nowhere but the result already on screen), so the value comes straight out of the in-memory view rather than through a re-run. Deliberately scoped to exactly one row: `gen.password --count 5` has no row-selection UI (that requires `capActionSpecs`, which a stateless generator has none of by construction), so "copy" on an ambiguous multi-row result does nothing rather than silently always grabbing the first. | **Mutation-testing the extraction surfaced a real bug, not a planted one.** `Copy`'s success path returned a hardcoded `nil` for the programs that had already failed before one finally worked — invisible to every caller today, since both `kv.copy` and the new action ignore `failed` once `ok` is true, but wrong against the function's own doc comment the moment anything reads it. Caught by a test the original code never had either: "the first installed program fails, the second succeeds" was undocumented behavior repeated verbatim from `kv`'s original comment ("carry on down the list") but never actually exercised end to end. A second test bug on the way there — the new test's own `t.Setenv("PATH", dir)` replaced PATH instead of prepending it, the exact trap `fakeClipboard`'s own comment already named ("a PATH holding nothing but the stand-ins leaves it unable to find `cat`") — walked into once by not reusing the helper closely enough, caught by actually running the test rather than trusting it green. Verified end to end against the real binary: `kv set`/`kv copy` through a stub `pbcopy` still round-trips a value with `!`, `#`, `$` in it byte for byte. |
| D65 | **Correction one turn later: D64's report was about the terminal's own right-click copy, not `y` — and the case D64 deliberately declined (`gen.password --count 5`, more than one row) got a picker instead of staying a dead end.** The visual mechanism was already right there in D64's own investigation (`lipgloss/table` wrapping a spaceless string mid-cell on a narrow terminal) — the correction just confirmed that *is* the one that matters, since a native terminal selection copies exactly what is drawn, wrapped cell and all, and `c` bypasses that rendering path entirely regardless of which bug put the wrong thing on the clipboard. The picker itself was the user's own proposed shape ("an action key with form to choose which of the generated value to copy") — `c` on an ambiguous result now opens a `modeCopyPick`, one `huh.Select` naming every row's raw value, Enter copies whichever is highlighted. Purpose-built rather than a generalization of row navigation: making `gen.password --count 5` row-interactive would mean loosening `interactive()`'s tie to `atTop()`/the actionable-view trail (`enterTrail` only pushes a capability with `capActionSpecs` entries), a shared mechanism several other screens depend on, for a property (row highlighting) this picker does not need at all. `copyPickForm`/`startCopyPick`/`updateCopyPick`/`confirmCopyPick` mirror `themeForm`'s own split exactly, down to the reason: `confirmCopyPick` is separated from `updateCopyPick` the same way `saveTheme` is separated from `updateThemeForm`, so a test can set the bound value directly and call the completion method rather than reverse-engineer which keys `huh.Select` answers to. | **A test written against a shortcut, not the real thing, said the feature was broken when it was the test's shortcut that was.** Driving the form with a bare `Model.Update(tea.KeyPressMsg{...})` and discarding the returned `Cmd` — `startCopyPick` returns `m.copyPick.form.Init()`, needed for huh to reach a state where Enter completes a single-field group — left the form stuck; a real end-to-end run through `teatest`'s actual program loop, which does execute returned commands, showed a single Enter on the freshly opened picker correctly confirms and copies the default choice, "✓ copied value" visible in the very next frame. Kept both: `confirmCopyPick`, called directly with a hand-set value, for the copy logic itself, and one `teatest`-driven test (`TestPressingCThenEnterCopiesTheDefaultChoice`) proving the real keypress path actually reaches it — the shortcut alone would have shipped a picker that opens and then does nothing to a real Enter. The mutation worth catching in `newCopyPickForm` was giving `huh.NewOption` the same string for label and value — passes every test that only checks a *label* renders, and silently copies `"1: qT8!vN2…"` instead of the password itself; caught by asserting the picker's seeded bound value directly rather than the form's rendered text. |
| D66 | **`c` now reaches a tile's own preview directly, without opening it — asked for by name against `gen.overview`, which already had the right shape for it.** Its compact table (`builtin/gen/sample.go`) always names its one generated cell "Value" regardless of which recipe produced it, so `copySpecs["gen.overview"] = {column: "Value"}` was the entire extraction change; the table always carries five recipes, never one, so every press reaches the picker built for D65, never the direct single-value copy. What is new is the dispatch, not the mechanism: `copyOrPick(spec, cap, v, returnTo)` factors the "try the direct copy, fall back to the picker" branch `resultView`'s own `"c"` case already had, and `copyHint` does the same for the footer's label, so `dashFooter` and `resultView` compute the same hint from the same two functions rather than each re-deriving it. The dashboard's `"c"` case reads a tile's own `.view` — already sitting there, refreshed on the normal timer — the same way a result's `"c"` reads `m.result.view`; neither one drives a run to get it. Picking the panel head off `cap`, carried on `copyPickForm` itself, rather than off `m.current`, is what keeps the picker's title correct when opened from a tile: a tile never sets `m.current` (only entering a result through `open()` does), so reading it here would have shown whatever capability the *previous* result happened to be. | Extending `TestEscapeGoesBackFromEveryScreenThatHasABack` (keys_test.go) to the picker one entry back — added when D65 built modeCopyPick, covering only the modeResult-opened case — would have missed a tile-opened picker returning to the wrong screen entirely; a dedicated dashboard-path test (`TestConfirmingATilePickerReturnsToTheDashboardAndRestartsRefresh`) covers what the universal test's own shape does not reach, since a tile-opened picker's `returnTo` is `modeDashboard`, never the `modeResult` every other entry in that test exercises. Verified end to end through the real bubbletea/teatest loop, landing on the real `gen.overview` tile by arrow key from a fresh dashboard, not a synthetic capability standing in for it. |
| D67 | **`shift+enter` accepts whatever is currently bound across a form's remaining fields — capForm, themeForm and the copy picker alike — asked for as "a quick action to validate a form quickly."** Bubbletea v2 requests basic key disambiguation from the terminal by default (confirmed against `tea.KeyboardEnhancements`'s own doc, which names `"shift+enter"` as one of the keys this makes reachable with no further setup), so nothing had to be enabled to make the key arrivable at all — on a terminal that does not support it, it simply never does, and plain enter, field by field, keeps working exactly as before. Deliberately not `Form.NextField`: that sends `nextFieldMsg` straight to the active group, which blurs the focused field (validating) and advances the selector *unconditionally* — nothing checks the result. A real Enter reaches the *field's own* `Update` first, and only emits `NextField` once its own `Validate` passes; sending the same key synthetically (`advanceFormBySyntheticEnter`, form.go) is what keeps that gate, which is the whole reason "quickly submit" cannot mean "skip past whatever a field is currently holding." Proven concretely: a required field left blank stays put and refuses the run; a destructive capability's confirmation is a plain `huh.Confirm` no different from any other field, and `Confirm.Update` never touches its bound value except on an explicit Toggle/Accept/Reject — a Confirm nobody answered stays at capForm's own seeded `false` (declined), so racing through it declines the same way leaving it alone would. A two-stage prefill form stops at the boundary on a more structural basis than a design choice enforced after the fact: `afterFormUpdate` does not even build the second stage's `capForm` until the first completes, so there is nothing of it yet for one call to have advanced through. | **Two engine-level facts, found the hard way, are why this is not a plain `for` loop calling `*huh.Form.Update`.** First: huh's own field-to-field and group-to-group advance is not synchronous within one `Update` call — submitting a field returns a Cmd (`NextField`) whose *message*, fed back through `Update`, is what actually moves the selector; clearing the *group's* last field needs a second such round (`nextGroupMsg`) before the form's own `State` changes at all. A tight loop that calls `Update` and immediately loops again, batching every returned Cmd without ever running one, never lets that second step happen — early attempts read as "stuck" (state never left Normal) when nothing had actually settled yet. `settleForm` (form.go) drains that cascade itself, synchronously, the same settling a real `tea.Program` gives every message for free. Getting the ordering right at Program level first (routing through a self-rescheduling `fastSubmitMsg` and letting bubbletea's own engine settle each step) looked correct and was not: `tea.Batch` runs its commands on goroutines with no ordering guarantee between them, so a rescheduled next-step message could reach `Update` *before* the previous step's own cascade had — a two-stage form's boundary held on some runs and silently ran straight through both stages on others, confirmed by forcing the ordering with `tea.Sequence` (which fixed one interleaving) and then by direct timing instrumentation once a *different* order-independent bug remained. Second: a focused field's cursor blinks by design forever — `Focus` starts a Cmd that reschedules itself on every tick — and `settleForm`'s first version waited on it like any other Cmd, since `cursor.Blink`'s actual wait happens inside a fresh closure over a new context every call, not a stable, comparable function value there was any way to recognize in advance and skip. `resolveCmd` races every Cmd against a 20ms budget instead (measured against this exact form: real validation and selector work lands in microseconds, several orders of magnitude inside that margin) and treats a Cmd still running past it as a timer, not a step to wait for — cutting one theme-form save from 4.9s to 0.3s and a would-be-perpetual hang to a bounded, correct one. Both mistakes were caught by running the real thing rather than trusting a mental model of it: a lightweight direct-`Update` unit test could not have shown either, since both are specifically about what happens *between* two `Update` calls, not what either one returns alone. |
| D68 | **`eol` is the second external plugin, built out of catalogue order.** One capability, `eol.check <product> [cycle] [--warn-days]` (§7, Wave 3) — chosen ahead of §11 M3's own named targets (`s3`, `vault`) because it is the cheapest possible next proof of the third-party-authoring path itself: no connection, no credential, no config section, no live infrastructure to stand up, against a plugin the catalogue already called "the ideal 'your first plugin' tutorial subject" before it existed. Fetches a product's full release list once and grades/filters client-side rather than calling the API's separate per-cycle endpoint, so a missing cycle's error can name every cycle that does exist and the no-cycle case ("show everything") costs nothing extra. Aliases resolve for free — `eol check postgres` and `eol check postgresql` name the same product — because endoflife.date 301s one to the other and `http.Client` follows redirects by default; found by testing the real API before writing a client-side alias table nothing needed. | Closes a different gap than `vault`/`s3` would: not the untested `Write`+`NeedsGrant` path (still open — every `pg` capability and this one are `Read`), but whether the scaffold-to-shipped path (`rta plugin new` → `dev` → real logic → tests → install) is actually the fifteen-minute experience M2's gate claims, exercised with something that has real logic rather than the literal hello-plugin stub. It was, start to finish. `check` trips `sdktest`'s verb-vocabulary warning (D8) and was kept anyway — `cert.expiry` set the precedent that a graded, threshold-based check earns a domain word over `get`/`status`/`inspect`, and the catalogue's own example command already committed to this exact word (§7). Testable two levels deep without a network call in CI, mirroring `builtin/audit`'s `queryOSV`/`queryOSVAt` split: `fetchProduct(ctx, client, base, product)` isolates the HTTP/JSON/error-classification layer, and `runCheck`/`runCheckAt` does the same for the whole capability, so an `httptest.Server` answering wrongly on purpose covers the field-wiring from `plugin.Request` through to the rendered `Table` — coverage `pg`'s own query path does not have, for lack of an equivalently cheap way to fake a wire protocol instead of plain JSON. |
| D69 | **`eol.check`'s LTS column trusts `isLts` over recomputing it from `ltsFrom`.** The two can disagree in a real, common way: `nodejs` releases spend time in a "Current" phase before graduating to LTS, so a cycle can carry a *future* `ltsFrom` while `isLts` is still `false` — found by curling the real API rather than assumed from the field names. `ltsCell` (`plugins/eol/main.go`) reports `yes` on the flag, the scheduled date when only `ltsFrom` is set, and `-` when neither is — three states, not the two a bare boolean column would show. | Same call `eolStatus` already makes for `isEol` (D68): endoflife.date's whole purpose is having answered this correctly, so recomputing it from a raw date would be answering a question the API already answered — and would get `nodejs 26` wrong in the direction that matters, reporting a release LTS before it actually is. |
| D70 | **`debug` and `keys` reclassified from Wave 3 (external) to Wave 1 (built-in).** Neither needed to be external in the first place. `debug` (`debug.ansi`, absorbing `sequin`) needs no credential and no live service. `keys` (absorbing `melt`, not yet built) touches `~/.ssh` directly, and `internal/pluginhost/denyset.go`'s `tier2` denies a confined plugin *read* access there on every platform the sandbox actually covers — deliberately, per the function's own comment, which already considered and rejected a carve-out for "a plugin that legitimately runs `ssh-keygen`... on the user's behalf." §4.5 states the same thing from the other direction: a plugin is never handed a raw file path, only a declared `Secret` resolved from one restricted environment variable — nothing covers "read this file the host can read and you cannot." `kv --identity` is the concrete precedent already in the tree: it reads `~/.ssh/id_ed25519` today, built-in, with the same ambient filesystem access the person running rta already has. | Wave 3's "community-shaped" label carried no stated technical requirement to be external — checked directly, there is no prose under that heading, only a table — so this is the catalogue getting revisited as each entry is actually built, the same thing that happened to `fs.tree` before the M2 freeze. Confinement is macOS-only today (`internal/pluginhost/confine_other.go`: Linux/Windows run plugins unconfined, Landlock deliberately dropped rather than shipped half-working), which sharpens the case rather than weakening it: an external `keys` would work by accident on two platforms and fail — or need a special exemption from a deny set that already reasoned about and rejected exactly this exemption — on the one platform where confinement is real. |
| D71 | **`ansi.Parser.Data()` includes the OSC command number `Command()` also parsed — undocumented, confirmed by reading the vendored source.** `parser.go`'s `parseStringCmd` reads leading ASCII digits out of the collected data to build `Command()` but never removes them from `Data()`. `debug.ansi`'s first OSC test run showed it directly: `\x1b]0;my title\x07` explained as `set window/icon title: 0;my title`, not `...: my title`. `oscPayload` (`builtin/debug/ansi.go`) strips the same digits-then-one-semicolon prefix `parseStringCmd` itself scans for, with a comment naming the exact function so a future reader checks it against whatever version of the dependency is current rather than trusting this note forever. | The second reason in one file to verify a vendored API against its actual source rather than its doc comment: `DecodeSequence`'s own example is stale in an unrelated way, showing a two-argument `NewParser(32, 1024)` against the current zero-argument signature — caught before it shipped by checking `go doc -src` rather than copying the example, the same discipline that caught this one after a test already failed. |
| D72 | **`keys` ships as the ed25519-only SSH-key ↔ BIP39-words built-in, absorbing `melt`'s idea rather than its module.** Three capabilities: `keys.list` (Read, `NoPreview` — `describeKey` may parse an unencrypted key's raw bytes to answer "what type is this" when there is no `.pub` sibling, and that should not happen unasked on a five-second dashboard tick), `keys.backup` and `keys.restore` (both Write, both refuse `SurfaceMCP` outright — the `grant.allow`/`grant.revoke` precedent, not merely `NeedsGrant`: once melted, the words work forever with no revocation and no per-call log, the same reasoning `share.secret.set/get` (§7, Wave 3) will use when it is built). `melt.go` itself is a twenty-line wrapper over `github.com/tyler-smith/go-bip39`, so this package calls go-bip39 directly rather than adding a dependency on a dependency — the same call `debug` made absorbing `sequin` as `charmbracelet/x/ansi` rather than as `sequin` itself. The SSH-specific half (parsing/marshaling an OpenSSH private key, prompting for its passphrase) mirrors `builtin/kv/crypt.go`'s already-established pattern rather than melt's own `cmd/melt/main.go`, which is unexported and CLI-only. Verbs are `backup`/`restore`, not melt's own `melt`/`restore` — matching `pg`'s stated (if not yet built) `backup`/`restore` pair rather than requiring the catalogue's reader to already know what "melt" means. | Determinism is provable, not assumed, and turned out to be narrower than first written down: an ed25519 private key is entirely derived from its 32-byte seed, so `keys.restore` reproduces cryptographically identical key material — same fingerprint, always, verified against real `ssh-keygen -lf` output and not just this package's own round trip (`TestBackupRestoreRoundTrip`). The on-disk PEM is not byte-identical, though: OpenSSH's container writes a random per-encode nonce alongside the key material every time, caught live running the real binary against a real key before a "bit-for-bit" claim shipped in `keys.restore`'s own Description — `TestRestoredKeyMaterialIsIdenticalButThePemBytesAreNot` now pins both halves so neither claim can silently become false. |
| D73 | **A four-dimension review (crypto correctness, security exposure, convention consistency, test coverage), each finding adversarially verified before being accepted, found five real defects in `keys` before it shipped — none of them hypothetical.** (1) `keys.backup`'s `--passphrase` (unlocks the key being read) and `keys.restore`'s `--passphrase` (would have locked the key being written) resolved from the identical `RTA_KEYS_PASSPHRASE`, since `plugin.LocalEnvVar` scopes by namespace only, not by capability — an operator scripting a backup with the env var set would have gotten a silently-encrypted restore later in the same shell, contradicting the field's own "omit for none" promise. Fixed by renaming the restore field to `new-passphrase`, a distinct env var, rather than teaching `LocalEnvVar` a per-capability scope nothing else needs. (2) `fromMnemonic` forwarded go-bip39's own error text verbatim on an unrecognised word — `` word `adandon` not found in reverse map `` — leaking one word of a twenty-four-word private key into ordinary CLI/JSON error output, directly against `runBackup`'s own warning that these words must never go anywhere the key file itself would not. Fixed by keeping only the two fixed-text sentinel errors (`ErrInvalidMnemonic`, `ErrChecksumIncorrect`) and replacing everything else, including that one, with a message that never echoes input. (3) `unlockKey` prompted up to three times for a locked key's real passphrase before checking whether the algorithm was even eligible for word-based backup — wasting a passphrase entry on an RSA or ECDSA key rejected afterward regardless. Fixed by reading the type off `PassphraseMissingError.PublicKey`, which the OpenSSH container carries in cleartext regardless of encryption, before the prompt loop rather than after it. (4) The same mechanism applied to `keys.list`: a locked key with no `.pub` sibling used to report `type=unknown` even though that type was recoverable with zero decryption — `probeKey` now answers both `describeKey` and `unlockKey` from one function. (5) A corrupt `.pub` sibling left a perfectly good, unencrypted key reported as unknown, since the private-key fallback only ran when `.pub` was entirely absent, not merely unparseable — `describeKey` now falls back whenever `.pub` fails to answer, corrupt or missing alike. | Ten more findings survived scrutiny too, all fixed: operator-facing recovery text ("move the stray .pub aside and restore again") that could not actually be followed, since the private key it referred to was already durably written by that point; six test-coverage gaps — the entire interactive word-prompt path, a locked-RSA-key path, an unparseable-key path, and `publishRestoredKey`'s own race-detection branches — invisible to `go test` despite being real, reachable code, confirmed by coverage-profile inspection rather than by grepping test names alone; a stale doc comment describing a pointer-vs-value key-shape distinction as encrypted-vs-unencrypted when it is actually OpenSSH-format-vs-PKCS8-format. Not one finding was accepted on the reviewing agents' word alone — every one was independently reproduced against the real code (a standalone Go program against the pinned `golang.org/x/crypto` version, a coverage profile, or a temporary throwaway test) before a fix was written. |
| D74 | **The same review discipline turned on existing, already-shipped code — `builtin/kv` and the grant system (`internal/grant`, `builtin/grant`, `internal/mcp/bridge.go`) — across eight parallel dimensions with adversarial verification, and found real, live defects rather than a clean bill of health.** The headline: `kv.env`'s `--prefix` was concatenated into the `export`/dotenv output with no filtering, unlike `key` (character-whitelisted) and `value` (shell-quoted) — a newline in `--prefix` broke the output into extra lines, one of which could be a live command substitution, a direct hit against the capability's own documented `eval "$(rta kv env …)"` usage, and reachable from an MCP caller holding only a per-key grant. `envName` now filters the whole combined name together, not each half separately. Four more real bugs, not just gaps: (1) a locked SSH key's own multi-key age identity file (`age-keygen >> identities.txt`, age's documented convention) only ever had its first `AGE-SECRET-KEY-` line read — `parseIdentity` → `parseIdentities`, trying every key in the file for decrypt and adding every one as a recipient on write; (2) `parseRecipient`'s fallback error echoed up to 32 characters of a mistakenly-pasted private key verbatim — `redactedSpec` refuses to echo anything `isPrivateKey` recognises; (3) `Unlockable()` trusted a stale or typo'd `RTA_KV_IDENTITY` without checking the path exists, unlike its sibling `LockedIdentity()` two functions below it — `rta doctor` could report an MCP agent's environment as able to decrypt the store when a real call would fail outright; (4) `internal/grant`'s `scopes()` neither deduplicated a repeated record name (a `--max-uses 1` grant could authorize itself twice within one call naming the same key twice) nor rendered an `Int`-typed scope the way an operator types it (`fmt.Sprint(float64(1000000))` is `"1e+06"`, demonstrated — not just asserted — to never match a grant issued for the plain string) — both fixed in `scopes()` itself, the one function every grant check and spend goes through. | The architectural fix underneath all of it: every `Local` field used to resolve from `RTA_<NS>_<FIELD>` unconditionally, which is right for a credential and wrong for a field that only chooses a destination — `kv.get`'s own `--out` is `Local` specifically so a grant on `kv.get` cannot be read as "and write the value wherever you like," and an ambient `RTA_KV_OUT` in the server's own environment defeated that exactly as quietly as an explicit MCP argument would have, reproduced end to end against the real bridge. `Field.EnvFallback` (`pkg/plugin`) makes this opt-in per field rather than automatic for every `Local` one — carried across the wire protocol (`proto/rta/v1/plugin.proto` field 13, non-breaking per `buf breaking`) so external plugins get the same fix, not just built-ins. Set on `kv`'s `passphrase`/`identity`, `keys.backup`'s `passphrase`, and `pg`'s `password` (genuine credentials); left unset on `kv.get`'s `out`, `kv.set`'s `file`, `kv.init`/`kv.rekey`'s `recipient`, and deliberately not restored on `keys.restore`'s `new-passphrase` either, since a restore is a one-off action, not a standing session credential. A second, independent gap closed the same way — panic rather than silent bypass: `pkg/plugin.Page` composes a sibling capability's `Handler` directly, with none of the checks `internal/mcp/bridge.go`'s `grant.Reserve` applies to a capability an MCP call actually names. Nothing in the registry embeds a Write or Destructive handler today, but nothing stopped a future one either — `Page.Add`/`AddAs`/`Run` now require the caller to state the safety class and panic on anything but `Read`, turning "nothing currently does this" into "nothing ever can," at the cost of one literal argument at each of the ten real call sites. `saveTo`'s two-file, non-atomic commit (the store re-encrypts, then `kv.recipients` records who to — separately) got a narrower fix: the error, when only the second write fails, now says outright that the store *was* re-encrypted and `kv.recipients` may be stale, since `rta kv recipients` itself has no way to detect the mismatch on a plain read. Coverage gaps closed alongside the bug fixes, each guarding something that actually matters: `checkPaths`' defensive non-string branch (the last line standing between an MCP caller and an unconfined path if the ordering ahead of it ever changed), `Reserve`'s fail-closed behavior under a corrupt grant file (the sole chokepoint every gated MCP call passes through), and `backupUnstamped` actually producing its recovery copy end to end, byte for byte — its own doc comment calls this "the one failure this package must not have," and no test had ever exercised it past the early-return. Left open, and named rather than silently dropped: `Reserve`'s fast-path skip-the-lock decision is made from a stale, unlocked snapshot, so a `MaxUses` grant created in the narrow window between that snapshot and the authorization check could authorize without spending — real, reproduced, but a window measured in microseconds against a human-driven operation that normally takes milliseconds, deferred rather than rushed; `builtin/kv/mcp_test.go` end-to-end-tests five of thirteen capabilities through the real bridge, leaving `kv.rekey`/`kv.init`'s already-flagged (ADR 0014) `StringSlice`-typed `recipient` field and `kv.rm`'s Destructive+grant gate unverified at that layer; `publicHalf`'s age-native-identity branch (`--recipient` pointed at an externally-supplied, non-`kv`-generated age identity file) remains reachable and untested. |
| D75 | **A report that `shift+enter` fast-submit (D67) "is not available on every form" in the TUI turned out to name a real coverage gap, not a real bug — checked by adversarial re-derivation plus live reproduction, not by re-reading the same code more carefully.** Two independent agents re-traced `internal/render/tui/tui.go`'s dispatch from scratch (every `mode` value, every `m.mode = modeForm` call site, the plugin-config editor's `configTarget` branch in `afterFormUpdate`) and found no missing case, no early intercept, and no second `Update` loop anywhere in the repo — `rta init`'s own wizard (`internal/app/init.go`) is the only huh.Form outside this package's loop, and the report explicitly excluded it. That left field *shape* as the only place a real gap could still hide, since `internal/render/tui/fastsubmit_test.go` had only ever driven a String/Int-only form, a bare Destructive confirm, and a Prefill boundary through the synthetic-Enter path — never a `Bool`, an `Options`-driven Select or MultiSelect, a `Secret`, a `Path`, a multi-line `Text` field, a plain `Write`-safety capability, a Destructive capability with real `Inputs` ahead of its confirm field, or the plugin-config editor itself. Each was driven through the real bubbletea event loop via `teatest`, not just read — the one genuine risk in the set, `Text` (`todo.add`/`note.add`'s own multi-line body field, where Enter is a newline in most editors), holds up: huh's own `TextKeyMap` reserves plain `enter` for Next/Submit and moves the newline binding to `alt+enter`/`ctrl+j` (`charm.land/huh/v2`'s `keymap.go`), confirmed against the running form rather than the keymap table alone. Nine new tests (`internal/render/tui/fastsubmit_fieldtypes_test.go`) plus one exercising the plugin-config editor end to end (`pluginconfig_test.go`'s `TestShiftEnterSavesThePluginConfigForm`, asserting the saved config file on disk, not just an on-screen flash) all passed on the first correctly-written attempt — the only failure along the way was a test-authoring mistake (assuming a Destructive form with real `Inputs` stops mid-form at the confirm prompt; it does not — one `shift+enter` races clean through, same as a bare-Confirm form, since `huh.Confirm` submits on plain Enter whether or not it was ever explicitly toggled), not an application bug. | Why bother proving a negative this thoroughly rather than taking the static read as settled: a worktree-isolated probe agent (meant to run the field-type sweep independently and in parallel) failed outright — this repo is not recognized as a git-worktree host by the workflow tooling — so the one stage most likely to catch a live-only failure (a hang, a panic, a cascade that never settles) had to be redone directly rather than trusted from a report. Recorded as coverage closed, not a bug fixed: nothing in `advanceFormBySyntheticEnter`/`settleForm` (`form.go`) changed. |

| D76 | **D75 cleared the code; the report was real anyway — traced to a live VS Code conflict, not a terminal capability gap, and closed with a second, independent way into fast-submit rather than a config change.** The user's own terminal (VS Code, `1.133.0`) already has the Kitty keyboard protocol available; turning on `terminal.integrated.enableKittyKeyboardProtocol` and reloading changed nothing, which ruled out the obvious "needs a setting" explanation. A raw-byte capture (`stty -icanon -echo; cat -v`) showed `shift+enter` producing a bare `^[` — never a `[13;2u` CSI sequence — and `~/Library/Application Support/Code/User/keybindings.json` (read directly, not guessed at) named the reason: a `workbench.action.terminal.sendSequence` binding on `shift+enter`, installed by Claude Code's own `/terminal-setup`, that unconditionally injects literal `ESC`+`CR` whenever the terminal has focus — pre-empting VS Code's native key reporting regardless of the protocol setting, for any program running in that terminal, not just Claude Code. Reassigning that binding to a free key (`alt+enter`, itself Claude Code's own documented Option+Enter convention on other terminals) fixes it, but only for the one machine that gets reconfigured — not for every other person hitting the identical, structural VS-Code-plus-Claude-Code combination. Read genuinely: bubbletea's own input decoder (`github.com/charmbracelet/ultraviolet`, `decoder.go`'s two-byte-ESC-prefix case, `key.go`'s `KeyEnter = rune(ansi.CR)`) already parses `ESC`+`CR` as one real `alt+enter` key event — the universal "meta sends escape" convention every terminal speaks with no protocol negotiation at all, proven byte-for-byte in `TestAltEnterIsWhatEscThenCarriageReturnDecodesTo` rather than taken from the source read alone. `rta` simply never listened for it. `bindFastSubmit` (`internal/render/tui/keys.go`) now answers to `alt+enter` alongside `shift+enter`, wired at the three real dispatch sites (`tui.go`'s `modeForm`/`modeTheme`/`modeCopyPick` cases) the same way `enter`/`tab` already share one case elsewhere in the same switch. Reproduced end to end against the real, externally-loaded `eol` plugin from the same investigation D75 used to prove the opposite claim: typed `python` into `eol.check`'s form, sent the exact key VS Code now produces, got the real endoflife.date result table. | Fixing this in `rta` rather than only telling the user to edit their keybindings is the point: nobody using Claude Code inside VS Code's integrated terminal — a genuinely common pairing — should have to know this conflict exists, let alone resolve it by hand, before a `shift+enter` shortcut in *any* other terminal app works. `alt+enter` costs nothing to support alongside the primary binding: no plain `enter` ever carries `ModAlt`, so it can never fire from an ordinary keypress, and the existing fallback (plain `enter`, field by field) still works exactly as before on any terminal where neither key arrives distinctly. Left exactly where D75 left it — nothing in `advanceFormBySyntheticEnter`/`settleForm` changed, because the gap was never there. A pre-existing, unrelated test failure surfaced by this session's own verification run (`TestResultFooterOffersRerunAndCopy`, `internal/render/tui/tui_test.go` — a footer hint truncating mid-word under `-race`) was confirmed present on a clean stash of this repo before any of D75/D76's work and left untouched rather than folded into an unrelated fix; named here so it is not lost. |

| D77 | **`TestResultFooterOffersRerunAndCopy`'s failure, named rather than fixed at the end of D76, traced to an empty `$TERM` in this session's own sandbox — not a bug in anything this package renders.** `resultView()`'s own returned string, inspected directly rather than through the terminal, is correct byte-for-byte in every case (`r re-run · ↑↓ scroll · y copy json · esc back · q quit`, no exceptions) — proving the corruption seen on screen (`copy jso`, cut off mid-word around a stray `[4h`/`[4l` pair) happens after `rta`'s own code is done, inside the renderer it hands that string to. `charmbracelet/ultraviolet`'s `TerminalRenderer` picks its capabilities from `$TERM` (`xtermCaps`, `terminal_renderer.go`): an unrecognized or empty value gets zero capabilities, including `capICH`, which routes every line-insert through an `ansi.SetModeInsertReplace`/`ansi.ResetModeInsertReplace` (IRM) fallback instead of the direct `ansi.InsertCharacter` used everywhere else — and that fallback path is where the corruption actually happens. Confirmed by bisection, not guessed: `TERM=xterm-256color go test ./internal/render/tui/...` passes the same test instantly and consistently; unsetting `$TERM` again reproduces the failure on demand, in isolation, every time. Bumping `charm.land/bubbletea/v2` (`v2.0.8` → `v2.0.9`, reverted) and `github.com/charmbracelet/ultraviolet` to its latest available commit (reverted) changed nothing — this is not a version already fixed upstream. | No real terminal leaves `$TERM` unset — every terminal `rta` is meant to run in exports one, so this was never reachable outside a sandbox or CI runner that starts a test binary with an empty environment, which is exactly what this session's own tool sandbox does. That makes it a real bug in this package's *test suite*, not in `rta`: any environment sharing that one property would see the identical false failure on a test that has nothing to do with terminal capability negotiation, and would have no way to tell "the renderer's IRM fallback has a real bug" from "this sandbox forgot to export a variable" without doing the same bisection. Fixed with a `TestMain` (`internal/render/tui/tui_test.go`) that forces `TERM=xterm-256color` once for the whole test binary — the only representative choice, since it is what makes every test in this package describe the terminal `rta` actually runs in rather than whatever the ambient environment happened to leave set. The IRM fallback path itself may still have a genuine bug worth reporting upstream to `charmbracelet/ultraviolet` — silently truncating output is worse than the `repaintLine` fallback the same file already uses for a related class of diff-unsafe line — but that is this package's problem to route around, not `rta`'s to carry a workaround for; left for whoever hits it against a real `$TERM=dumb`-style terminal, not chased further here. |

| D78 | **The three coverage gaps D74 named and deferred — `kv`'s MCP bridge tests covering five of thirteen capabilities, and `publicHalf`'s untested age-native-identity branch — closed, all with teeth proven rather than assumed.** `kv.init`/`kv.rekey`'s `recipient` field is `Local` for a reason distinct from every other `Local` field this package has (`kv.get`'s `out`, `kv.set`'s `file`): `parseRecipient` accepts a *path* and reads it, so a `StringSlice` reaches the filesystem with nothing the path-confinement gate can hook (`Field.Path`-only), and ADR 0014 recorded that as a known, accepted gap rather than a bug. `TestInitRecipientCannotBeSuppliedOverMCP`/`TestRekeyRecipientCannotBeSuppliedOverMCP` drive the real bridge to prove the mitigation actually holds: the field is absent from both tool schemas, and a sneaked `"recipient"` argument naming an unrelated key is silently dropped before the handler ever sees it — `kv.rekey`'s case adding a `Destructive`-safety, `AllowDestructive`-gated call and an already-locked store to the scenario, since a fresh, empty one would not have exercised the "existing readers survive" half of the same property. `TestRmNeedsAGrantForTheKeyItRemoves` closes the other named gap the same way `kv.set`'s overwrite and `kv.rename`'s move were already proven at this layer: refused without a grant (the entry survives), goes through once one is issued for that specific key — through `AllowDestructive` rather than `AllowWrite`, the exposure list D74 flagged as unverified for this capability specifically. `TestParseRecipientDerivesTheRecipientFromAPathToARawAgeIdentity` (`builtin/kv/kv_test.go`) closes the last one: a `--recipient` path whose own content is a raw age identity — not one `kv` generated, and deliberately with no `.pub` sibling file, which is what proves `publicHalf`'s `AGE-SECRET-KEY-` branch ran rather than its `.pub`-lookup neighbor three lines below it — derives the recipient directly, exactly as `kv.rekey`'s own `--recipient` help text already documented ("a path to one — including a private key, whose public half is all that is read"). | Every one of the four new tests was proven capable of failing, not just observed to pass: each protection was independently, temporarily disabled (the `Local`-field drop in `internal/mcp/bridge.go`, `internal/grant.Required`'s `Destructive` clause, `publicHalf`'s `AGE-SECRET-KEY-` branch) and the corresponding test re-run to confirm it caught the regression, then reverted — the same discipline this project has applied to every fix since D67, now applied to coverage added with nothing to fix underneath it. Nothing in `builtin/kv`'s non-test code changed; this closes exactly the three items D74's own "why" column named rather than dropped, and no others — `internal/grant.Reserve`'s fast-path TOCTOU race is still open, on purpose, unrelated to any of this. |

| D79 | **`git` (§7.5) shipped: `git.overview`/`status`/`log`/`diff`/`branches`/`blame`, all Read, plus a design point §7.5 never anticipated — every capability accepts a remote URL in `path` alongside a local checkout, cloned entirely in memory for the one call.** The two are told apart by reusing `go-git`'s own endpoint parser (`transport.NewEndpoint`, classifying a bare path as protocol `"file"` and anything else as remote) rather than a hand-rolled prefix check, so "does this look like a URL" is answered the same way the `git` binary itself would answer it — proven, not asserted: a network-independent test drives a deliberately unreachable `https://127.0.0.1:1/...` through `openRepo` and asserts the *specific* failure code that only fires once routing reached the clone path, and a separate test proves the cloned `*git.Repository` is fully usable afterward (real commit, real message) against `go-git`'s own local "file" transport client — the identical code path a genuine network host goes through, exercised with zero network dependency. `git.diff` is scoped to two cases, not a general revision-range comparison: uncommitted changes against HEAD, and one commit against its parent. The first has no library support at all (`go-git` ships no working-tree diff) and is built from primitives the library already trusts internally — its own Myers line-differ (`utils/diff.Do`, the same algorithm `object.Commit.Patch` uses) and its own unified-diff encoder (`plumbing/format/diff.NewUnifiedEncoder`), driving both through four small interfaces (`Patch`/`FilePatch`/`File`/`Chunk`) this package implements itself rather than hand-formatting `diff --git`/`@@` text — the fiddly, correctness-sensitive part every other diff library wraps for exactly this reason. Every non-trivial claim about the dependency's own behavior was checked against its actual vendored source before code was written against it (matching the D71/D72 precedent), not assumed from an initial research pass that had already flagged two real discrepancies between two documentation lookups (`blame.Line`'s field names) and one unconfirmed claim (`CommitIter`'s early-stop convention) — both resolved by reading `$(go env GOMODCACHE)/.../go-git/v5@.../*.go` directly. | Two real bugs found building this, both fixed before shipping. (1) `PlainOpenWithOptions{DetectDotGit: true}` — the option every capability's local-path case used, to work from any subdirectory of a checkout the way the real `git` binary does — refuses a real, valid **bare** repository outright (`repository does not exist`), because `DetectDotGit` walks upward looking for a `.git` entry a bare repository never has: the path itself already is the git directory, not a checkout holding one. Caught building the remote-clone test fixture, not anticipated — confirmed against the vendored source with a four-line reproduction before touching `openRepo`'s own code, and fixed with a fallback to plain `PlainOpen` (no walking, exact-path only) once the walking one fails, regression-tested against a real bare repository built and pushed to on disk. (2) `git.diff`'s worktree case sends every changed path through `godiff.Do` even when either side of it is binary content, which is not what git itself does (`Binary files a and b differ`, no line-level diff attempted) — checked for explicitly (`isBinary`, a NUL-byte heuristic matching git's own) before this was ever exercised on binary content, not discovered by one being fed through it. SSRF-shaped risk was considered and deliberately left where `http`/`net` already leave it: neither of those plugins — whose entire purpose is reaching a caller-named host — restricts private/link-local IP ranges today, and inventing a bespoke restriction only for `git` would be a narrower, inconsistent policy rather than a more secure one; a repository-wide answer, if one is wanted, is a separate decision affecting all three plugins alike, named here rather than solved unilaterally. Authentication is out of scope for this cut on the same "state the limitation, do not chase it" basis — only unauthenticated (public) URLs clone; a private repository fails with a clear, immediate reason instead of hanging on a credential prompt nobody is there to answer. |
| D80 | **`git.config` and `git.hooks` shipped, both Read; branch switching (`git.checkout`/`git.switch`) deliberately not, named here rather than dropped silently.** `git.config` reads system, global and local scope through `go-git`'s own `config.LoadConfig`/`Repository.Config`, one row per scope a key is set in — the same shape `git config --list --show-origin` uses, not one pre-flattened "effective value" that hides which file it came from. `git.hooks` lists the hooks directory and classifies each entry the way `git` itself decides whether one fires: a `.sample` suffix never runs regardless of permissions, and a correctly-named file that isn't executable is silently skipped by real `git` too, reported here as `disabled` rather than `active`. Branch switching would be this plugin's first **Write** capability — the rest is uniformly Read — and a bad switch can discard uncommitted work outright, a materially different risk than anything shipped so far; it also cannot mean anything against a remote `path`, since that's cloned fresh into memory and thrown away at the end of the one call, nothing persists to switch. Scoping it out now rather than folding it in half-designed keeps the safety-class boundary this plugin has held since D79 (all Read) intact until checkout gets the same deliberate design pass diff and clone did. | The routing between "hooks require an on-disk repository" and "config works for either" was checked against source, not assumed symmetric: `Repository.Storer` is `*filesystem.Storage` for anything opened from disk and `*memory.Storage` for a `cloneRepo` clone, and only the former exposes a real `billy.Filesystem` a hooks directory could live on — `hooksFilesystem` type-asserts on exactly that and refuses the memory case with a hint, extracted as its own function specifically so the refusal could be driven by a real, working memory-backed repository (via `cloneRepo` against a local bare-repo fixture, D79's precedent) rather than merely asserted never to be reached. Two library behaviors were confirmed rather than guessed: `storage/filesystem/dotgit.RepositoryFilesystem.ReadDir` already routes `hooks/` through the correct backing filesystem for bare repositories, worktrees and `GIT_COMMON_DIR` alike (read directly from its `mapToRepositoryFsByPath` source), so hooks needed no manual path-joining of its own; and `config.LoadConfig` returns an empty `Config`, never an error, when a scope's file doesn't exist on the machine (no `~/.gitconfig`, no `/etc/gitconfig`) — so system/global scope is only ever absent rows, not a failure case `git.config` has to special-case. One real, stated limitation: `[include]`/`[includeIf]` directives are shown as the literal keys they are, not resolved into the file they point at — `go-git`'s own `Config` type keeps `Includes` as a separate, unflattened field, and following them is a distinct feature this cut doesn't attempt. |
| D81 | **`internal/grant.Reserve`'s TOCTOU race — left open on purpose at D74, "real, reproduced" and re-confirmed still open at D78 — closed.** The fast path (taken when the unlocked snapshot shows no `MaxUses`-limited grant at all, the overwhelming common case) called `Check(c, values)` to decide authorization, and `Check` took its own independent, unlocked `Load()` rather than reusing the snapshot already in hand. A `MaxUses` grant created in the gap between those two reads was invisible to the first read (so nothing about the call looked like it needed the lock or a spend) and visible to the second (so the call was authorized anyway) — a one-time grant delivered its secret for free, with `grant list` showing it still unspent afterward. Fixed by reusing the one already-loaded snapshot for both decisions — `checkAgainst(c, values, grants)` in place of `Check(c, values)` — so there is structurally only one read left to race against, not two. Priority followed the standing rule directly: this was the one concretely-named, still-open item of the "bugs and security before features" kind on the board, found by re-reading D74/D78 rather than by a fresh audit. | The fix is provably correct by construction (one snapshot, reused, cannot disagree with itself) rather than merely tested, but it was tested anyway: a 150-trial concurrent test races a `Reserve` call against a grant landing mid-flight with no coordination between them, and — because the real window is a few microseconds, too narrow to land reliably on its own — widens it deterministically by padding the grants file with 4,000 unrelated entries first, so `Load`'s own cost (unseal, parse, filter, sort) stretches the gap past ordinary goroutine start jitter without changing what is being raced. Teeth proven the standard way: reverted to the old `Check(c, values)` call, the test failed reliably within 6-20 of its 150 trials across repeated runs (never all 150, the race is still probabilistic — but never zero either); restored, it passes, including under `-race -shuffle=on`. |
| D82 | **`vault` (§7, Wave 2) shipped: `plugins/vault`, the third external plugin, all 13 capabilities §7 designed** — kv read/write/list, token status, lease show, seal status, policy list/get, transit encrypt/decrypt, and the two response-wrapping capabilities. Built to the same conventions `plugins/pg` already proved rather than inventing new ones: `sdk.Serve(Plugin())` for the whole `main`, a shared `connFields()` (`address`/`namespace`/`token`, the credential `Secret`+`Local`+`EnvFallback` exactly like `pg`'s `password`), `classify(err, req) *view.Error` turning `*vaultapi.ResponseError`'s `StatusCode` into a coded, hinted error the same way `pg`'s `classify` reads `pgconn.PgError.Code`, and `cap()` forcing `NoPreview` on every capability including `vault.overview` — a live Vault deployment is exactly the kind of off-box cost `pg`'s own `cap()` comment argues the automatic dashboard must never decide to poll on its own. Three capabilities were renamed from their first draft once `sdktest`'s verb-vocabulary check ran: `kv.put` → **`kv.set`**, matching `builtin/kv`'s own capability name for the identical overwrite-risk operation rather than mirroring the `vault` CLI's own verb; `token.lookup` → **`token.status`** and `lease.lookup` → **`lease.show`**, both closer fits from the shared vocabulary than the warning's own suggestion ("search," which reads as querying among many rather than fetching one named or implicit record) — `lease.show` in particular matches `kv.show`'s own framing, "everything about one entry." `transit.encrypt`/`transit.decrypt` kept their vocabulary warning deliberately, the same call D68 made for `eol.check`: no existing rta verb means "perform a keyed cryptographic transform," and `codec`'s `encode`/`decode` is the wrong pair to reach for instead — those are lossless representation changes anyone can reverse, not a security-relevant operation using key material, and blurring the two would understate exactly what changed between `vault.transit.encrypt` (plain Write) and `vault.transit.decrypt` (Write+`NeedsGrant`, since it reveals a plaintext the caller may not already hold — `transit.encrypt` never does, since the caller supplied that plaintext to begin with). | A real, load-bearing bug caught by `sdktest` before any manual testing: `vault.wrap.get`'s first draft named its own positional input `token`, colliding with `connFields()`'s shared `token` (the plugin's own auth credential) — `sdktest`'s declaration check refused it outright ("declares input \"token\" twice") rather than the confusion surfacing later as two different values silently fighting over one resolved field. Renamed to `wrapping-token`. Every non-trivial claim about the dependency's own behavior was checked against `$(go env GOMODCACHE)/github.com/hashicorp/vault/api@v1.23.0`'s actual source before code was written against it (the D71/D72/D79 precedent), not assumed from training data: `KVv2.Get`/`Put` wrap `ErrSecretNotFound` through `fmt.Errorf("%w: ...")`, so `classify` needs `errors.Is` to see through it, confirmed with a dedicated test rather than trusted; `Logical().List` on an empty or nonexistent path returns `(nil, nil)`, not an error, matching `vault.kv.list`'s empty-table (not error) handling; and — the one that would have been genuinely hard to guess right — `sys/wrapping/wrap` gets a default 5-minute wrap TTL from the client library itself (`DefaultWrappingLookupFunc` special-cases exactly that path) even with zero configuration, so `vault.wrap.set`'s own TTL override only had to replace that lookup function for the one client instance already scoped to a single call, not invent wrapping behavior from nothing. Verified live rather than only against synthetic `*vaultapi.ResponseError` fixtures: a real `vault server -dev` exercised `seal.status`, `token.status`, `kv.set`/`get`/`list` (including the sub-path case), `transit.encrypt`/`decrypt` (round-tripped through real base64 en/decoding), `policy.list`/`get` (including confirming the built-in `root` policy is genuinely unreadable by Vault's own design — the real `vault policy read root` CLI gives the identical refusal, not a bug in this plugin's error path), and the full `wrap.set` → `wrap.get --dry-run` (twice, proving it is non-consuming and repeatable) → `wrap.get` (consumes it) → `wrap.get` again (Vault's own "not valid or does not exist," proving single-use) sequence end to end. `s3`, `etcd` and `redis` remain unbuilt — `s3` and `etcd` are named in §7/§11 but neither has vault's level of capability detail worked out yet (`etcd`'s own line there is one sentence, and it is slotted at M4, a milestone later than `vault`/`s3`'s M3), and `redis` has no capability list at all yet, only "mirrors `pg`'s shape once `pg` proves the contract" — which it now has, twice over. |
| D83 | **A nine-dimension adversarial audit workflow (nine finder agents over grant/atomicfile/pluginhost/mcp/kv/plugin-contract/registry-config/cross-plugin/tunnel-net, every finding put through three independent skeptics requiring a 2-of-3 non-refuted majority to survive) found ten real bugs, ranked critical through low; fixed in that order rather than resuming `s3`/`etcd`/`redis`, per the standing "bugs and security before features" rule and the maintainer's explicit invitation to make any breaking change the audit called for.** First fixed: `internal/grant.spend` could spend a single `MaxUses`-limited grant past its budget within one call, when that call named several distinct records at once (`kv.env --key a --key b --key c` against a `--max-uses 1` grant with no `Scope`). `checkAgainst` and `spend` each walked `scopes(c, values)` independently — `checkAgainst` asked only "does *anything* cover this scope", with no notion of a budget, so it approved all three named keys against the one grant that covered them; `spend` then incremented that same grant three times in the one `Save` the call made, taking `Uses` from 0 straight to 3 against a `MaxUses` of 1. The call was never refused, `Uses` correctly ended up ≥ `MaxUses` (so the grant did stop authorizing *afterward*), but a grant documented and issued as "reveal this secret exactly once" revealed three, in a single `Reserve` call with no concurrency involved at all — distinct from the D74 duplicate-scope-value bug (the same record named twice) and the D81 fast-path TOCTOU (a race between two calls), neither of which this reproduces or overlaps with. Fixed by replacing the two independent walks with one shared `allocate(c, values, grants)` that both `checkAgainst` and `spend` now call: it walks each named scope once, greedily assigns it to the first covering grant that still has budget left *considering what this same call has already tentatively assigned it*, and falls through to a further covering grant if the first is exhausted rather than assuming the first match is the only candidate. A call whose named records add up to more than any reachable grant can afford is refused outright — no partial spend, since a single tool call has no notion of executing for some of its named records and not others. | Regression test `TestDistinctRecordsInOneCallCannotExceedAGrantsBudget` issues a `MaxUses:1` grant with no `Scope`, calls `Reserve` with three distinct keys, and asserts both that the call is refused and that `Uses` stayed at 0 (nothing partially spent) — then, in a fresh store, confirms a call naming exactly as many distinct records as a grant can afford still succeeds and spends exactly that many, so the fix is a budget check, not an accidental single-record-per-call regression. Proven to have teeth: reverted `grant.go` back to the independent-walk version, re-ran — the test failed exactly as the bug describes ("a call naming three records was authorized against a one-use grant"); restored, it passes, including the full package suite under `-count=1 -race -shuffle=on`. The other nine findings (`internal/atomicfile.Publish` following a symlink on its already-published fallback; `builtin/http`'s grant scope not surviving a redirect; `builtin/kv`'s unlocked concurrent load/save; `pkg/plugin`'s missing duplicate-`Field.Name` check; a YAML alias-expansion DoS in `internal/config`; `builtin/net/probe.go`'s unbounded TLS handshake and its `--wait`-defeating banner-read reset; dead, misleadingly-documented code in `builtin/all`; and `internal/tunnel`'s uncapped port-forward wait) are tracked for the same fix-and-test treatment; each gets its own decision entry as it lands. |
| D84 | **`atomicfile.Publish`'s "someone already published" fallback followed symlinks.** `Link` failing with `EEXIST` is supposed to mean exactly one thing — a prior, whole publication already sits at `path`, since `Link` is the only thing this package's own `Publish` ever creates that path with. The fallback that reads what's there, though, called plain `os.ReadFile`, which follows symlinks: a symlink sitting at the target path (planted, left over from a botched restore, or created by an unrelated tool) is also `EEXIST` for `Link`, and `os.ReadFile` silently resolved it and returned whatever file it pointed at as though that were the genuine publication — the actual callers (`internal/grant`'s seal key, `internal/pluginhost`'s cache seal key) only check that what comes back is ≥32 bytes, so a long-enough foreign file reachable through such a symlink would have been adopted outright, defeating the MAC those files exist to provide. A *dangling* symlink hit the opposite failure: `os.ReadFile` returns `ENOENT`, which the loop treated as ordinary lock contention ("go round again") and retried all 10 times before permanently failing with a "gave up after repeatedly losing and re-losing the race" error — even though nothing real ever occupied the path and the create should simply have succeeded once the symlink was gone (which, being a symlink and not this package's own lock-file churn, it never would be). Fixed with an `os.Lstat` (which does not follow) before ever reading through the path: a symlink, dangling or not, is refused outright with a clear error naming it as the reason, never read through and never silently retried as if it were contention. | Two regression tests: one creates a real symlink to a readable ≥32-byte foreign file and asserts `Publish` errors rather than returning the foreign bytes; one creates a dangling symlink and asserts the error names the symlink rather than reading "gave up after repeatedly losing" (proving it takes the direct refusal path, not the retry-exhaustion one). Both proven to have teeth: reverted `atomicfile.go`, re-ran — the first failed by actually returning the foreign file's bytes with no error, the second failed with exactly the old misleading "gave up" message; restored, both pass, including the whole package plus every consumer (`internal/grant`, `internal/pluginhost`, `builtin/kv`, `builtin/keys`) under `-race -shuffle=on`. |
| D85 | **`http.get`/`post`/`put`/`delete` silently followed redirects, defeating their own `Scope: "url"` grant.** The MCP bridge checks a grant against the literal `url` argument once, before `Run` executes at all; `Run` then sent the request through `stdhttp.DefaultClient`, whose default `CheckRedirect` follows up to 10 hops to wherever a `3xx` response points, with no re-check against the grant and no restriction to the original host. An agent authorized for one destination — `rta grant allow http.get https://status.example.com/health` — could be answered by a response from any host that endpoint chose to redirect to, cloud instance metadata (`169.254.169.254`) being the concrete case that makes this more than theoretical. Fixed by giving the plugin its own `http.Client` whose `CheckRedirect` returns `stdhttp.ErrUseLastResponse` — the redirect response itself (status, headers, `Location`) comes back instead of being chased, and following it, if that's actually wanted, is a second, separately grant-checked call rather than something that happens invisibly inside the first. Applied uniformly, not only when a grant is in play: CLI/TUI callers are ungated but get the same behavior, which reads as more correct for a request-inspection tool anyway — seeing a redirect step named explicitly, with its target, beats it disappearing into a followed chain when debugging an API. The response now surfaces a `location (not followed)` pair whenever a `Location` header is present, so the change is visible rather than a silent behavior shift. | Regression test spins up two real `httptest.Server`s — a "forbidden" one standing in for an unauthorized destination, and a "redirector" one that 302s straight to it — and asserts both that the forbidden handler is never invoked and that the response reports the 302 with the forbidden URL in `location (not followed)`. Proven to have teeth: reverted `http.go`, re-ran — the forbidden handler fired, exactly the leak the finding described; restored, it and the whole package pass, including under `-race -shuffle=on`. |
| D86 | **`builtin/kv`'s write handlers (`kv.set`, `kv.rename`, `kv.rm`, `kv.rekey`) had no locking around their own decrypt-mutate-encrypt-write round trip, so two concurrent calls could silently lose one writer's change with no error on either side.** Each handler loads the whole store, changes one entry in memory, and writes the whole thing back; nothing detected that the on-disk file had changed in between, so goroutine 1 loading `{X}`, adding `A`, and saving `{X,A}` while goroutine 2 (having loaded the same pre-write `{X}` a moment earlier) added `B` and saved `{X,B}` left `A` gone with no error — `kv.set A` had already reported success. `kv.edit` alone had a narrower version of this fixed already (`edit.go`: re-read immediately before its own save and refuse on conflict), reasoning in a comment that "every other write in this plugin closes the load..save window in milliseconds" — an assumption the MCP bridge's one-goroutine-per-`tools/call` dispatch invalidates outright, since two calls scheduled concurrently do not need a long window to collide. Fixed properly rather than by narrowing the window further: extracted `internal/grant`'s own file lock (a `Publish`-based sentinel with stale-lock reclaim and identity-safe release, already proven correct by that package's own concurrency tests) into a new, generic **`internal/filelock`**, so `builtin/kv` reuses the identical mechanism instead of growing a second, independently-maintained copy — the exact kind of duplication-that-becomes-a-hazard the audit's own cross-plugin-consistency dimension was watching for. `kv.set`/`rename`/`rm`/`rekey` now hold `filelock.Acquire` across their whole load..save window; `kv.edit`'s final re-read-and-save step now takes the same lock too (closing its own residual window) while still not holding it for as long as an editor is open, which would have serialized every other write behind one interactive session. | Two-part verification. `internal/grant`'s existing test suite (including the D81 TOCTOU regression test and the two `breakStale` tests, both relocated to `internal/filelock/filelock_test.go` since that is where the logic now lives) passes unchanged after the extraction, proving it a pure refactor. New regression test `TestConcurrentSetsDoNotLoseEachOthersWrites` fires 8 concurrent `kv.set` calls for 8 distinct keys against a pre-seeded store and asserts all 9 entries (8 plus the seed) survive. Proven to have teeth: reverted the locking in `kv.go`/`edit.go`, re-ran 5x — every run lost exactly 6 of 8 writes (2 of 9 entries survived, matching the "last writer wins the whole snapshot" failure mode exactly); restored, it and the whole package pass, including under `-race -shuffle=on`. |
| D87 | **`Capability.validate` never checked for two inputs sharing a name — the same class of "structural correctness at load time" it was supposed to guarantee, one level below the sibling duplicate-ID check `Plugin.Validate` already had.** `declareFlags` (`internal/app`) registers one `pflag.Flag` per declared input in declaration order, for every capability of every loaded plugin, as part of building the whole command tree at startup — not lazily per-command. Two inputs on one capability sharing a name validated cleanly and then hit `pflag`'s `AddFlag` panicking with `"<cmd> flag redefined: <name>"` the moment the second one registered — one badly-declared external plugin crashing `rta` for every command, not just its own, with `Validate`'s own doc claiming exactly this class of mistake was what it caught. The gap was already being patched over one layer up: `pkg/sdk/sdktest`'s `checkInputs` had its own, separately-maintained copy of this same check, catching it for any plugin author who ran the conformance suite in their own tests — but not for a plugin loaded directly by the host, which calls `Validate` alone. Fixed at the source: `Capability.validate` now tracks seen input names the same way `Plugin.Validate` already tracks seen capability IDs, refusing with `"capability %q declares input %q twice"`. `sdktest`'s own copy is now genuinely dead for this failure mode (`checkDeclaration` calls `p.Validate()` first and already returns before `checkInputs` would run) and was deleted rather than left duplicated, closing the exact kind of two-copies-of-one-rule hazard the audit's own cross-plugin-consistency dimension exists to catch. | New table-driven case in `pkg/plugin`'s existing `TestValidateFailures` (two inputs named `target` on one capability, want an error containing `"twice"`) plus an updated `sdktest` test now driving the failure through `checkDeclaration` (the real load-time path) rather than calling the deleted `checkInputs` logic directly. Proven to have teeth: reverted `plugin.go`, re-ran the new case — `want error containing "twice", got <nil>`, the exact silent-acceptance the finding described; restored, the whole repo's test suite passes, including all three external plugin modules (`pg`, `eol`, `vault`) in their own separate `go test` runs, confirming no existing declaration was quietly relying on the gap. |
| D88 | **`config.LoadFile` had no defense against a YAML "billion laughs" bomb: a syntactically valid file using a handful of nested anchor/alias pairs (`&name`/`*name`), each fanning out 10x into the next, expands to 10^9+ decoded values from well under 1 KB on disk — verified against this project's own pinned `goccy-yaml v1.19.2`: 6 levels already produced 1.1 million values in well under a second, and no alias-count or depth limit is exposed anywhere in the library's own option API.** `config.Load`/`LoadFile` runs at the start of essentially every `rta` command, including read-only ones (`--help`, `doctor`, `mcp serve`), so simply having such a file at the config path — `RTA_CONFIG`, or the default location, both routinely shared between operators and machines, `internal/pluginconf`'s own docs say as much — denies service on every subsequent invocation, not one-off. Fixed without picking a "safe" depth or alias-count threshold a cleverer bomb could still clear: `Config`'s own schema (`Output`, `Dashboard`, `Plugins`, `Theme` — a flat, known set of fields) has no legitimate use for YAML's value-reuse syntax at all, so anchors and aliases are refused outright, checked by parsing alone rather than decoding. `goccy-yaml`'s parser builds a graph proportional to the bytes on disk — an `*ast.AliasNode` holds the *name* it references, not a dereferenced copy of the anchor's subtree — so walking that graph with `ast.Walk` to look for any `AnchorNode`/`AliasNode` costs exactly what parsing already did, before anything is substituted; only `Unmarshal`'s own walk would have done the substitution that explodes. | Two regression tests. `TestConfigRefusesYAMLAnchorsAndAliases` pins a small, harmless anchor (nothing here fans out into anything) is refused the same way an unrelated syntax error already is. `TestConfigRefusesAnAliasExpansionBombQuickly` builds a real 6-level, 10x-fan-out bomb (sized to the exact numbers measured above — large enough to prove decoding really would explode, small enough that reverting the fix and letting `Unmarshal` actually run on it does not itself hang the machine running the test) and asserts `Load` returns within 2 seconds — the property that makes this a fix rather than a slower way to eventually hit the same wall. Proven to have teeth: reverted `config.go`, re-ran both — both failed with `<nil>` (no error at all — the bomb decoded successfully, silently, in well under the 2s bound, exactly the pre-fix behavior); restored, both pass, and the whole package plus every consumer (`internal/app`, `internal/pluginconf`, `cmd/rta`) still pass under `-race -shuffle=on`. |
| D89 | **`net.probe`/`net.send`'s TLS handshake (`--tls`) was never bounded by the capability's own documented `timeout` field — only the TCP dial was.** `probe()` computes `timeout` and applies it to `Dialer.Timeout` and, for `net.send`, `SetWriteDeadline` — but handed `tc.HandshakeContext` the bare `ctx`, which carries no deadline of its own anywhere in the call chain (`cmd/rta/main.go`'s root context only cancels on `SIGINT`/`SIGTERM`; the MCP bridge passes through whatever the SDK hands it). A peer that accepts the TCP connection and then never sends a single TLS record hung the handshake forever, holding a goroutine and a socket open — reachable with `rta net probe <host> 443 --tls --timeout 5` against any host/port, both fully caller-controlled, with **no grant needed and none of `--allow-write`'s gating involved**, since `net.probe` is classified Read. Repeated against many targets, an agent (or a script) could exhaust goroutines/file descriptors for free. Fixed with `context.WithTimeout(ctx, timeout)` around the handshake specifically, mirroring how the dial is already bounded. | Regression test spins up a real listener that accepts the TCP connection and then sleeps 5 seconds without sending anything, calls `runProbe` with `--tls --timeout 1`, and asserts the call returns within 3 seconds with `net.probe.tls` rather than hanging. Proven to have teeth: reverted `probe.go`, re-ran — timed out at exactly the 3s bound the test itself imposes (the real hang would have run far longer, to the listener's 5s or beyond); restored, it and the whole package pass, including under `-race -shuffle=on`. |
| D90 | **`readBanner`'s per-read deadline reset to a fixed 200ms (`drainWait`) after the first byte, no matter how long the call had already run, so a peer trickling one byte at a time could hold `net.probe`/`net.send` open for up to `bannerLimit` reads * 200ms — about 13.6 minutes — regardless of what `--wait` (documented "seconds to wait for a reply", capped at 120s) actually asked for.** Host and port are fully caller-supplied, so a remote endpoint an agent is pointed at controls how long the local call stays blocked, silently defeating the one knob that was supposed to bound it. Fixed by giving the whole call one ceiling — `deadline := time.Now().Add(wait)` — and shrinking each read's own patience to fit inside what is left of it (`min(drainWait, time.Until(deadline))`), so the loop now ends on `--wait`'s own terms rather than a trickling peer's; a timeout past that ceiling returns whatever was collected, the same "silence is itself an answer" ending the function already documented for the first-byte case. | Regression test spins up a listener that writes one byte every 50ms — comfortably inside every pre-fix 200ms window — for far longer than a 1-second `--wait`, and asserts the call returns within 3 seconds. Proven to have teeth: reverted `probe.go`, re-ran — timed out at the 3s bound exactly as the finding predicted; restored, it and the whole package pass, including under `-race -shuffle=on`. |
| D91 | **`builtin/all.Namespaces()` was dead code — zero callers anywhere in the repository, production or test — whose doc comment claimed it was the mechanism behind a real, specific security fix (the MCP gate correctly telling a built-in from a plugin), when that fix actually lives entirely in `internal/registry`'s `Origin`/`RegisterFrom` bookkeeping, wired through `internal/mcp/bridge.go`'s `Options.Origin` and `entryMatches`.** Both places describe the identical incident almost verbatim — "a plugin that stayed registered while dropping out of the plugin host's bookkeeping was read [here] as built-in" — strongly suggesting `Namespaces()` was an earlier attempt at the fix, superseded by the `registry.Origin`-based mechanism that actually shipped, and simply never deleted afterward. Left in place, it was a hazard rather than a harmless leftover: a maintainer reading its comment could reasonably believe it was load-bearing and hesitate to touch it, or "fix" a future regression by wiring it in without noticing the real mechanism already lives elsewhere — two sources of truth for the same origin question. Deleted rather than wired in, since the mechanism that is actually correct and tested (`internal/mcp`'s existing suite, unaffected by this deletion) already exists. | No regression test needed or possible — there is no behavior to pin, only unreachable code and a misleading comment to remove. Verified by the same evidence the audit found it with (a repo-wide grep for any caller, still zero) plus the full test suite (every package, including `internal/mcp`) passing unchanged after the deletion. |
| D92 | **`internal/tunnel.Open` had no fallback ceiling of its own — `awaitForwarding`'s select waits on the listener line, kubectl exiting, or `ctx.Done()`, and nothing else — so a caller passing a context with no deadline (`context.Background()`, which every call site including this package's own tests used) combined with a `kubectl` that neither prints the forwarding line nor exits (stuck behind an interactive exec-credential-plugin prompt, or a network partition eating the SYN) hung forever.** Currently unreached from any wired capability, CLI command or MCP tool — the package has no callers outside its own tests — so the impact was latent rather than exploitable today; fixed now anyway rather than left for whenever it does get wired up, on the same "close it while it's cheap" reasoning the rest of this audit followed. Fixed with a package-level `openCeiling` (60s, generous headroom over the handful of seconds a listener ordinarily takes) layered onto the caller's own context via `context.WithTimeout` — which always takes the *earlier* of two deadlines, so a caller-supplied one that is already sooner is never overridden, this only ever adds a floor under an absent one. Deliberately scoped to a separate `waitCtx`, not the `ctx` the long-lived `kubectl` process itself runs under: cancelling the wait-bound context the moment `awaitForwarding` returns must not kill a tunnel that had just finished opening successfully. `openCeiling` is a `var`, not a `const` — the same pattern the file already used for `kubectl` itself — so a test can shrink it rather than really waiting out a minute. | New regression test overrides `openCeiling` to 200ms, points `Open` at a fixture `kubectl` that loops forever printing nothing, and asserts the call still returns `tunnel.open.timeout` well under 3 seconds despite `context.Background()` never expiring on its own.
