That is a one-time grant delivering a secret sixteen times, and it was untested in the only place it can happen. Every `Check` site now goes through `Reserve` and releases, so the thing asserted about is the thing that runs. | **Two gates cannot agree, so there is one.** `bounded` and `pinned` were separable only for the caller that used one without the other, and that caller was the weaker gate; merged into `reachable`, the arrangement that produced the D110 data-loss bug is unreachable and so is the D109 filter bypass. The doctrine both wrappers carried — why a filter and not a check, why the empty pin matches nothing, why the positions are the point — moved onto it rather than being deleted with them. Three comments were also corrected rather than left: `Uses` described incrementing *after* a successful call, the opposite of what Reserve does and the ordering whose removal closed the TOCTOU at D81; `Save` credited its atomicity to *"Consume's lock"* and to *"Load, called from Check on every gated MCP call"*. A stale comment in a security package is not cosmetic — it is the map the next reader navigates by. **And a 1-in-248 flake that presented as a segfault.** `TestRestoreRejectsAWordWithABrokenChecksum` swapped the last mnemonic word for a fixed one and *assumed* the checksum broke; the last word of a 24-word mnemonic carries 8 checksum bits over 3 of entropy, so the substitution leaves a valid mnemonic about once in 256 — measured at 806/200,000, one in 248. It now tries candidates until `fromMnemonic` actually rejects one, so it cannot silently stop testing the branch it names. The reason it panicked rather than failed was `errCode`, which read `.Code` off a `view.AsError(nil, …)` that is nil by contract — turning *"this call unexpectedly succeeded"*, the thing all 27 of its assertions exist to catch, into a stack trace. |
| D112 | **The tunnel is wired: `kube:` opens a port-forward, fills the call, and tears it down.** Asked what to do next and offered three Kubernetes slices, the answer was the tunnel — `internal/tunnel` had been built, driven end to end against a real cluster and left unwired since ADR 0018, and the RBAC generator would have emitted manifests for a feature still refused by name. **The design point the ADRs left open was which inputs a forward fills.** ADR 0018 §2 proposed `Field.Endpoint`; ADR 0019 dropped it because a two-role Host/Port scheme cannot express `s3`, which has one input holding `host:port`, or `vault`, which has one holding a URL. That objection is fatal to two roles and dissolves with five: Host, Port, Address, URL, TLS covers every plugin in the tree, and each of those three shapes is now one plugin's declaration. Chosen over making the operator hand-map it, because "which of *my* inputs is the host" is a fact about the plugin — fixed at compile time, identical for every operator and every profile — and asking every operator to re-derive it in every connection is a new instance of the class `checkSet` already exists to catch. It is `Field.Config` one step along: the plugin says which input a value belongs in, the host decides whether there is a value. **And it is the only way to deliver ADR 0018 §7**, which is measured rather than argued: PostgreSQL's `prefer` kills a forward on the *clean disconnect*, on the first call, for somebody who changed nothing — only the host knows a forward is in the path and only the plugin knows which input decides TLS. | **Fill and Dial are two functions because their lifetimes are two.** Fill's answer describes the environment and is the same for every call made while it stands, which is why the TUI resolves it once at the switch and holds it — unlocking an age store costs about a second of scrypt and the dashboard refreshes every five. A forward is per call by decision (a port-forward outlives the pod it points at). One function doing both would have been wrong in whichever direction it was cached, and the TUI is where that shows: its bind loop covers every capability an environment mentions, so dialling there would have raised one forward per capability and held them all for the session, while *not* dialling there would have sent every TUI run on a `kube:` profile to the plugin's default host — a live PostgreSQL on plenty of machines, so it connects to the wrong thing rather than failing. The cached binding carries its connection for the same reason: re-resolving at run time would re-read the config file on the one path the cache exists to keep off it, and would read a *different* file if the operator edited it in between. **`Connection.Unwired` is gone**, with the two branches that called it: both halves it named are wired, so it could only return empty, and a permanently-false branch guarding a shipped feature is the shape of a refusal left behind. **A crash I introduced and the suite did not catch.** `resolveProfile` returned a nil teardown when `Fill` failed, and `runCapability` defers it *before* checking the error — deliberately, so a forward raised by a call that then fails is still closed. Every `--profile` run against a profile with a broken `secrets:` reference panicked instead of printing the refusal it had already composed. Found by running the built binary against a fake `kubectl`, not by a test; the test that now walks every return out of that function panics against the old code. The same session's end-to-end also showed `rta profile show` printing a connection without its coordinate — a page describing somewhere else, since a `set:` host beside a `kube:` coordinate is two statements about the destination and it showed only the losing one. **And one more path had no tunnel at all, found by being asked "does this work in the TUI?" rather than by the suite**: `tileCmd` ran its handler with the cached bind values and never dialled, so a dashboard tile under a cluster profile ran against `localhost:5432` while the badge said `homelab` — real data from the wrong database on any machine with a local PostgreSQL, refreshed every five seconds, with nothing saying so. The wiring commit had three Dial call sites and needed four; "which paths run a handler?" is a question the suite could not answer because no test asserted a tile's *destination*. Tiles dial now: a dashboard that lies is worse than one that costs, and the cost is the per-call price ADR 0018 §4 already accepted for the TUI. |
| D113 | **The tunnel worked in YAML and was unreachable from the editor, which is the same as most people not having it.** Asked "how do I target a kube service or pod and use a Secret key as the password — in the TUI?", the answer was that you could not: the `profile.plugin` form offered `set.*` and nothing else, so a coordinate and a `kube:` credential were expressible only by hand-editing the config file. Somebody looking at that screen would reasonably conclude rta could not do it. The connection form carries the coordinate now, above the `set.*` fields because it decides where the call goes and they are subordinate to it; it round-trips, or editing a host would silently drop it, which is the hazard the credential mapping already had; and a coordinate that is not one is refused at the keystroke rather than saved and reported by `rta doctor` afterwards, which would mean the profile was saved broken and the operator has moved on. The credential form gains the cluster as a source, offered only when the connection names one — a Secret is read from the namespace the coordinate gives, so without a coordinate it would dead-end the way referencing an empty store does. | **And the picker offered the wrong set, which turned out to be the same bug as a false report.** It listed a namespace's *Secret* inputs, so `user` could not be mapped at all — while in the file it works, because `internal/profile.Fill` gates on `ProfileFillable` rather than on `Type == Secret`. Mapping `user` onto a Secret's `username` key is an ordinary thing to want and is exactly what was asked for. The same narrow rule was also used by `rta profile show`, which therefore printed *"secrets.user names an input pg does not offer"* about a mapping that resolved perfectly well, and printed nothing at all about the value deciding the connection. Report-versus-reality drift for the fourth recorded time, in the direction that cries wolf rather than the direction that hides a failure — but the fix is the one this repo keeps arriving at: **the rule that decides is the one on the path that uses the value**, and the test asserts the page and a run agree in *both* directions, because a rule that flags nothing agrees with a run just as badly as one that flags everything. Two existing form tests changed premise rather than expectation, since the ordinary fixture declares a Config-keyed `host` beside its password and so genuinely has two fillable inputs now; "one input is not a question" is preserved against a fixture that really has one. **Process note:** `git checkout -- <file>` discarded uncommitted work twice in one session. Commit first, then probe. |
| D114 | **Two design questions asked and answered before building: no to folding the tunnel into the legacy fields, yes to cluster completion.** *"Why not just use `host`/`username`/`password`?"* — because a coordinate is not a host: `set.host` is data the plugin dials, `kube:` is an instruction rta executes first, and folding them means prefix-sniffing, where `kube;homelab/…` (one typo'd character) stops being a malformed coordinate and becomes a literal hostname failing minutes later as a DNS error pointing nowhere near the mistake. "The host field" is not even a stable target — one coordinate fills pg's `host`+`port`+`sslmode`, s3's `endpoint`+`tls`, vault's `address` — so the plugin-agnostic `kube:` key is the *more* uniform design, and the `set`/`secrets` split is enforced rather than stylistic: plugin validation refuses `Config` on a `Secret`, which makes "no credential in the plaintext file" a structural property of every plugin that will ever exist. Port-into-host merging dies on IPv6 (`::1`). **Completion is ADR 0018 §8, written before the code**: the purity doctrine (`Bind` opens no process, socket or store, because shell completion resolves per keystroke) meets a feature whose answers live in an apiserver, and the line moves once — local may be automatic, sockets only on a deliberate keypress. ctrl+g completes the segment under the cursor: contexts from kubeconfig, kinds statically before kubectl is even looked for, namespaces/names/ports/Secrets/keys from listings pinned to the *typed* context and namespace, never the ambient current-context — completing `prod-mirror` must not poke `prod`. Secret keys are the sharp edge: no keys-only read exists (RBAC `get` grants the values), so the listing is a real audit-visible `get secret`, on the explicit key, under help text that says so — and rendered through a go-template so kubectl prints key names and the values never enter rta's process, tighter than the call-time read that must decode what it delivers. Fail-open throughout: the operator with `get`+`port-forward` on one service and `list` on nothing is the expected least-privilege shape, and gets kubectl's own refusal beside a field that still takes typing. | **The build was decided by three library facts, each read in the source before writing code.** huh re-evaluates a `SuggestionsFunc` only when a *bound string's* hash changes, so an async answer would not surface until the next keystroke — the fetch therefore sets `Input.Suggestions` directly on the widget, whose setter re-matches immediately, and bubbles' own prefix matching, ↓-cycling and accept do the rest with no cache, no generation counters, no watch plumbing. huh's tab dispatch emits NextField *and* lets textinput accept — one press accepts and hops away, right for whole-value suggestions and fatal for a value built four segments deep — so the two segmented fields get a per-field `WithKeyMap` where tab accepts in place and enter still advances, applied after form construction because `huh.NewForm` clobbers per-field keymaps at build. And bubbles matches suggestions only against non-empty text, so an empty box's fetch flashes the names themselves while a non-empty one flashes the affordances. Answers land by `capForm` identity — a fetch for a closed form is dropped, not applied to whichever same-named field a later form holds. Contexts a coordinate cannot hold (EKS ARNs, slashes) are filtered from suggestions rather than offered as traps. All three load-bearing tests verified by probe against the committed tree: drop the keymap override, drop the identity guard, swap the keys-only template for `-o json` — each fails its named test. **And one self-inflicted scare that the discipline caught**: `Write` to the generic name `complete_test.go` silently replaced an existing file holding three shared helpers — recovered whole from the previous commit, because commit-first-then-probe had left nothing uncommitted. The lesson is now a habit: a new file's name gets checked against the tree before it is written. ConfigMaps deliberately got nothing: `secrets:` exists so a value is never written down, a ConfigMap value is what `set:` is for, and a completion for a feature that does not exist would be scope invented to fill a keybinding. |
| D115 | **"I guess we are using different keys for completion — harmonize" was right, and the audit says how far.** The app's completion key was already tab, everywhere, in its own words: "tab completes paths", "press tab", "tab completes" under the theme fields. ctrl+e is not a completion key in rta at all — formKeyMap moved AcceptSuggestion off huh's default onto tab long ago, leaving ctrl+e meaning "open $EDITOR" on Text bodies (its help says so) and emacs line-end inside inputs; the copypick form builds without formKeyMap but is Select-only, so it is already consistent for its field type. The one intruder was ctrl+g, introduced a session earlier for cluster fetches. It is gone: **tab fetches when nothing on offer strictly extends the box, accepts when something does** — one key, state decides, which is what tab has meant in a shell for decades, and the precedent ADR 0018 §8 itself cites (kubectl's completion) runs on the same key. §8 holds unchanged: tab is not typing, and on the segmented fields it cannot be navigation either, because it never leaves them — enter advances — so a tab there is always a completion press. Help texts tightened in the same pass ("we highly prefer responsive design"): the coordinate example is gone from help that wrapped to three lines, because tab now produces real examples; help wraps at form width, and every wrapped line pushes a field off a small screen. | **Both defects this change produced were caught by the discipline, one each.** The first draft of needsFetch treated a trailing separator as "empty segment, fetch" — which also describes the box the moment a fetch *lands*, so the tab meant to accept the ghost refetched forever; the walk test caught it on first run, and the fixed rule is one question with no special case: does anything offered strictly extend the box (equal is not an extension, which is precisely why the moment after an accept reads as fetch-next). The second was in a *test*: rerouting accept through Model.Update made the keymap-override probe pass vacuously — Update returns huh's NextField command unrun, so focus never moved in the test while it would in a session — found because every load-bearing test is probed against deliberately broken code after committing, and this one stopped failing. It now drives the accept through settleForm, which cascades commands the way a live program does, and the probe bites in both directions again. The needsFetch table pins the pair that matters: the same string "homelab/" against the context list (moment after accept → fetch) and against the namespace list (moment after fetch → take the ghost) — the two moments the separator heuristic could not tell apart. |
| D116 | **One pasted `rta profile show me` held three defects, each a different layer of the same failure: the screen and the call disagreeing about where a run goes.** First, `pg@90f88ba4c640` predates the endpoint roles — roles are declared by the artifact, so a stale binary declares none, the forward opens, fills nothing, and the call proceeds to the plugin's default host: `nothing is listening on localhost:5432`, under a picker saying homelab. The page *knew* — "declares no input a tunnel can fill, so the forward would be opened and ignored" sat right there as an advisory `problem` row — while every run sailed past it: page-versus-run drift, fifth recorded instance, in the direction that hides the failure. `Lookup` refuses now (`core.profile.untunnellable`), `Check` reports the same words, and the two control tests that stated coordinates against a role-less fixture moved to a tunnelled registry — premise changed, not point. Second, **the run form handed back everything it displayed as caller values**: the plugin's declared `localhost`/`5432` in untouched boxes, and Caller beats Profile *by design* (`--profile prod --host x` connects to x) — so even against a correct plugin, the boxes nobody touched would have outrun the forward. Third, **the connection editor wrote every displayed default into `set:`** — `set:host localhost` beside a `kube:` coordinate, four phantom keys out of one typed database name. | **The fix is one distinction, `capForm.derived`: what a form displayed is never what the caller said.** `fromProfile` guarded only the environment's seeds; the same failure arrives from every layer — config, declaration — and the seed-time environment is not even the one the run uses once the picker moves. Now runForm marks everything it seeded that the caller did not give, the connection and config editors mark what their file does not state, an untouched declared default on a final form is a display too (the run re-derives it at the layer it belongs to, which is exactly what lets a layer the form cannot know yet — a forward's per-call endpoint — win), and the emptied-box default stand-in is deleted: it stood in as a *caller* value, which both outran environments and resurrected the very key the config editor's absent-means-cleared contract had just deleted. Guardrails that held: prior answers survive re-edits (seeded-unmarked = a person's), stage-one prefill forms keep everything (their values become stage two's base, not re-derived — `displayed` gates on `final`), editors round-trip file values, and pickers/confirms entered through are choices, not displays. Probes bit on both marks — the second only after the fixture gained a *picker*, because untouched text is caught by the declared-default fallback while a select always holds a choice and only the editor's marks know the file never made it. The display half: under a cluster connection, endpoint boxes open empty (the forward's endpoint does not exist yet), TLS shows the off value the run will use, help says the port-forward fills them, and a value the person gave stays on screen and wins. |
| D117 | **Six catch-all files became twenty topic files, in six pure-move commits, after "we are getting really long files — review our code structure."** The review's verdict held: the macro structure (internal/ vs the pkg/plugin contract vs builtin/ vs plugins/) is right and packages did not move — a package is an API boundary and splitting one is a design decision; a file inside a package is free organization and splitting it changes nothing. The giants were the residuals: tui.go (2098 → 1102 + run/formflow/result — the execute pipeline, the model-side form driving, the result pane), pkg/plugin/plugin.go (1146 → 426 + validate/request/completion, so the contract's front page reads in one sitting and the registration rules live beside each other), mcp/bridge.go (1414 → 452 + options/schema/args, keeping NewServer and the whole gate pipeline together because the security path reads as one piece), kv.go (1576 → 379 + store/caps), dashboard.go (1147 → 412 + actions/grid), audit/graphfiles.go (1272 → 507 + graphjs/graphtoml). Deliberately untouched: generated *.pb.go; internal/grant (a security argument that reads top-to-bottom and was adversarially reviewed twice this month — churn resets that assurance); test files, which are navigated by test name. | **Mechanically: a block mover, not an editor.** A small script relocates top-level declarations with their doc comments verbatim — the doc comments are the doctrine record, and a split that orphaned or paraphrased them would cost more than any length saves — asserting every requested name resolves exactly once (method names qualified as Type.Name after `Surface` the type collided with `Request.Surface` the method) and that the source shrinks by exactly what the targets gained. New files take the source's full import block and the compiler prunes it: iterate on "imported and not used" (both spellings — the aliased form says "imported as huh and not used") deleting reported lines highest-first. One commit per source file, package tests + vet between, `-race -shuffle=on` across the tree at the end. Phase two, deliberately not done in the same pass: tui.go's remaining bulk is the 630-line Update switch, and carving that into per-mode methods is restructuring logic, not moving it — a different kind of change with a different risk, which is exactly why it does not belong in a commit that promises bytes were only relocated. |
| D118 | **Endpoint boxes disappeared, `set:`/`kube:` conflicts became refusals, and completion went live — buckets, mounts, KV paths — after "hide hosts/ports when profile-kube is used?" and "could we get completion for Vault and S3 too?".** The hiding landed in both places the investigation found. A run form whose picked connection tunnels no longer asks about endpoint-role inputs at all (`forwardFilled`): an empty box with a hint was still a question, and three questions the operator must not answer outweigh three lines of help — the picker's own help now carries the one line ("runs through a port-forward, which fills host, port, sslmode"), a caller-given value keeps its field and still wins, and the TUI's type-a-host-to-bypass-the-forward escape hatch was given up deliberately (that path opens a forward and ignores it — the bug class D-earlier removed; "(none)" in the picker and CLI flags keep the capability). The connection editor stops offering endpoint-role `set.*` boxes beside a coordinate except ones the file already states — those stay, labelled "overridden by the coordinate — empty the box to remove it", because the editor is the repair tool and hiding the value that blocks the profile would leave nowhere to clear it. And the conflict itself is refused, not advised: the new checkSet rule feeds Lookup like its siblings, because a first draft's "advisory, not a refusal" comment was exactly the page-versus-run drift this file records five times — worse here, since profilePicker excludes any Check-flagged profile, which would have made the advisory a silent de-facto refusal with no message anywhere. saveConnForm refuses writing the pair on the one screen with both boxes, for CheckKube's stated reason. | **Live completion is `Field.Live` — one bit that moves a Suggest to the deliberate channel.** The investigation's finding was that the plumbing existed end-to-end (Suggest crosses gRPC as has_suggest; the TUI evaluates it per keystroke; CompletionRequest strips secrets after the $RTA_S3_SECRET_KEY incident) and exactly two deliberate design changes were needed: cadence and credentials. `Candidates` — the per-keystroke channel both the TUI and shell completion ride — answers nil for a live input, one gate at the fork; the TUI's tab (completeFromService, the third completer on the app's one key) resolves what the run would see on the event loop as startRun does, refuses a connection naming a cluster coordinate ("the forward opens per call and a completion is not a call"), and hands the plugin `LiveRequest` — credentials included, which the CompletionRequest doc itself already conceded is "early delivery, not a new principal": same pinned recipient, moments earlier, at the operator's explicit request; the surface still says completion. A landed fetch also lands in capForm.liveGot behind its own lock, or the keystroke channel's re-evaluation wiped it off the widget one keypress later. Items carry trailing separators so needsFetch's extends-or-fetch rhythm composes unchanged (minio's CommonPrefixes and Vault's `folder/` LIST entries already speak it). s3 wires bucket/key/prefix/dest-bucket (listings capped at a screenful, GETs pinned by a recording fake); vault wires kv mount+path, transit mount+keys, policy names (LIST is Vault's names-only op — values never move; kv.get's gated path completes ungated because the identical listing is vault.kv.list's whole ungated answer); pg's database stays deferred — it needs a live server connection, which under `kube:` is the forward that only exists per call. ADR 0018 §8 amended. Last, the picker now re-seeds the run form the moment it moves (reseedOnPickerMove): seeds re-derive, forwardFilled re-decides what is asked, a listing fetched under the old environment's credentials dies with the old form, and what the person typed is exactly what survives — the screenshot session's "strange to not get fields filled with the profile config", finally answered on screen rather than only in the resolve order. CLI live completion is the noted follow-up: shell tab is already deliberate, but resolving profile credentials silently inside a completion needs its own design. |
| D119 | **The repair the refusal forgot, twice: the sslmode select trap, then the `secrets:` twin of the whole set:/kube: rule.** D118's editor kept a file-stated endpoint key's box "so it could be cleared" — which assumed clearing is something every widget can do. A stated `sslmode` is a select, a select always holds one of its Options (and a `tls` toggle always holds a bool), so the operator faced a box whose one legal action the widget could not perform while the save guard refused every save of the entry: found in the field, by the operator this session, one day after it shipped. The fix inverted the design: the boxes are hidden under a coordinate regardless of what the file states, and **the save is the repair** — endpoint `set:` keys are dropped whether they came from the file past hidden boxes or from boxes visible while the coordinate was typed, and the flash names them ("saved pg in me — removed set.host, set.sslmode: the forward fills these"). A refusal is only honest when the operator can comply; where the widgets cannot express compliance, repair-with-receipt is the honest form. checkSet's refusal stands for hand-edited files — the editor simply never produces the pair. | **Then "can `user`/`access-key` come from a kubernetes Secret too?" — yes, it always could (Fill gates on ProfileFillable, kubeSecrets reads N keys of one Secret in one get, the editor offers every fillable input) — but verifying that surfaced the mapping-side incoherences, and checkSecretRefs now closes three at once, feeding Check and Lookup in the same words like checkSet does: a mapping onto an input the namespace does not offer and a `kube:` credential with no coordinate were run-path refusals only (the page called those profiles valid); a mapping onto an input the coordinate's own forward fills existed nowhere — Fill fetched the credential, a real read in the store's or cluster's audit log, and Dial laid the endpoint over it, discarded on every call. The editors follow the rule rather than merely refusing after it: the credential editor stops offering endpoint-role targets under a coordinate (they stay offered on direct connections, where host-from-a-local-entry is ordinary), and the conn editor's save repairs shadowed mappings beside shadowed set: keys in the same flash. Process note recorded at cost: this round's probe-and-restore ran before the commit, so each probe's `git checkout` silently reverted the uncommitted implementation and the "expected" failures were the rule being absent, not the neuter biting — caught only by the full-suite gate before committing. Probe after committing, never before; the restore step assumes the commit exists. |
| D120 | **"Fix every bug you found before new features": a self-review of my own completion and editor work surfaced four real defects, all shipped within days of writing them.** Worst pair, found by one test's first honest run: completeFromService resolved the environment before checking its coordinate — so a tab that was about to be refused read the connection's `kube:` Secret out of the cluster first, an audit-visible access caused by a keypress that then did nothing, plus a Fill stall on the update loop — and, uncovered while pinning that, completion **stripped the picker before resolving**, so it always completed against the switched-on environment while the picker above the field named another: wrong credentials, wrong listing, silently. resolveProfile split into pickedConn (config-only) + fillConn (the fetching half); completion checks the coordinate between them and resolves from full values like startRun always did; a poison-kubectl test pins that no completion press fetches. Third: plugin.Request's readers deliberately do not coerce text, so a `secrets:` mapping onto an Int/Bool/Float input resolved, authenticated, and handed the handler a zero — refused now (checkSecretRefs), and the credential editor stops offering those targets. Fourth: the conn editor's save rebuilt `set:` from the boxes it had — none, when the plugin is not installed — silently emptying the block; a form must not rewrite what it could not show, and a key nothing reads is now dropped with a receipt instead of silently. Verified suspects that cleared: profileSeed is Bind-pure, so the picker reseed costs file reads only. | **Then two plan items resolved in opposite directions.** CLI live completion is closed as won't-do rather than built: fish evaluates completion functions speculatively while typing (autosuggestions fall back to completions), so a shell completion invocation is not reliably a deliberate press — the premise §8 requires — which is what the ADR's own "the CLI's shell completion stays pure" already said; the follow-up dissolved under its hypothesis check, and recents remain the CLI's answer. And D117's deliberately deferred phase two landed: Update's 380-line key switch became one handler per pane (dispatch.go, tui.go 1102 → 605), the new logic being exactly the handled bool D117 predicted — false hands the key to the pane's passthrough and **promises m was not changed on the way**, since the passthrough updates Update's own copy and a mutation on the handler's copy would be silently lost; every fall-through arm was audited for that before extraction. Process scar, again: the handlers were first Written to keys.go — which exists, and holds the key vocabulary; "updated successfully" instead of "created" was the tell, the committed tree made it one stash away from recovered, and the content landed in dispatch.go after actually checking the name. Two filename collisions in one project is the pattern the memory exists for; the tell is now written down beside it. |
| D121 | **`ssh:` targets shipped — the tunnel promise D10 and ADR 0018 §1's own example carried since the beginning — and the design the kube path would suggest turned out not to exist.** First fact, measured before a line was written: OpenSSH refuses a dynamic local forward outright (`-L 0:` is "Bad local forwarding specification" on OpenSSH 10.2, verified against a throwaway sshd in the scratchpad), so there is no kubectl-style "parse the port the child prints" — and bind-then-release is the race tunnel.go already rejects in writing. The shape that survives: **rta owns the listener** (binds 127.0.0.1:0, holds the socket) and splices each accepted connection through `ssh -W dest -- host`, ProxyJump's own primitive; open probes once with stdin already closed, so success is a deterministic exit 0 — EOF crosses, the destination closes back — and auth/hostkey/DNS/bastion/dest failures classify from stderr captured live, not recalled. Shells out for §3's argument turned up: an SSH library means adopting ~/.ssh/config, agent, ProxyJump and known_hosts maintenance, and known_hosts re-done wrong is a security regression. Injection refused at parse (leading `-`) and dead in argv (`--` before the destination, verified: ssh reads `-evil` as a hostname). `config.Connection` gains `SSH` beside `Kube`, one scheme per connection, and **every forward rule re-derives from one `Tunnelled()` predicate** — endpoint hiding, `set:`/`secrets:` shadowing, untunnellable, completion refusal — which is D119's lesson applied in advance: the second scheme is exactly the moment per-scheme twins go unwritten. Kube-only stays kube-only where the cluster is the point: `kube:`-scheme secrets (beside `ssh:` the hint names the real choice — `kv:`, or switch the tunnel — because "add `kube:`" would steer into the both-tunnels refusal), the credential editor's cluster source, segment completion. | **The teardown taught the two lessons of the phase.** exec.Cmd.Wait also waits for the copier blocked reading the caller's connection, so reaping the child is not ending the splice — teardown closes the registered conns too, or Close stalls past its bound (caught by the first honest run of the close test). And the race detector turned a plausible-looking register-then-Start into a real hole: teardown could reap a nil Process and the child would launch *behind* the kill pass — start-and-register became one critical section, so a snapshotted child is a started child. The integration test drives the exact production argv against a real sshd booted in t.TempDir (fresh keys, trust through an ssh config, banner read back through the forward) — via the sshBin seam, because OpenSSH expands ~ from passwd, not $HOME, so a test cannot relocate ~/.ssh/config; production argv stays byte-identical. TUI: the editor gains the ssh box with aliases completed from the operator's own ssh config (local read, per-keystroke legal under §8), two tunnels typed at once is a *refusal* where the endpoint keys get the *repair* — both boxes are plain text, so "empty one" is an action the widget can always perform, exactly D119's line. **And the docs sweep caught the one predicate cannot cover for**: ConnStamp — what a grant is bound to — enumerates Connection's fields by hand, so `SSH` was in the file and resolved by every call while a repointed tunnel kept a standing grant's pin matching. Fixed, both schemes labelled apart, and a reflection test now confronts the stamp with every exported Connection field: adding one fails by name until its grant-visibility is decided in the test's own ledger — a hand enumeration cannot notice its own omission, so something else has to. Seven probes post-commit, all bite; whole tree `-race -shuffle=on` green. |
| D122 | **ADR 0017's core shipped: the index states claims, rta.lock records facts, and install is where the two meet.** `internal/plugindist` + the `rta plugin install/upgrade/remove/search/index` verbs. An index is a git repo of `plugins/<name>.yaml` manifests cloned through the operator's own git (the kubectl/ssh shell-out doctrine, third application); a manifest is hostile input — strict-keyed, size-capped, prose held to textclean.Deceives, `http:` refused as "accepted from whoever is nearest", archive paths clean-relative, IDs confined to the manifest's own namespace, **and the filename is the claim** (D40, fourth application: a manifest declaring another name is refused). Install fetches (https/file, capped, hashed while writing), refuses a checksum that disagrees naming the index, **launches the binary through the real sandboxed spawn path** and refuses when its declaration is not what the index said — both directions, over exactly the fields an authorization hangs off (ID set, safety, grant). Only then: store/<name>/<digest>, bin/ symlink, trust (install IS the trust decision), lockfile — and a refused install leaves *nothing*. Signatures per §5: cosign when present, four recorded outcomes, none a gate. Upgrade diffs declarations by launching the installed binary (never the old claim), keeps the old digest + its trust for rollback; remove untrusts by digest (a name would revoke an unmanaged namesake) and names the config statements now pointing at nothing. No default index (§0's repo does not exist; hardcoding its future URL points rta at a name nobody controls yet); `oci://` parses as a claim, fetch refuses with the reason. | **The user-journey smoke caught what no package test could.** The first `rta hello greet` through the managed bin/ symlink died with EPERM while install's own verification launch of the same bytes succeeded: the store lives inside rta's data dir, tier1 denies `file-read*` there, and SBPL blocks *resolving a symlink* in a denied subtree while allowing the exec of its target — verified against sandbox-exec directly, three spellings. Fixed where it generalises: `Identify` resolves symlinks, so Identity.Path names the file the digest was read from (a Homebrew-symlinked plugin recorded a link whose target could move under the pin), pinned by a pure test and by a real spawn through a denied-tree symlink. Discovery gained the managed bin as its *last* entry — $PATH is the operator's statement, the store is rta's, so a deliberate local build shadows the managed copy the ordinary, reported way. Doctor gained the provenance rows (lock-vs-store drift, detached index, unmanaged shadow). And the full-suite run flushed a pre-existing flake: cluster-completion tests raced the production 3-second operator-patience timeout against a loaded machine — the wall-clock-vs-load pattern this ledger has recorded twice; the fixture now raises it. Heredoc scar: one test file arrived mangled through a `cat <<EOF` append and was rewritten via python — scripts beat heredocs for file content, added to the memory. Six probes post-commit, all bite; tree `-race -shuffle=on` green. |

**Still genuinely open** (fine to defer — none block M0/M1):

1. ~~**A drift test that derives path coverage from what handlers do.**~~ **Answered 2026-08-20 (D49)**, by testing the property rather than deriving it: `TestNoInputBehavesLikeAFileWithoutBeingDeclaredOne` moves a canary file under a fixed input value and fails any input whose answer changes. What is still uncovered, measured rather than assumed: capabilities that bail before the read in a test environment (`kv.init`, on its passphrase) and the three namespaces excluded for reaching off the machine (`cert`, `http`, `net`). A sweep of all twelve namespaces found no unprotected input today, so the test guards a clean tree rather than papering over a dirty one.
2. `Stream` proto details (flow control, backpressure) — settle while building `pg backup`.
3. `x/vt` PTY compositing for exec-tier TUIs — M3 spike, not a commitment.
4. Whether `http` collections deserve their own file format or live in profiles — decide when building `http`.
5. **An "AI agent management" plugin — investigated (2026-08-18) and explicitly not designed further**, the same treatment as the WASM tier (§4.2): RTA's safety-class-plus-grant model gates actions RTA's own registry executes and can dry-run/audit; a capability that hands a task to an external, RTA-uncontrolled agent session has its real blast radius happen somewhere the model structurally cannot reach, which is a different problem from "needs a bigger grant". Distinct from the M3 `ai` plugin (§6.2), which is RTA driving its own bounded agent loop over RTA's own gated capabilities — not the same idea under a different name. Revisit only if a mainstream agent harness ships a stable, documented, external session-listing/control surface (the bar `git`/`gh`/`kubectl` already clear) *and* real demand shows up from RTA's stated sysadmin/DevOps/SRE audience, not solely from this project's own dogfooding context.
